- Count: 60 judgments, 2 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Yes: ~7-word sentences, one thought per sentence, answer only. But the old prompt wording ('speech of thanks', 'as an elder would pronounce it', 'exchange') invites length. The new bank should cap length in the prompt and ask for one named part of a long genre.
- Evidence:
  - ig_bank_auth_015 [annotator_3] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Agba o, [friend] mi. Úgbò ẹ lò? (won 5 of 6 against a four-turn dialogue)` -> speaker `Ẹnẹ adugbe mi, abu wẹ le? Ugbo ẹgwo kpai ugbo ẹlo? (annotator_4 G90)`. "Well spoken"
  - ig_bank_auth_033 [annotator_6] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `[god] Chámáchálá, ánẹ́ Igálá, a d'[hand] wẹ́. [house] ẹ́yọ́ mị́ k'á kọ́ yí ... [god] k'í ch'ẹ́gwa. Í chẹ́! (eight lines)` -> speaker `[god] ki du ẹnyọ ñwu [house] titọ ki dẹ-i (annotator_6 G177), Wọla ọjilẹ [house] ẹtito (annotator_7 G178)`. "A has few perfect statement while B is perfectly correct"
  - ig_bank_auth_020 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `[child] mi, gbọ́ ọ̀la mi. Nẹ́ ẹdọ-ẹwẹ, todu kí ẹdọ-ẹwẹ chí ogun uwa. Ẹnẹ kù ma nẹ́ ẹdọ-ẹwẹ, i a rí ẹnyọ n. Tàkí tàkí a mu ẹja [water]. ...` -> speaker `[child] mi fẹdọ jẹ (annotator_7 G121), [child] mi f'ẹdo jẹ (annotator_4 G118), Ẹdejẹ che nyọ (annotator_3 G117)`. "Both outputs are wrong (tags tone_marks, invented, grammar)"
  - ig_bank_auth_030 [annotator_6] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Ám'omaye mi, n gbá wá [hand]. A k'ọpẹ nwu [god]-chamachala ... pẹlu wa efu ihiọya ám'[child] wa yí ... (three paragraphs)` -> speaker `Mẹ gba chakadu, ku mẹ wa bu wa yọ atodu udama ichọlọ [money] kpai ọya ki dẹ i (annotator_7 G169)`. "There are a few Igala in between but does'nt make sence in Igala"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Explicit cap ('one sentence', 'at most eight words') and, for long genres, one named component (the opening line; the line that thanks the two families; the line the audience answers). Each component is then comparable with a one-line gold.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Give the one opening line an elder says when he stands up to thank the guests at a wedding, at most ten words.
  - In one Igala sentence, an elder tells a young man to be patient.
  - Praise the cook after a meal in one Igala sentence of at most eight words.
  - Write only the first line of an Igala folktale as the storyteller calls it out, and the line the listeners answer.

### authenticity 13. Fixed cultural texts are invented rather than known. Three annotators gave the same traditional lullaby (mother went to the market to buy ..., something gave birth on the road); the models produced humming and [mother] akeli ñwẹ'. For the folktale opener three annotators gave Alọ mi kpa kpa kpa, annotator_6 Kp'ita kp'ita, annotator_8 a call and response (Kpì táa / Ámee); the rag arm wrote Ita mi de, and annotator_3 corrected that ita means proverb, alọ means tale.

- Rule a speaker would state: A folktale opens with the storyteller's call Alọ mi kpa kpa kpa (or Kp'ita kp'ita) and an audience response; alọ is a tale, ita a proverb. The lullaby is a fixed song beginning [child] wẹ wẹ wẹ, [mother] lọ t'[market] ...; it is recited, not composed.
- Count: 9 judgments, 1 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: No. Nothing on alọ versus ita, nothing on the storyteller's call and response, no song text. These are corpus facts to collect; the prompt should hand the model the first line and ask for the next, so knowing versus inventing is measurable.
- Evidence:
  - ig_bank_auth_029 [annotator_3] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Ita mi de. I che ita Ígáláà kpùkẹ̀. [goat] ka de.` -> speaker `Alo mi de. I che alo Ígáláà kpùkẹ̀. (E33); gold Alọ mi kpa kpa kpa (annotator_3 G161, annotator_4 G162, annotator_7 G164), Kp'ita, kp'ita (annotator_6 G163), Kpì táa / Ámee / Álọ mì kwemì gba gbà gbá! (annotator_8 G165)`. "Ita is different from alo (segment reasons: Alo is tale or story; Ita is proverbs)"
  - ig_bank_auth_032 [annotator_6] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `[child] mi, ẹ kì su ǹ. [mother] de, [mother] akeli. Lale [child] Ígáláà. [mother] akeli ñwẹ.` -> speaker `[child] we we / [mother] wẹ ñy'[market] l'akala wa / Eja d'[road] f'[child] bi / Idenekwu d'[house] anu agigbo / Kpo kpo kpo (annotator_6 G174); [child] wẹ we wẹ / mama lọti [market] na li ibọbọ wa / ibọbọ dọna fi [child] bi ... (annotator_7 G175); [child] mì dago dago dago / [mother] lọ t'[market] kíá lìbisìkìtì (annotator_8 G176)`. "They are both wrong"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Continuation prompts: give the attested first line and ask for the response or the next two lines; and one contrastive prompt on alọ versus ita.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - An Igala storyteller opens with 'Alọ mi kpa kpa kpa'. Write the words the listeners answer, then the storyteller's next line.
  - The lullaby that begins [child] wẹ wẹ wẹ, [mother] lọ t'[market] ...': write the next two lines as they are sung.
  - What is the difference between alọ and ita? Give one short Igala example of each.

### authenticity 14. Market and money talk: both arms failed the market exchange (013: 5 of 6 both_inadequate; 024 trader's call: 5 of 5). The buying verb, the money word, the price question, the bargaining idioms and the numbers were all wrong or absent: rag 'Naira ogwu kpai megwa' (kpai between number words), 'Gba [hand] lẹ', 'I ti, lọ wane'; bare 'Náyíra ọ́gwọ́mẹ́lú chẹ', 'Fú kù ñw mi', [hand] wẹ yí'. Speakers converged on a small idiom set.

- Rule a speaker would state: How much is X = [king] chi X or Agba [king] ẹ ti X. Prices in vigesimal words: ọọjẹ 50, ọgbọmẹlẹ 80, ọgwumẹlu 100, ijẹka'ka 200. Expensive = a gbuna. Last price = ejẹ ñwu dẹ? Bring it = du'wa / du mi. Trader's call = Mẹ lia bumi lẹ ñwu ku na ta i (come help me buy what I sell). Money = [money].
- Count: 16 judgments, 3 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Numbers yes (ogwu, ooje, ọgbọ mẹlẹ, ñyọ linker, never kpai between numbers) and buy = la. Not told: [king] chi X, a gbuna, ejẹ ñwu dẹ, du'wa, the trader's call. Test the numbers with specific prices; collect the idioms one line at a time.
- Evidence:
  - ig_bank_auth_013 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Ẹnẹ ki a ra eñwu: I ti, lọ wane. ... Du naira ogwu kpai megwa wa. ... Gba [hand] lẹ.` -> speaker `Ẹnẹ ki a lẹ eñwu: di [market] ñwu wane. ... Gba [money] lẹ. (E18); gold Buyer: [mother] ẹja, [king] chi ẹja / Seller: ijẹka'ka / Buyer: ijeka'ka? Ẹja wẹ a gbuna / Buyer: ẹta pamo'je ñ / Buyer: tor duwa ke'ñ (annotator_4 G77); Ẹnẹ ki ala: [king] ẹta? Ẹnẹ ki [father]: Naira mẹlu. Ẹnẹ ki ala: ejẹ ñwu dẹ? (annotator_6 G80); Agba [king] ẹ ti [yam] / [king] ku na ta chi ọgwumẹlu / Du mi naria ọọjẹ / Otitala ku na ta chi ọgbọmẹlẹ / Du'wa (annotator_7 G81)`. "Di [market] ñwu - bring the price; Gba [money] lẹ - take the money"
  - ig_bank_auth_024 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Àbo-ujà mi, ẹ wá rà ẹnyọ o! Ẹ wá gbá ẹnyọ kí nyọ. [hand] wẹ́wẹ́, ẹnyọ [hand] dẹ o!` -> speaker `Mẹ lia bumi lẹ ñwu ku na ta i (annotator_4 G132), Agba mẹ wa l'ẹñwu ku na ta-i (annotator_6 G134), Mẹ lia lẹ'ñwu gwi ugbọ mi (annotator_7 G135)`. "Both outputs are wrong (tags wrong_word, invented, grammar)"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Single market turns, each with a concrete number in the prompt so the vigesimal form is checkable, and each a single speaker's line (buyer or seller), never a whole dialogue.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Ask a fish seller how much the fish is, in one Igala line, as a buyer says it.
  - The seller answers that it is 200 naira. Write her one-line answer in Igala with the number in words.
  - Tell the seller her yam is too expensive and offer 150 naira: two short Igala lines.
  - Ask if that is the last price, in one Igala line.
  - A trader calls out to passers-by to come and buy. Write only her call, one line.

### authenticity 15. Word-level items the speakers corrected with near-unanimity but which v4.4 does not list: food/eat = jẹñwu (model jẹu, [king], úje); lives in = dodo (model fodo do, gwù); death heading = Ukwu (model Úkú); announce = ch'akoya with na as speaker (model u chokalatu); wait for me = dago, d'eju mi (model dẹ de mi); late = gbanẹ or kpọpẹ (model gbéé); strong = gbiti/ẹgbiti (model gbede, ukpahiu accepted by two); record = ch'ọda (annotator_6) or the loan recordi (annotator_7); near = a'mẹru; mother = [mother] (model Íiye, Lílè).

- Rule a speaker would state: A short lexicon for the GRAMMAR block: jẹñwu (eat, food), dodo (live at), ukwu (death), ch'akoya (announce), dago (stand, wait), d'eju (wait for, look after), gbanẹ or kpọpẹ (be late, take long), gbiti (strong), a'mẹru (near), [mother] (mother), [money] (money), [market] (market), alọ (tale), nago (sorry), iii (yes).
- Count: 30 judgments, 14 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: dodo, onobulẹ, neke, ugbo, the na speaker frame and buy = la are in the grammar rows. Not told: jẹñwu as the eat/food word, ukwu, ch'akoya, dago, d'eju, gbanẹ/kpọpẹ, gbiti, a'mẹru, [mother], alọ, nago, iii. Collect these with one forced-slot sentence each.
- Evidence:
  - ig_bank_auth_014 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `... ku ma wa jẹu.` -> speaker `... ku ma wa jẹñwu (E20); annotator_4 E19: jẹwñu; annotator_5 on 019: 'Food in Igala is [food]`. "Jẹñwu not jẹu"
  - ig_v42_auth_004 [annotator_3] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Abajọyí, i fodo do ef [goat] Port Harcourt.` -> speaker `Abajọyí, I do do ef [goat] Port Harcourt. (E45); annotator_6 E46: i dodo efewo Port Harcourt; gold ama abajọ-i i dodo efewo Port Harcourt (annotator_6 G205)`. "It wasn't properly placed"
  - ig_v42_auth_011 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Àbàkwéne Ólayi / Úkọ̀ché / Úkọlọ / Úkú` -> speaker `Úkwu (E51); annotator_7 E52: Úkwú; gold Ùchane ìlìe nwù / Ìgbé ǔkọcha nwu / Ukọlọ nwù / Ukwu nwù (annotator_8 G214)`. "I corrected the spelling"
  - ig_v42_auth_001 [annotator_6] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Sunday, dẹ de mi!` -> speaker `Sunday, dede mi! (E39); gold Sunday, agba dago d'eju mi (annotator_6 G201), Sunday agba dágo Ọmò dẹju mì ku daba wùgbo-wẹ (annotator_8 G202)`. "Output Ais nostly correct"
  - ig_bank_auth_003 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Jẹñwu nyọ́nyọ́ kí ọla wẹ nẹ ugbó.` -> speaker `Jẹñwu nyọ́nyọ́ kí ọla wẹ ki gbo (E3); annotator_6 E4: Jẹñwu nyọnyọ ki ọla wẹ gbiti; annotator_3: Jẹñwu nyọ́nyọ́ kí ọla wẹ ki gbiti; annotator_7 E5: Jẹñwu ñyo'ñyo ki ni ukpahiu`. "Ki gbo means to get mature"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: One sentence per target word, the context making the word unavoidable, no synonyms available in the prompt.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Tell a friend in one Igala sentence that your uncle now lives in Lokoja.
  - Write the four section headings of a biography on Igala Wikipedia: early life, education, work, death.
  - Shout to a friend across the road to wait for you: one line.
  - Ask a child in one Igala sentence why he came home so late.
  - Announce to a room in one Igala sentence that the meeting will start at ten o'clock.

### authenticity 16. Variety mixing is invisible in the judgments because no annotator names a dialect in an explanation; the only signal is the gold dialect field. annotator_3 declares 'other' for all 34 gold rows and writes clipped forms (Gẹ kẹ gbiti, A'bẹle, Ẹ wọlulẹ, Wọla gwu); annotator_8 declares ankpa for 18 rows then general_idah for 36, with forms nāgo, ẹñ-nì (today), omamale (good), àbò ofugbẹlì (family), ùgbade (thanks), ẹne kìa (person who), akakì, and low-tone kì throughout; annotator_4 marked one row ibaji (A chu ugbẹdẹ [god] tọdu [food] ki du ñwu wa). The models mix Yoruba-shaped, Bible and Idah forms in one answer (bare 030: pẹlu, alafia, [god]-chamachala in one speech), which matches the write-up's one-variety complaint, but the arena cannot currently measure it.

- Rule a speaker would state: One variety per answer. The default is the Idah (central) variety; a speaker's own variety is acceptable if consistent and named. Ankpa and Ibaji forms must not be mixed into an Idah sentence.
- Count: 0 judgments, 0 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: No. v4.4 has no dialect instruction; rule 8 only forbids asserting which town uses a form. The grammar row on sounds mentions the Dekina variety. The write-up names Central/Idah, Ankpa, Ibaji and Ogwugwu (r for l). Collect: ask speakers to name their variety and give both their form and the Idah form.
- Evidence:
  - ig_bank_auth_012 [annotator_8] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Agba [god]` -> speaker `Ùgbade [god] todu ìnabalì ẹnyo ka gbọò (annotator_8 G75, dialect ankpa) versus [god] agba ooo (annotator_5 G72, general_idah)`. "In Igala, the word for how an Igala speaker would naturally say 'thank God' on hearing good news is 'Ùgbade [god] todu ìnabalì ẹnyo ka gbọò'"
  - ig_bank_auth_003 [annotator_3] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `é-jẹu í gbede kẹ nẹ ukpahiu` -> speaker `Gẹ kẹ gbiti (annotator_3 G15, dialect other) versus Jẹñwu ñyọ'ñyọ kẹ ni ugbiti ọla (annotator_7 G18, general_idah)`. "Jẹñwu nyọ́nyọ́ kí ọla wẹ ki gbiti"
  - ig_bank_auth_025 [annotator_4] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `[god] Chámáchálá, à gbáwè tódù [food] yí kí ẹ dú nwù wà.` -> speaker `A chu ugbẹdẹ [god] tọdu [food] ki du ñwu wa (annotator_4 G139, dialect ibaji)`. "Both are wrong"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Pairs: 'in your own town's Igala, then in Idah Igala if different, and name your town', on high-frequency items (thank you, today, how are you, see, rat) including the Ogwugwu r/l items from the write-up.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Say 'thank you' in Igala as it is said in your own town, then as it is said in Idah if different, and name your town.
  - Say 'today' and 'tomorrow' in Igala in your own variety; mark any word that Idah speakers say differently.
  - Write 'how are you?' in the Igala of Ankpa and in the Igala of Idah.
  - Write 'I saw a rat in the kitchen' in Igala; if you know a variety that says it with r instead of l, give that too and name it.

**Shapes that produced decisive, explained judgments**

- One-line fixed speech act where at least one arm can retrieve the community formula (blessing, thanks after a meal, welcome, congratulations, reply to good news).: 100 percent decisive on 001 (7 of 7), 005 (6 of 6), 007 (6 of 6), 83 percent on 009, 010, 015, 80 percent on 023. Explanations on these carried the corrected form (annotator_3 [god] ki Dẹnyọ ñwu wẹ', annotator_5 'Wẹ in igala is used for one person') and the edits were one-word swaps (E1, E8, E31). The gold converged across annotators, so a hit or miss is a clean measurement. (ig_bank_auth_001, ig_bank_auth_005, ig_bank_auth_007, ig_bank_auth_023, ig_bank_auth_010)
- Scenario with a proper name, an institution, a number or a year the answer must carry (Zara Adejoh and NTA, Sule Ejeh born in Ankpa, a population and census year, a town founding date, biography headings).: Decisive share 67 to 100 percent (v42_002 4 of 4, v42_004, v42_014, v42_015, v42_016 3 of 4 each, v42_001 4 of 6) with no both_inadequate; produced the most precise edits in the bucket, each with a rationale (E40 to E46, E51 to E57: Ónobirẹ -> Ónobulẹ four times, fodo do -> dodo, Ef -> Efu, che -> chi, le -> lẹ, Úkú -> Úkwu). The fixed content leaves the speaker nothing to dispute but the Igala. (ig_v42_auth_002, ig_v42_auth_004, ig_v42_auth_014, ig_v42_auth_016, ig_v42_auth_001)
- Prompts whose two answers differ in one dimension only (012: identical words, tone marks only).: Isolated the variable: two annotators picked the marked form, two tied, two rejected both, and the single edit changed word order (Àgbá [god] -> [god] agba oo) rather than marks, which showed that word order mattered and marks did not. Minimal pairs like this should be designed on purpose. (ig_bank_auth_012)
- Prompts naming a plural addressee (children, guests).: Elicited the mẹ correction and the plural gold directly (E19 Am'[child] mi, mẹ wa jẹwñu; 036 rag Mẹ wọla'ule beat the singular ẹ), making agreement with the addressee measurable. (ig_bank_auth_014, ig_bank_auth_036)

**Shapes that produced ties and "both inadequate"**

- Speech act where neither arm knows the formula (welcome back, good night, take care, asking after the family, farewell, trader's call).: 24 of 29 verdicts on 002, 004, 008, 011, 028 and 5 of 5 on 024 were both_inadequate, mostly 'Both are wrong' with no form (annotator_6, annotator_7). The ranking signal is zero. The gold, however, was unanimous, so the content is right and only the shape is wrong: give the model the situation plus a request for the literal meaning, or supply the first word, so one arm can hit. (ig_bank_auth_002, ig_bank_auth_004, ig_bank_auth_008, ig_bank_auth_011, ig_bank_auth_024, ig_bank_auth_028)
- Long genre compositions: wedding speech, family prayer, market dialogue, consolation exchange, elder-and-youth meal dialogue, folktale, praise poem, lullaby.: Both arms wrote long texts; 18 of 28 verdicts where both answers exceeded 100 characters were both_inadequate, explanations were verdict-only ('Both are incorrect', 'Wrong outputs'), and the gold was long and idiosyncratic (annotator_8's seven-part wedding speech, annotator_7's praise lines) so no single rule can be read off. annotator_8 tied most of these ('Both out are correct' on 035, 039, 033). (ig_bank_auth_030, ig_bank_auth_025, ig_bank_auth_013, ig_bank_auth_034, ig_bank_auth_039, ig_bank_auth_035)
- Meta or rewrite prompts ('rewrite a stiff sentence and note what changed').: The bare arm answered with English notes and still won 2 of 4; the gold collapsed to placeholders ('V', [water] alọti ọk'). Nothing measurable about Igala came out. (ig_bank_auth_037)
- Abstract or modern topics in three or four sentences (democracy, directions to the clinic, harmattan definition, what a computer is) judged once.: Single judgment each, both_inadequate with 'Both are wrong' or empty, no edit except E48; the gold leans on loans (Demoracy, gormenti, juctioni, Ìkọputá) and is itself unsure, so neither arm nor rule is learnable from one row. (ig_v42_auth_008, ig_v42_auth_009, ig_v42_auth_012, ig_v42_auth_006)
- Prompts whose only constraint is the adverb 'naturally' or 'authentic' (27 of the 39 bank prompts).: The word gives the speaker no criterion to check and the model no slot to fill; the verdict degenerates to taste. The v42 shapes that replaced 'naturally' with a concrete scene and fact were the most decisive in the bucket. (ig_bank_auth_016, ig_bank_auth_020, ig_bank_auth_026, ig_bank_auth_006)

**What the speakers' own writing shows**

- tone marks: Six of seven annotators write gold with no tone marks (annotator_3, 4, 5, 6 and 2 at 0.00 marks per word, annotator_7 at 0.02); only annotator_8 marks (0.45 per word, mostly grave, sometimes macron ā and caron ǐ, e.g. [god] kì dẹnyo nwu mẹ', 'Àgbá ì chodudu'). 35 of 58 edits reduce the model's marks, one adds (annotator_7 E47). Dotted vowels ẹ and ọ are kept and are what annotators mean when they tag 'tone_marks' (E0 wẹ -> wa, E38 dẹ -> de 'The tone mark was not required'). Model: bare 0.72 marks per word, rag 0.36.
- contractions: Pervasive, marked with an apostrophe at the joint: 112 of 220 gold rows (annotator_6 32 of 39, annotator_4 14 of 21, annotator_5 14 of 22, annotator_8 21 of 54, annotator_7 18 of 49, annotator_3 13 of 34). Forms: d'eju tọla wẹ, ch'[morning], w'ọla ulẹ, l'ẹñwu, ñw'[food], k'ẹdọ n'[god], cha'ka, ñyọ'gba, du'wa, tor duwa ke'ñ. annotator_7 writes both vowels and the apostrophe (Dẹ'ẹju tọ'ọlawẹ, ñyọ'ñyọ, lẹ'ñwu, Chọ'[morning]); annotator_5 puts the apostrophe after the fused vowel (Kọ'la, Gbọ'la, pamo'je). Edits add contractions (chẹnyọ -> d'ẹnyọ, Gba ọla -> Gbọ'la, Àmọ́ma -> Am'[child]) and fuse place words (ef [goat] -> efewo, dẹ de -> dede). No speaker wrote an expanded form.
- ki usage: Gold counts: ki 107, kì 48 (all annotator_8), kẹ 41, ku 36, kí 9 (annotator_7 3, annotator_8 6). Unmarked ki serves the optative ([god] ki d'ẹnyọ ñwu wẹ), the prohibitive with a final nasal (ẹ ki kp'ẹdọ biẹ-n, Ẹ ki cho'kpo ñ), purpose (ki gbo), and the relative (Ẹnẹ ki ala, Ẹnẹ ki ya lẹ ñwu, ẹne kìa táa in annotator_8). ku appears before ma, mẹ and na (ku ma bi mi, ku mẹ d'iko, ku na ta i). kẹ joins verbs (lia kẹ bumi, jẹñwu kẹ gbiti, Na tene kẹ jẹñwu). 'That' after say or know is kakini / ka ki ni / kaki (U che ma kaki ya wọ; na tẹnẹ ku ka ñwu mẹ ka ki ni). After tọdu speakers write ku or ki or kẹ, never kí (todu ku mẹ d'iko, to du kẹ ñu kpayu, todu ki ch'ọda). The bare model's high-tone kí on every optative was never flagged as such; annotators simply stripped the mark.
- dialect: 166 rows general_idah, 34 'other' (every row of annotator_3), 18 ankpa (annotator_8's August rows; later rows of the same annotator declared general_idah), 1 ibaji (annotator_4 G139 'A chu ugbẹdẹ [god] tọdu [food] ki du ñwu wa'), 1 unset. annotator_8's declared Ankpa forms: nāgo, ẹñ-nì (today), omamale (good, better), àbò ofugbẹlì (family), ùgbade (thanks), ẹne kìa (person who), akakì, ownu, and low-tone kì. annotator_3's 'other' forms: Gẹ kẹ gbiti, A'bẹle, Ẹ wọlulẹ, Wọla gwu, Akwọra [house] wẹ a, Ẹdejẹ che nyọ. Lexical spelling variants across annotators: Ch'[morning] (3, 6) vs ch'[morning] (4, 7); [house] vs [house]; ugbo vs ugbọ; ẹnyọ vs ẹñyọ vs enyọ; nyọ vs ñyọ; Agba vs Àgbá; [mother] vs [mother] vs [mother].
- other: Vocative lengthening with o/oo/ooo (Wọla ulẹ ooo, [god] agba ooo, Agba oo, Ch'[morning] ke-oo). Amen as Amin, Amini, Ámee. Yes = iii (annotator_6 twice). Sorry = nago. Please = Agba before a request (Agba [king] ẹ ti [yam], agba dago d'eju mi, Agba mẹ wa l'ẹñwu). Kin vocatives open most utterances: [child] mi, Onuku mi / [friend] mi, Ọmaye mi, [father], Mama, [mother], Aju mi (grandchild). Negation spelled -n (11), 'ñ/’ñ (12), no (9), ñ (7), n (6), leñ (3), nee (1). Dialogue gold uses English role labels (Buyer:, Seller:, Imoto:, Ogijo:). annotator_8 answers both_inadequate with a template 'In Igala the word for ... is ...' (18 of 44 judgments), which is gold in disguise; annotator_6 and annotator_7 give verdict-only explanations (34 and 30 of their judgments) and should be asked for the form; annotator_3 and annotator_5 give the corrected form or a word-level 'X wrong, Y right'.
- contractions note: Salem's expanded-form request and the speakers' practice point in opposite directions; the bank should collect both from the same speaker.

**Open questions (speakers disagree)**

- Strong: gbiti/ẹgbiti (annotator_3 'ki gbiti', annotator_6 E4 'gbiti', annotator_7 G18 'ugbiti ọla') versus 'ki gbo' = mature (annotator_5 E3 and #15 'Nẹ gbo is wrong, Ki gbo is correct') versus ukpahiu (annotator_7 E5 'ki ni ukpahiu', annotator_8 G19 'kì kpìtì') versus annotator_4 E2 'ugbọ kpaki igbiti'. Four forms for one prompt (003).
- Why-question: annotator_3 bans tọdu with chi ('We can't use todu and chi together', E32) and writes Ẹñwu chi, as do annotator_4 (G150) and annotator_6 (G151); annotator_7's gold uses [child] mi tọdu ẹñwu kẹ ma wa gwẹ ñyi ni'ñ' (G153, G154). The v4.4 wh-row sides with annotator_3.
- Give good versus do good in blessings: annotator_3, annotator_6 correct ch'ẹnyọ to d'ẹnyọ (E1, E8, #1, #25); annotator_4 keeps ch'ẹñyo (E7) and annotator_2 writes [god] ki dẹ ẹñwu wẹ'.
- Blessing to the community: wẹ accepted by six annotators on 001; annotator_5 insists on wa ('wa is more than one person', E0).
- Thank God word order: [god] agba oo (annotator_4 E16, annotator_5 G72, annotator_7 G74) versus Agba [god] (annotator_6 E17; annotator_3 called 'Àgbá [god] correct) versus [god] ki n'ugwa (annotator_6 G73, annotator_7 G62) versus [god] chegbẹ (annotator_3 G58, G71) versus Ùgbade [god] (annotator_8, ankpa).
- Folktale opener: Alọ mi kpa kpa kpa (annotator_3, 4, 7) versus Kp'ita, kp'ita (annotator_6) versus a call and response Kpì táa / Ámee (annotator_8); annotator_3 says ita means proverb, so annotator_6's Kp'ita needs checking.
- Alafia shapes: v4.4 bans every alaafia shape as Yoruba, yet gold uses them freely: ch'ọlafiya ba? and uch'ọlafia (annotator_6 G67, G92), kpọ'lafìa and ọla fiya (annotator_8 G13, G160), gbọ lafia (annotator_5 G9), and the grammar farewell row itself gives 'Ẹ lọ kpaì ọla fiya' as community verified. The rag arm's 'lo kpai'ọlafia' (028) was called 'slightly correct' by annotator_4 and inadequate by three others. Is ọla fiya (two words) Igala while ọlafia (one word) is not, or is the ban simply wrong for this community?
- Tone marks: the write-up asks for marks always; six of seven annotators never write them and 35 edits strip them; v4.4 says both 'no tone marks unless asked' and 'mark tone as the dictionary does'. Which standard is the arena scoring?
- Contraction: the write-up asks for expanded forms; every speaker contracts with an apostrophe and edits add contractions. No speaker produced an expanded form.
- Good night: the v4.4 greetings row lists night among the wọla slot nouns, but all seven speakers gave ch'[morning] (till morning), never wọla + night.
- Take care: D'eju tọla wẹ from five annotators, but annotator_5 also accepts 'Gbọ'la wẹ nyọnyọ' (E11) and annotator_4 first glossed 'Nẹju kp'ọla wẹ' as 'May God be with you' (#44) before correcting it to d'eju t'ọla (E10).
- Describing versus speaking: annotator_3 and annotator_5 accepted and corrected narrated answers (014 [mother] Ígáláà a di àmọ́ma ñwù ku ma wa jẹñwu', 017 [mother] kakini'); annotator_8's own gold on v42_010 narrates ('Onobule kì défu [goat] ... ì mìí ọdọ ku ma bì-ownu n'). Rule 7 is not shared by all speakers.
- Nnọ tolerance: annotator_4 and annotator_7 picked the rag answer that opens with Nnọ on 018 ('a better option but has few correction on it'); annotator_5, annotator_4 (in the edit) and annotator_3 removed it. Same annotator_4 accepted it in the judgment and deleted it in the edit.
- Record a song: ch'ọda (annotator_6 E42, G203) versus the loan recordi (annotator_7 E43) versus kó accepted unchanged by annotator_3 and annotator_5 (E40, E41). Loan policy for new verbs is unsettled.
- Wait for me: de de mi (annotator_3 E38) versus dede mi (annotator_6 E39) versus agba dago d'eju mi (annotator_6 G201) versus dágo ... dẹju mì (annotator_8 G202); is de/dede a real verb or a repaired model form?
- Numbers: annotator_8 glossed 'ogbometa' as 120 (G82) where the vigesimal rule gives 60; annotator_7 glossed ọgbọmẹlẹ as 80 and ọgwumẹlu as 100, consistent with the rule.
- Label-explanation mismatches to check with the annotator: annotator_7 #172 picked B (the long bare blessing) but wrote 'Output A is very correct'; annotator_7 #184 chose both_inadequate while translating the rag answer as correct English.
- Ígáláà: the rag arm's long-vowel spelling was removed or shortened to Igala by annotator_4, 6, 7 (E12, E13, E43, E44, E46, E48, E56) but kept by annotator_3 (E33, E34, E45) and annotator_5 (E23, E41).

## Grammar and tone (130 judgments, 67 edits, 244 gold)

Data read: Read in full with the Read/Bash tools: review-split/comparisons-grammar_tone.json (130 rows), review-split/edits-grammar_tone.json (67 rows incl. segments), review-split/gold-grammar_tone.json (244 rows), review-split/prompts-grammar_tone.json (102 prompts), review-split/prompt-informativeness.json (44 grammar_tone rows), review-split/v44-vs-gold.json (66 grammar_tone rows: the v4.4 served output next to gold), prompt-v4-4.txt (served system prompt), grammar-rows-oct8.json (30 rule rows, topics and rule text), writeup-oct8.txt (Salem and Lydia). Paths under /private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-halim-Documents-wikitongues--claude-worktrees-fervent-jemison-b6bb05/4e7b27ad-9966-41d9-a2d6-dec139f0636b/scratchpad/.

### grammar_tone 1. Locative before a town or inside a market: the model clips 'in' to a bare consonant (ef Idah), uses efu before a town, or elides before the market word; speakers want efẹwọ/efewo before a town, efu before a state or year, and most write efu [market] or ef'[market] unelided but never ef' before a consonant.

- Rule a speaker would state: 'in' = efu; fused efẹwọ (efewo) before a town name; efu [market] efewo Idah for 'at Idah market'; ef' only before a vowel and even then speakers split between ef'[market] and efu [market].
- Count: 5 judgments, 7 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Yes: CLOSED-CLASS GRAMMAR says 'in' is efu, ef' only before a vowel, efẹwọ before a town; grammar row 2 says the same. v4.4 output on gram_001 (Musa dodo efẹwọ Idah) matches annotator_6's gold, and gram_004 gives ef'[market] Idah. So the next prompts must test whether telling worked on towns and places not in the rows, and whether 'at X market / X school / X river' takes efu + place noun + efẹwọ + town.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_gram_001 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Musa dodo ef Idah` -> speaker `Musa dodo efẹwọ Idah`. "I corrected the spelling"
  - ig_v42_gram_001 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Musa dodo ef Idah` -> speaker `Musa chi efu [goat] idah i gwu (gold)`. "IN in igala is efu"
  - ig_v42_gram_003 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `ma bí ma méjì lẹ efu Ankpa` -> speaker `ma bí ma méjì lẹ efẹwọ Ankpa`. "I corrected a word"
  - ig_v42_gram_004 [annotator_6] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `a t'[yam] ef'ádṣa Idah` -> speaker `at'[yam] efu [market] Idah`. "Output is almost perfect"
  - ig_v42_gram_005 [annotator_4] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `chukolo efu Abuja` -> speaker `chukolo efẹwọ Abuja`. "(segment efu -> efẹwọ, no reason given)"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Short translation (6 to 10 English words) with a named person and a named place, one locative per sentence, varying the place type (town, state, year, market, school, river, compound) so that a wrong choice is a single visible word. This shape gave decisive judgments (gram_001 3/4, gram_002 4/4) and single-word edits.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Translate 'Ojochide lives in Ankpa' into Igala.
  - Translate 'Her mother sells fish at Ayangba market' into Igala.
  - Translate 'He was born in Lokoja in 1988' into Igala.
  - Translate 'The children are at the school in Dekina' into Igala.
  - Translate 'Musa works in Kogi State but sleeps in Idah' into Igala.

### grammar_tone 2. Motion verb without ti before the destination, and an invented verb for 'leave for': the bare arm writes lọ Lokoja, lọ [house], lọ [money]; the retrieval arm wrote chẹ àwéhì tì Lokoja. Five different annotators rewrote the verb phrase to lo ti / loti / le ti.

- Rule a speaker would state: Destination = motion verb + ti (+ town): lo ti Lokoja, lo t'[house], lo t'[money]; 'has already left for' = chẹ loti X (mẹ) in Idah speech; no verb 'àwéhì' exists.
- Count: 12 judgments, 7 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Yes: 'A destination follows a motion verb with ti: lo ti Idah, lo t'Ankpa' and row 9. v4.4 output on gram_006 is 'Amọnẹ Ajaokuta ka kakini Ibrahim chẹ loti Lokoja' (ti present). Test whether it holds with verbs other than lo (come, return, run, send, carry to) and with pronoun or compound destinations.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_gram_006 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Ibrahim chẹ àwéhì tì Lokoja mẹ` -> speaker `Ibrahim chẹ lotì Lokoja mẹ`. "Loti - gone to"
  - ig_v42_gram_006 [annotator_6] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Am'Ajaokuta kà kíni Ibrahim lọ Lokoja kpa.` -> speaker `Abo Ajaokuta kakini Ibrahim lo ti Lokoja.`. "The output was not well constructed"
  - ig_v42_gram_006 [annotator_3] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Ibrahim lọ Lokoja kpa` -> speaker `Ibrahim chẹ le tì Lokoja mẹ (edit on the rag arm)`. "Word order"
  - ig_v42_gram_043 [annotator_3] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Amí [child] lọ [money] kpaí amí [mother] ma` -> speaker `Ami imoto lo t'[money] kpai ami [mother] ma (the rag answer a3 picked)`. "(tagged grammar on the bare arm, no text)"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: One-sentence translations where the destination is a common noun (farm, house, river, school) rather than a town, and where the motion verb is wa/dabi/kpe/du, so that the model has to generalise ti beyond the attested lo ti Idah.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Translate 'My sister has gone to the river to fetch water' into Igala.
  - Translate 'The boy ran to his father's compound' into Igala.
  - Translate 'They sent the goat to the market in Ejule' into Igala.
  - Translate 'We came back from the farm when the sun set' into Igala.
  - Translate 'Take this letter to the teacher's house' into Igala.

### grammar_tone 3. Completed and 'already finished' actions: the model gives a bare verb or the Yoruba-shaped 'kpa' alone; speakers build 'has finished V-ing' with the object fronted by fi/fu and closed by kpa mẹ (jẹ kpa mẹ), 'has already' with chẹ ... mẹ, and they reject the model's 'abeku ẹ kpa ma' for 'whether she has finished'. 'All (gone)' kpa at the end of gram_041 was removed by one annotator and replaced by chakadu by another.

- Rule a speaker would state: Completive frame: (subject) fi/fu + object + V kpa mẹ ('I fi [food] jẹ kpa mẹ' = he has finished eating); 'already' = chẹ + V ... mẹ; 'whether she has finished' = í chẹñwu kẹ che kpa mẹ. The closing mẹ/ma is part of the frame.
- Count: 9 judgments, 5 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: No. The prompt only says 'bare verb = completed; preverbal á = not yet complete'. Neither the fi ... V kpa mẹ frame nor chẹ ... mẹ nor the closing mẹ is in the prompt or the 30 rows, and v4.4's gram_024 output (I chẹ jẹñwu kpa) is not what any of five speakers wrote. The next prompts must collect the frame.
- Evidence:
  - ig_bank_gram_024 [annotator_5] (gold): model `I chẹ jẹñwu kpa. (v4.4 served output)` -> speaker `i fi [food] jẹkpa mẹ (a5); E fu [food] jẹ kpa mẹ (a7); Ì fùjẹñwù jẹ kpà mẹ (a3); I jẹ kpa ma (a6)`. "He has finish eating"
  - ig_v42_gram_031 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `i natene abeku ẹ kpa ma` -> speaker `i natene í chẹñwu kẹ che kpa mẹ`. "í chẹñwu kẹ che kpa mẹ not abeku ẹ kpa ma."
  - ig_v42_gram_031 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `abeku ẹ kpa ma` -> speaker `chẹ'ñwu kẹ chẹ kpa ma`. "Added some words to complete the sentense"
  - ig_v42_gram_041 [annotator_3] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `lẹ lo t'[house] kpa` -> speaker `lẹ le t'[house]`. "Wrong tone marks"
  - ig_v42_gram_041 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `lo t'[house] kpa` -> speaker `t'[house] chakadu`. "I corrected a spelling error and a word"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Minimal triplets in one prompt: 'he is eating / he has eaten / he has finished eating' (and 'not yet'), forcing the speaker to show the completive morphology side by side; plus narrative pairs 'X had already V-ed when Y'. Three-way prompts produced edit-rich answers (gram_030) where single sentences produced ties.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Write three Igala sentences: 'She is washing the clothes', 'She has washed the clothes', 'She has finished washing the clothes'. Keep the same verb.
  - Translate 'The farmers have already harvested all the yams' into Igala.
  - Your mother asks whether you have finished sweeping. Write her question and your reply if you have finished, and your reply if you have not yet finished.
  - Translate 'By the time we reached the market, the traders had all gone home' into Igala.
  - Translate 'He had already eaten before his father came back' into Igala.

### grammar_tone 4. Future and irrealis: speakers' gold uses na as a preverbal future/intention marker with any subject (a na lo = we will go, Mama na li = Mama will buy, ẹnẹ ... a na mi = whoever ... will rest, Na alọ = I am going / will go), and ma after the subject for 'would have' in counterfactuals (a ma dẹdẹ = we would have waited). The model (and v4.4) only knows á for incomplete and uses na as a first-person pronoun in fixed announce frames.

- Rule a speaker would state: Future / intention = subject clitic + na + verb (1sg: na + verb); 'would have' = subject + ma + verb; the simple present-progressive is subject + á/a + verb. These are three distinct preverbal particles, not one.
- Count: 0 judgments, 0 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Partly and in the wrong direction: row 1 restricts na to 'I want to tell you / I say to you / I will come' and says 'Outside these frames I stays u'; the prompt says preverbal á covers both is-doing and will-do. Sixteen gold rows in this bucket (gram_016 x2, gram_033 x4, gram_036 x3, gram_037 x2, gram_031 x5) show na and ma as general preverbal markers. The next prompts must collect the paradigm, not test the rule.
- Evidence:
  - ig_bank_gram_016 [annotator_5] (gold): model `Ichẹñwu [water] a lọ, a lo t'[money] ñ. (v4.4 served output)` -> speaker `i jẹñwu Komi lọ, a na loti okoñ`. "If it rains, we will not go to the farm"
  - ig_bank_gram_016 [annotator_4] (gold): model `a lo t'[money] ñ` -> speaker `Chẹ'ñwu kọ'mi lọ a na lọtọ [money] ñ`. "(gold, no gloss)"
  - ig_bank_gram_036 [annotator_7] (gold): model `U lo t'[market] k'u l'[yam]. (v4.4)` -> speaker `Mama na li ami ẹñwu ọbọ gwa [market]`. "(gold market-day story: Mama na li = Mama will buy)"
  - ig_bank_gram_033 [annotator_8] (gold): model `I á lo ti Idah ọla. (v4.4 chose third person)` -> speaker `Past=I went "ùlọ", present=lam going "Nà álọ", Future=I will go "Ná á lọ"`. "(gold with inline gloss)"
  - ig_bank_gram_031 [annotator_7] (gold): model `a ma nẹdẹ (v4.4)` -> speaker `Chẹ ñwu ka ma ka ki ni ọgijọ a wa a ma dẹdẹ`. "(gold; four annotators wrote a ma dẹdẹ/dede, a6 wrote a ma dago)"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Same-verb tense ladders (past / now / tomorrow / would have) in one prompt with the subject varied (I, we, they, Mama) so the particle is isolated against a constant verb; gram_033 produced glossed paradigms from four annotators. Avoid asking for 'the future form' in the abstract.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Write four short Igala sentences with the same verb: 'I cooked yesterday', 'I am cooking now', 'I will cook tomorrow', 'I would have cooked if you had come'.
  - Translate 'We will go to Idah next week, but they will stay at home' into Igala.
  - Translate 'Mama will buy fish tomorrow and the children will eat it in the evening' into Igala.
  - Translate 'If the rain had stopped, we would have gone to the farm' into Igala.
  - Your friend asks what you will do after the harvest. Answer in two Igala sentences, one about this week and one about next year.

### grammar_tone 5. Negation: in 'why are you not V-ing' questions three annotators insert ma before the verb and still close with the nasal (wẹ ma ch'íya ... n), which the prompt forbids; the plain negative is written six different ways by seven speakers (ǹ, no, 'ñ, -n, nọ, nóò); the bare arm used Yoruba má for prohibition and both annotators rejected it; v4.4 adds an idle á in 'Ma á j'ẹñwu ñ' that no speaker wrote.

- Rule a speaker would state: Statement negative = clause + final nasal (ñ/n, spelling unsettled); prohibition = subject + kì + verb + nasal (annotator_3 drops kì); negative inside a 'why' or 'that' clause = subject + ma + verb ... + nasal. Never a preverbal á in a negative.
- Count: 9 judgments, 3 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Told for the final nasal and the kì prohibition; told the OPPOSITE for the preverbal ma ('Negation: ONLY a clause-final nasal'). The ma ... n frame in embedded negatives (a5, a7, a8 independently on gram_029) is not in the prompt or rows. Prompts must collect embedded negation and test the prohibition.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_gram_029 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Éñwú chẹ kí wẹ a ch'ẹyẹ kpaí ma n?` -> speaker `Éñwú chí kú wẹ ma ch'íya kpa ma n?`. "Play in igala is íya not ch'ẹyẹ"
  - ig_v42_gram_029 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `todu eñwu ki wẹ a chira kpai ma n?` -> speaker `todu eñwu ki wẹ ma chiya kpai ama ñ?`. "Adjusted the spelling"
  - ig_v42_gram_029 [annotator_8] (gold): model `Ẹñwu ki che ki wẹ a j'ẹfẹ kpai ma ñ? (v4.4)` -> speaker `[child] kẹkẹ en̄wu a'chẹ ku wà ma chìya kpì aḿo'nuku wá n?`. "Child what is wrong that you're not playing with your friend?"
  - ig_v42_gram_039 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `ẹ́ má kọ́ [money] efí anẹ lẹ ọdọ yí` -> speaker `mẹ kì ch'[money] ojane lẹ ǹ (rag answer picked by a3 and a7)`. "(tagged grammar on the bare arm)"
  - ig_bank_gram_019 [annotator_7] (gold): model `Ma á j'ẹñwu ñ. (v4.4)` -> speaker `Ma jẹnwu nọ (a7); Mà jẹnwu nóò (a8); Ma jẹñwu-n (a6); Mà jẹñwu ǹ (a3)`. "They are not eating"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Question-plus-two-answers prompts and 'why not' embedded negatives, where the speaker must write the negative inside a subordinate clause; and command pairs (do / do not) so kì and the nasal appear side by side. gram_030 (three-part) and gram_029 produced the richest edits.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - A girl is not greeting her uncle. In one Igala sentence, ask her why she is not greeting him.
  - Write the Igala for 'Eat the yam' and then for 'Do not eat the yam', as a mother would say both to a child.
  - Translate 'He said that he did not see the goat' into Igala.
  - Translate 'The people who did not come to the meeting will not hear the news' into Igala.
  - A boy says he has not washed the plates. Write his sentence, then his mother's reply telling him not to go out until he has washed them.

### grammar_tone 6. ki as the general clause opener: every one of seven speakers puts ki/k' after chẹñwu in 'if it rains' (chẹñwu k'[water] lọ), which v4.4 drops (Ichẹñwu [water] a lọ); speakers contract ki + u (I) to ku (ẹnwu ku ka = what I said) and annotator_5 writes kú/kẹ before wẹ/ẹ, so ku is not 'before plural ma' only; and no speaker closes a relative clause with lẹ on gram_022 or gram_030 (14 gold rows), while the prompt requires head + kì ... lẹ.

- Rule a speaker would state: if = (i)chẹñwu ki + clause; ki + u -> ku, ki + ẹ -> kẹ; relative clause = head + ki + clause with no closing lẹ in most speakers' writing; ki is unmarked for tone in 90 percent of gold.
- Count: 2 judgments, 4 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Told partly and partly wrong: prompt has 'ichẹñwu = if' without ki, 'Relativizer kì (singular), ku before plural ma/me', and 'lẹ also closes relative clauses'. The gold contradicts the ku rule (ku = ki + u) and does not support the lẹ closure. Salem's kí (COMP) vs kì (REL) tone contrast is invisible in speaker gold (ki unmarked). Prompts must collect ki + pronoun contractions and relative clauses with varied heads.
- Evidence:
  - ig_bank_gram_016 [annotator_6] (gold): model `Ichẹñwu [water] a lọ, a lo t'[money] ñ. (v4.4)` -> speaker `Chẹñwu k'[water] lọ, alo t'[money]-n`. "(gold; a3 kì [water], a4 kọ'mi, a7 kọ'mi, a8 k'[water], a5 Komi)"
  - ig_v42_gram_028 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Wẹ gbo ẹnwù kí n ka n?` -> speaker `Wẹ gbo ẹnwù ku ka ba?`. "I did spelling correction"
  - ig_v42_gram_029 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `chẹ kí wẹ` -> speaker `chí kú wẹ`. "(segment tagged tone_marks)"
  - ig_v42_gram_032 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `k'ẹ chè egba k'ẹ ch'ónokẹ́rẹ` -> speaker `kẹ chè egba kẹ chokolobia`. "I corrected the words and spelling"
  - ig_bank_gram_030 [annotator_7] (gold): model `Onokẹlẹ kì a lo t'[money] ñwu lẹ leku. (v4.4, with lẹ closure)` -> speaker `Ọnẹkẹlẹ ki a lọti [money] ñwu lẹ'gwu`. "(gold; a6 Ẹnẹkẹlẹ ka lo t'[money] ñwu lekwu, a3 [father] kà lò tókó ñwù lèkwù: ka = ki + a 'we')"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Translations with a relative clause whose subject is a pronoun (I, you, we, they), so the contraction ki+u/ẹ/a/ma surfaces as one word; conditionals with varied subjects; and a few 'expanded form' prompts asking the speaker to write ki and the pronoun as two words. Avoid abstract 'use the relativizer' instructions.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Translate 'The yam that I bought yesterday is rotten' into Igala.
  - Translate 'The woman who sold you the cloth is my aunt' into Igala.
  - Translate 'If you see my brother, tell him to come home' into Igala.
  - Translate 'The goats that we tied under the tree have run away' into Igala.
  - Write 'what I said' and 'what you said' in Igala, first as you would normally write them, then with every word written out in full and separated.

### grammar_tone 7. Reported speech 'X said that': the model stacks ka + kakini (say + say-that); annotator_5 deletes ka, annotator_3 reduces to ka kini, annotator_6 rewrites the bare arm's kà kíni to kakini; v4.4 still outputs 'Amọnẹ Ajaokuta ka kakini'. Gold shows four spellings of the same linker (kakini, ka kini, ka ki ni, kaki) and 'told the children' as ka ñwu / ka ñwi + object + kakini.

- Rule a speaker would state: 'said that' = kakini (one unit, ka + ki + ni), never ka kakini; 'told Y that' = ka ñwu Y kakini ...; 'heard that' uses the same ki ni.
- Count: 6 judgments, 5 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Row 30 lists 'said that = kakini' and the prompt table gives nothing on reported speech; the ka + kakini doubling is not addressed and v4.4 still produces it. Test whether a direct instruction is followed, and collect 'told / asked / heard that'.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_gram_006 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Àmònẹ Ajaokuta ka kakini Ibrahim` -> speaker `Àmònẹ Ajaokuta kakini Ibrahim`. "Say that in igala is kakini"
  - ig_v42_gram_006 [annotator_3] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `ka kakini` -> speaker `ka kini`. "Some words were wrong"
  - ig_v42_gram_006 [annotator_6] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Am'Ajaokuta kà kíni Ibrahim` -> speaker `Abo Ajaokuta kakini Ibrahim`. "The output was not well constructed"
  - ig_bank_gram_032 [annotator_6] (gold): model `Ma ka ñwu ami imọtọ kakini ma chẹ du ọdun lẹ (v4.4)` -> speaker `Ma ka ñwu abimọtọ lẹ kakini ma m'[god] icholo lẹ du tọgba`. "(gold; a4 and a7: Ma chẹ ka ñwi ami imọtọ ka ki ni ...)"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Short reported-speech translations with the matrix verb varied (said, told her, asked whether, heard, announced) and the embedded subject different from the matrix subject, so that pronoun switching (i vs u) is also tested. gram_006 with 6 judgments and 5 edits was the single most informative prompt in the bucket.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Translate 'My father said that the rain will come tomorrow' into Igala.
  - Translate 'The chief told the young men that the meeting has been moved to Sunday' into Igala.
  - Translate 'I heard that Onoja has gone to Lagos' into Igala.
  - Translate 'She asked me whether the children had eaten' into Igala.
  - Translate 'They announced that nobody should fish in the river this month' into Igala.

### grammar_tone 8. Numbers, ages, prices and dates: the model (bare arm) composes 70 as twenty-three-plus-ten (ọ́gwu-mẹ́ta nyo mẹ́gwa), 85 as ogwẹlẹ kpai ẹlu, prefixes mẹ- onto 18 and 19 when counting (Megwejo), spells out 1965 and 2018 as compounds, and misorders the date. Speakers fix each with a specific numeral: ẹ̀tẹẹ̀gwa 70, ọgbọmẹlẹ ñyọ mẹlu 85, ẹgwẹjọ 18, ọchu ẹkẹla nọlu mi mẹgwẹlẹ ọdọ 2018, digits for years. But they disagree on 70 (ẹtẹẹgwa vs ọ́gbọmẹta nyọ mẹgwā), 100 (ọ́gwú-mẹ́lù vs ọgbọ mẹlu) and 1000 (akpulu vs the prompt's icham nyogwoko).

- Rule a speaker would state: Base-20 with ñyọ as the only additive linker; blended forms ẹtẹẹgwa 70, ọgbọmẹlẹ 80; counting aloud uses bare ẹgwẹjọ/ẹgwẹla; years in digits after ọdọ; date = ọchu + ordinal month + nọlu mi + ([god]) mẹ-day + ọdọ + digits; age = X chi ọdọ mẹ-N; price = naira + numeral with akpulu for thousand (a5).
- Count: 23 judgments, 11 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Yes, in detail (rows 7, 14, 27 and the Dates and Numerals lines). v4.4 outputs on gram_014, 020, 021, 023 match the speaker forms; v4.4 gram_019 writes 'icham nyogwoko nyoke ọgbọ mẹ́lu ugbo mẹ́lu' for 1500, which no annotator wrote (a5: akpulu mébie ny'oje). Next prompts must test numbers NOT in the rows (35, 47, 60, 90, 120, 300, 2500) and let three or more annotators settle 70, 100 and 1000.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_gram_018 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `ọdọ ñwu chí ọ́gwu-mẹ́ta nyo mẹ́gwa` -> speaker `Íyeyemi chí ọdọ'mẹ ẹ̀tẹẹ̀gwa (edit on rag)`. "70 in igala is ẹ́tẹẹ̀gwa"
  - ig_v42_gram_018 [annotator_8] (gold): model `etegwa (rag) / ẹ̀tẹẹ̀gwa (a5)` -> speaker `[mother]-ye mì chì ọdọ ọ́gbọmẹta nyọ mẹgwā`. "My mother -mother is seventy years old."
  - ig_v42_gram_020 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Amonẹ ogwẹlẹ kpai ẹlu lewa` -> speaker `Amonẹ ọgbọmẹlẹ ñyọ mẹlú wa`. "85 in igala is ọgbọmẹlẹ ñyọ mẹlú  Not ogwẹlẹ kpai ẹlu"
  - ig_v42_gram_021 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Megwejo, megwela, ogwu` -> speaker `Egwejo, Egwela, ogwu`. "i just fixed the correction"
  - ig_v42_gram_014 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `[god] ẹ́gwẹ̀fà, ochù Sẹptẹ́mba, ọdọ́ ọ̀kẹ́gwá ñyọ ẹ́gwẹ̀jọ` -> speaker `[god] ochu ẹkẹ'la nolu mi ẹgwẹlẹ efu ọdọ 2018`. "14th is ẹgwẹlẹ not ẹgwẹfa"
  - ig_v42_gram_019 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `[money] wñ chi Naira idọsi onka kpai ọgbọ mẹ́lu ẹ́lu` -> speaker `[market] ñwu chí naira Akpulu mébie ny'oje (gold)`. "[money] not [money]"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Spoken-line numeric prompts (age, headcount, price, date, counting aloud) with the number stated in the English; these produced decisive judgments and exact-form edits (gram_018 2/3, 020 2/3, 021 2/3) while open-ended 'say the year' prompts tied. Ask for the number written in words so digits cannot hide it.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Your uncle is sixty years old. Say his age in one Igala sentence, writing the number in words.
  - Count aloud in Igala from thirty-eight to forty-two, one after another.
  - A trader asks how many yams you harvested. It was one hundred and twenty. Answer in one Igala sentence with the number in words.
  - A buyer asks the price of a goat. It is twenty-five thousand naira. Say it the way you would at Anyigba market, number in words.
  - Your child was born on 3 March 2015. Say her date of birth aloud as one Igala line, with the day and month in words.

### grammar_tone 9. The tone_marks tag does not mean diacritics: of 40 segment edits tagged tone_marks, most change letters, words or dotted vowels ([father] -> [father]; akọchẹ -> ẹnẹ ukọche; ki -> ku; efewo lẹ -> efu [goat] yí; Abobule -> Abobulẹ 'Wrong tone marks'); only annotator_5 on gram_021 (ogwu -> ógwú) and gram_018 added accents. The heavily accented bare arm drew 16 tone_marks tags, mostly for wrong words under the accents. Speakers are asking for the right LETTERS, and are not policing accents; four of six annotators write gold with no accents at all.

- Rule a speaker would state: Dotted vowels (ẹ, ọ) and the nasal letters are obligatory and corrected; acute/grave accents are optional in community writing and uncorrected; annotator_3 and annotator_8 add them inconsistently; a wrong word with correct accents is still 'tone_marks' to speakers.
- Count: 29 judgments, 40 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: The prompt says both 'no tone marks unless the question asks' (REGISTER) and 'Mark tone as the dictionary and examples do' (ORTHOGRAPHY), and Salem's write-up asks for marks always. The speakers' behaviour supports neither strongly. Prompts must separate the two questions: can the model mark tone correctly when asked, and do speakers correct marks when a minimal pair depends on them.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_gram_004 [annotator_6] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `[father] Amina` -> speaker `[father] Amina`. "(segment tagged tone_marks: accent removed, consonant doubled)"
  - ig_v42_gram_041 [annotator_3] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Abobule ... lo t'[house] kpa` -> speaker `Abobulẹ ... le t'[house]`. "Wrong tone marks"
  - ig_v42_gram_021 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `ogwu ñyọ ka` -> speaker `ógwú ñyọ ka`. "I corrected the tone mark and spelling"
  - ig_v42_gram_002 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `akọchẹ` -> speaker `ẹnẹ ukọche`. "Ẹnẹ ukọchẹ meaning someone that teaches (tagged tone_marks)"
  - ig_v42_gram_021 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Ẹ̀gwẹ́fẹ̀jọ, ẹ̀gwẹ́fẹ̀la` -> speaker `Egwejo, Egwela`. "(bare arm tagged tone_marks and wrong_word: the word is wrong, not the accents)"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Two shapes: (a) explicit 'write with full tone marks' prompts on short sentences built around attested minimal pairs (ka/kwa, u/ú, ki/kì), then ask speakers to mark only the accents they would change; (b) ordinary prompts with the instruction to annotators to tag tone_marks only for accents (a UI note, not a prompt). Avoid long accented sentences: they get tagged for lexicon.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Write 'The child is sleeping' in Igala with every tone mark written, then once more the way you would text it to a friend.
  - Write the Igala words for 'say' and 'shout' with tone marks, each in a short sentence that shows the difference.
  - Translate 'I went to the farm' and 'He went to the farm' into Igala with tone marks on every vowel.
  - Write 'the food is sweet' in Igala with full tone marks, then say which marks could be left out without changing the meaning.

### grammar_tone 10. Copula choice and the agreeing tag: speakers change che/chẹ to chi in equatives (che odo eta -> chi ọdọ mẹta; chẹ odo etegwa -> chí ọdọ'mẹ) and close predicate nominals with a number-agreeing tag (Zainab chi ẹnẹ ukuko i che; Ami ọnọbulẹ ... chi ọmayẹ ma chẹ), while chẹ appears as the completive auxiliary (chẹ loti, chẹ bie mẹ). The model treats chi/chẹ/che as free variants and v4.4's gram_027 'Abobule mẹji lẹ ch'ami omaye' has no tag where four speakers wrote ma che/ma chẹ.

- Rule a speaker would state: Equative 'is' = chi; closing tag agrees in number: ... i che (singular subject) / ... ma che (plural subject); chẹ before a verb = already/completed; che is a spelling of chi in some speakers.
- Count: 8 judgments, 5 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Partly: 'Copulas: chi/chẹ = is (equals)' and row 22 'X S chẹ' tag; the number agreement of the tag (i che / ma che) and the chi-vs-chẹ split are not in the prompt. v4.4 used the tag once (gram_037 'ẹnẹ [money] i che') and omitted it on the plural gram_027. Prompts must collect plural equatives and test singular ones.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_gram_012 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `che odo eta ki le` -> speaker `chi ọdọ mẹta ki la le gwudu yi`. "Ki le is not a complete sentence"
  - ig_v42_gram_018 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Íyeyemi chẹ odo etegwa.` -> speaker `Íyeyemi chí ọdọ'mẹ ẹ̀tẹẹ̀gwa.`. "I corrected the tone mark and spelling"
  - ig_v42_gram_037 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `[father] mi ch'akọkọ.` -> speaker `[father] mi chi éluchẹ i che yí`. "Farmer in igala is Éluchẹ not Akọkọ"
  - ig_bank_gram_027 [annotator_6] (gold): model `Abobule mẹji lẹ ch'ami omaye. (v4.4)` -> speaker `Am'onobulẹ meji lẹ ch'ọmaye ma che`. "(gold; a4 and a7: Ami ọnọbulẹ i chi ọmayẹ ma chẹ)"
  - ig_v42_gram_002 [annotator_6] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Zainab chi akọchẹ.` -> speaker `Zainab che ak'onẹ ẹñwu i che`. "Not well constructed"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Equative pairs in one prompt, singular then plural with the same predicate ('He is a hunter' / 'They are hunters'), so the tag's agreement is visible; plus age/number equatives. Short, named-subject sentences gave decisive judgments (gram_002 4/4).
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Translate [god] is a hunter' and [god] and Audu are hunters' into Igala, keeping the same shape.
  - Translate 'These two men are my uncles' into Igala.
  - Translate 'My father is a blacksmith and my mother is a trader' into Igala.
  - Translate 'The old woman is ninety years old' into Igala.
  - Translate 'Those children are twins' into Igala.

### grammar_tone 11. Verb number agreement and spatial nouns in 'carry and put' commands: the bare arm uses singular du ... tẹ for many yams and is tagged grammar twice; the retrieval arm's 'kó [yam] lẹ du t'ane [tree] is rewritten by annotator_5 as 'kó [yam] lẹ jọ ofe [tree] (plural placement verb jọ, 'under' = ofe) and annotator_7 adds ọfẹ; v4.4 writes 'kó [yam] lẹ jọ anẹ [tree] lẹ' (right verb, wrong relational noun). For the single stool the model's òkìtì/ukpo/àkpà are replaced by okpẹti/akpẹtẹ and 'outside' is ugbọda / ọdọda, not [house].

- Rule a speaker would state: kó + plural object + jọ (place); du + single object + tẹ/wa; 'under X' = ofe X; 'outside' = ugbọda / ọdọda; 'inside the house' = efu [house]. The second verb agrees with the first in number (Salem's verb-object concord).
- Count: 6 judgments, 4 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Agreement is told (rows 3, 4, 11, 13, 24, 25 and 'du = take one, kó = take many'), and v4.4 now selects kó ... jọ; the relational nouns ofe (under), ugbọda (outside) are not in the prompt. Prompts must test agreement on new object/place pairs and collect the spatial vocabulary.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_gram_035 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Mẹ kó [yam] lẹ du t'ane [tree].` -> speaker `Mẹ kó [yam] lẹ jọ ofe [tree].`. "Under the tree in igala is ofe [tree] not t'ane [tree]"
  - ig_v42_gram_035 [annotator_3] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Mẹ dù [yam] lẹ tẹ ate ukwú lẹ.` -> speaker `Mẹ k'[yam] lẹ ji ofe [tree] (gold)`. "Wrong answer"
  - ig_v42_gram_034 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `dù òkìtì kí dẹ àtè wá tẹ́fù [house]` -> speaker `lo kẹ ná mi akpẹtẹ kítí okolo ugbọda lẹ duwe jefu`. "Outside in igala is ugbọda not [house]. [house] means house"
  - ig_v42_gram_034 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `dù òkìtì kí dẹ àtè` -> speaker `di òkpẹti kí di ọkọlọ`. "I corrected the sentense"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Imperatives with a countable object in both numbers and a spatial target (under, behind, beside, on top of, outside, inside), one pair per prompt (one pot / three pots), so both the verb pair and the relational noun are isolated. Command prompts drew explanations from a3 and a5 where statements drew ties.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Tell your son to take the one pot and put it under the bed, then tell him to take the three pots and put them outside.
  - Tell the children to carry the firewood and put it behind the kitchen.
  - Translate 'Bring the chair and put it beside the door' into Igala.
  - Translate 'She took the goats and tied them under the mango tree' into Igala.
  - Tell your brother to take the lamp from inside the room and put it on top of the table.

### grammar_tone 12. Plural prefixes on people nouns: speakers use three shapes, ami/àmì/am' (ami imọtọ, am'onobulẹ), abi/ab' (abimọtọ, abìmọto), abo/abọ (Abo Ajaokuta, abo obulẹ, abobulẹ), and annotator_6 rewrites the bare arm's Am'Ajaokuta to Abo Ajaokuta; annotator_3 tags 'Amí [child] with grammar and prefers 'Ami imoto'. The prompt lists only abọ and ama.

- Rule a speaker would state: People plural = ami/am' or abo/abọ (a6, a8 prefer abo for 'the people of X'); 'children' = ami imọtọ or abimọtọ (abi- is a third attested prefix); women = abobulẹ / am'onobulẹ.
- Count: 5 judgments, 1 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Partly: row 18 gives abọ/ama for people, ama only for animals; abi- and am' before imọtọ are not mentioned. v4.4 writes am'[child] / ami imọtọ, which matches a3/a5 but not a6/a8. Prompts should test plural marking across people, animals and things in one sentence.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_gram_006 [annotator_6] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Am'Ajaokuta kà kíni` -> speaker `Abo Ajaokuta kakini`. "(segment tagged tone_marks)"
  - ig_v42_gram_043 [annotator_3] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Amí [child] lọ [money] kpaí amí [mother] ma` -> speaker `Ami imoto lo t'[money] kpai ami [mother] ma eñíni (rag, chosen)`. "(bare arm tagged grammar)"
  - ig_bank_gram_017 [annotator_6] (gold): model `Ogijo lẹ a n'ọla am'[child] lẹ. (v4.4)` -> speaker `Ogijo lẹ agwa abimọtọ lẹ`. "(gold; a3 àmì ìmọtọ, a5 ami imọtọ, a8 amo'ma)"
  - ig_bank_gram_032 [annotator_8] (gold): model `ami imọtọ (v4.4)` -> speaker `abìmọto lẹ`. "(gold)"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Sentences with three plural nouns of different animacy (people, animals, things) so that one answer shows which get a prefix, which repeat and which stay bare; and 'the people of [town]' phrases.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Translate 'The women, the goats and the baskets are all at the river' into Igala.
  - Translate 'The people of Ejule bought many hoes at the market' into Igala.
  - Translate 'The children and their dogs ran into the houses' into Igala.
  - Translate 'Two elders and five young men came to the meeting' into Igala.

### grammar_tone 13. 'Now / currently' and clause linking: the retrieval arm joins clauses with oñ (Bible 'and'), rejected by four annotators (a4, a5 'Oñ is a wrong word', a6, a7), who replace it with ya (a4, a7), manyi ya (a6) or tákí (a5); annotator_5 also inserts ya into 'he is at the farm right now' (Ì ya dẹ́ èf'[money]). The bare arm's 'taki í chanẹ' was tagged by a5 and a7 for other words. For 'the king spoke and the people listened' three speakers juxtapose ([king] kọla amọnẹ nẹti), one uses taki, one kaki, one kà ... là; annotator_8 uses kpai between clauses.

- Rule a speaker would state: ya (preverbal) = now / at present / then; clauses are joined by plain juxtaposition, taki ('then, and'), or kaki/ka ki; oñ is not community Igala; kpai between clauses is one annotator's usage.
- Count: 8 judgments, 5 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: oñ is told ('oñ is Bible register the community rewrites') and v4.4 no longer produces it; 'clauses are joined by a new sentence' is told; the preverbal ya is absent from the prompt and rows (only 'aladi ki ya wa' appears in row 7 as 'next week'). Prompts must collect ya and the then/and linkers.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_gram_005 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `University oñ i chukolo efu Abuja abajoyi` -> speaker `University, ya chukolo efẹwọ Abuja abajoyi`. "I wrote the correct sentence"
  - ig_v42_gram_005 [annotator_6] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `oñ i chukolo` -> speaker `manyi ya chukọlọ`. "The output was not well constructed"
  - ig_v42_gram_011 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `2021 oñ i chanẹ echukolo ef'ọ́dọ́ ki wa le` -> speaker `2021 tákí i chanẹ echukolo ef'ọ́dọ́ ki le bọ`. "Oñ is a wrong word and wrong spelling"
  - ig_v42_gram_037 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Ì dẹ́ èf'[money] àbàjọyí.` -> speaker `Ì ya dẹ́ èf'[money] àbàjọyí.`. "(segment '' -> 'ya ' tagged wrong_word)"
  - ig_bank_gram_020 [annotator_6] (gold): model `[king] lẹ k'ọla, àmònẹ lẹ n'eti. (v4.4)` -> speaker `[king] lẹ k'ọla taki amonẹ lẹ neti`. "(gold; a4, a7, a8 juxtapose with no linker; a5 kaki)"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Biography-style two-event sentences ('studied X, now works in Y'; 'finished school, then started work') where the second clause is marked 'now' or 'then', plus present-state sentences with 'right now'. gram_005 and gram_011 drew 7 edits between them.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Translate 'She trained as a nurse in Lokoja and now runs a clinic in Ankpa' into Igala.
  - Translate 'He farmed for ten years, then he opened a shop' into Igala.
  - A visitor asks where your mother is. Say in one Igala sentence that she is cooking right now.
  - Translate 'The rain stopped and the children went out to play' into Igala.
  - Translate 'First we cleared the land, then we planted, and now we are waiting for the rain' into Igala.

### grammar_tone 14. Pronoun shape: the model uses the full pronoun wẹ as subject ('Wẹ kpa [hand] wẹ taki wẹ jẹñwu') where annotator_5 wants the clitic ẹ as subject and wẹ only as possessive (Ẹ gw'[hand] wẹ taki ẹ jẹñwu yí); six of seven speakers write 'Ugbo ẹlo' while v4.4 writes 'Ugbo wẹ alo?' and 'Wẹ chẹ gbọ ...'; annotator_5 corrects U de (I) to Í dẹ (he). For 'her mother' four speakers write [mother] ñwu, one [mother] wñ, one [mother] un, and the dative is written ñwi [mother] by four speakers, which the prompt bans as nwi.

- Rule a speaker would state: Subject clitics: u (I), ẹ (you), i (he/she), a (we), ma (they); wẹ is possessive or object; 3sg possessive = ñwu (majority), wñ/wn (a6, a8 variants); dative 'to' = ñwi before [mother] (du ñwi [mother] ñwu), ñwu elsewhere.
- Count: 3 judgments, 3 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Clitics are told (prompt table, row 20) yet v4.4 still writes wẹ as subject on gram_021 and gram_028, so this is a test-whether-telling-worked case. The dative ñwi and the possessive ñwu are told WRONG ('never nwi'; he/she poss = -wn): the gold majority is ñwi [mother] ñwu. Prompts must collect ditransitives and 2sg subjects.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_gram_030 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Wẹ kpa [hand] wẹ taki wẹ jẹñwu?` -> speaker `Ẹ gw'[hand] wẹ taki ẹ jẹñwu yí?`. "To wash hands in Igala is gw'[hand] not kpa [hand]"
  - ig_v42_gram_013 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `U de ìchèkpúlù lẹ odo mẹlu.` -> speaker `Í dẹ ìchèkpúlù lẹ odo mẹlu.`. "Í dẹ he was there / Not U dẹ meaning i was there"
  - ig_bank_gram_021 [annotator_6] (gold): model `Ugbo wẹ alo? (v4.4)` -> speaker `Ugbo ẹlo`. "(gold; a3 Úgbò ẹ lò, a4 ugbọ ẹlọ, a7 Ugbọ ẹlọ, a8 Ugbo ẹlo, a5 Ugbo ẹ loyi)"
  - ig_bank_gram_018 [annotator_3] (gold): model `I du ùjẹu lẹ ñw'[mother] wn. (v4.4)` -> speaker `Ìmì [food] dù ñwì [mother] ñwù`. "She gave the food to her mother (a4, a7: E mu [food] du ñwi [mother] ñwu; a6: ñwu [mother] wñ; a8: [mother] un)"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Second-person questions and commands (where ẹ vs wẹ is forced) and give/send/show ditransitives with a kin recipient; one object pronoun per sentence. Question prompts with a named addressee were decisive (gram_027 2/3, gram_030 2/3).
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Translate 'Did you see your brother at the market?' into Igala.
  - Translate 'Give the money to your father and the cloth to his wife' into Igala.
  - Translate 'I told him, but he did not tell her' into Igala.
  - Translate 'Where did you put your hoe?' into Igala.
  - Translate 'She sent her son to the teacher's house' into Igala.

### grammar_tone 15. Agent nouns: 'teacher' and 'farmer' split the speakers (akukọ a3; ẹnẹ ukọchẹ / ẹnẹ ukuko a5; ak'onẹ ẹñwu i che a6; iticha accepted by a7; éluchẹ a5; álucha a8; akọkọ kept by a3), and the models' akọchẹ / akọkọ / iticha are each tagged by someone. The formation (ẹnẹ + noun + i che, or a- prefix + verb) is the grammatical issue under the lexical disagreement.

- Rule a speaker would state: Agent noun = ẹnẹ (person) + activity noun + i che, or a prefixed deverbal (a-/ẹ-/o-: akukọ, éluchẹ); the loan iticha is tolerated by some; v4.4's 'ẹnẹ [money] i che' follows the periphrastic pattern.
- Count: 8 judgments, 4 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Row 15 records teacher as unsettled (iticha / akukọ / ẹnẹ ukọchẹ); farmer is not in any row; the formation rule is not in the prompt. Prompts must collect a paradigm of agent nouns so the linguists can state the rule.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_gram_002 [annotator_3] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Zainab ch'ítíchà.` -> speaker `Zainab chi akukọ`. "Akukọ is the real meaning of teacher in Igala"
  - ig_v42_gram_002 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Zainab chi akọchẹ.` -> speaker `Zainab chi ẹnẹ ukọche`. "Ẹnẹ ukọchẹ not iticha"
  - ig_v42_gram_037 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `[father] mi ch'akọko.` -> speaker `[father] mi chi éluchẹ i che yí`. "Farmer in igala is Éluchẹ not Akọkọ"
  - ig_v42_gram_037 [annotator_8] (gold): model `[father] mi chi ẹnẹ [money] i che. (v4.4)` -> speaker `[father]-mì chì álucha.`. "My father is farmer."
- Prompt shape that elicits it: One prompt per occupation in a fixed equative frame ('X is a Y'), five or six occupations whose Igala base verb is attested (sell, hunt, fish, drum, weave, drive), so the derivation pattern is comparable across answers.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Translate 'My brother is a fisherman' into Igala.
  - Translate 'That woman is a cloth seller' into Igala.
  - Translate 'His father was a hunter and his mother a potter' into Igala.
  - Translate 'The drummer at the festival is from Idah' into Igala.
  - Translate 'Our neighbour is a driver in Abuja' into Igala.

### grammar_tone 16. Yes-no questions and 'understand': speakers close yes-no questions with ba/bá (a5, a7), a (a3), yí (a5), lengthened final vowel (a5 'jeñwuu') or nothing (a3, a8), and annotator_6 adds d'ọmọ ([child] lẹ d'ọmọ aj'ẹñwu?'); the model's final n̄ on a question ('kí n ka n?') is rewritten to 'ku ka ba?'. 'Have you understood' splits a5 (nẹrejú) against a7 (gbo kept); the soup question 'is it ready' needs chẹ bie mẹ (a7) and both arms were judged inadequate.

- Rule a speaker would state: Yes-no question = statement + optional final particle ba / a / yí (no nasal); 'is it done/ready' = X lẹ chẹ bie mẹ?; 'understand' = nẹrejú (a5) or gbọ 'hear' (a7).
- Count: 9 judgments, 5 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Row 15 records the yes-no particle as open (a / á / ba); v4.4 now closes with ba (gram_025, 027, 031) and a (gram_024). Nothing on nẹrejú or chẹ bie mẹ. Prompts must get three or more annotators onto the same yes-no question so the particle can be settled, and collect 'ready / done / understood'.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_gram_028 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Wẹ gbo ẹnwù kí n ka n?` -> speaker `Wẹ gbo ẹnwù ku ka ba?`. "I did spelling correction"
  - ig_v42_gram_028 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Abíne, ewñ ki u ka lẹ ye wẹ?` -> speaker `Ẹ wú kú ka yí che nẹrejú? (gold)`. "To understand in igala means enẹrejú"
  - ig_v42_gram_027 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `aláfíya ma wà?` -> speaker `chọ'lafía bá?`. "(segment tagged wrong_word, grammar)"
  - ig_v42_gram_024 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `[mother], ọ́rọ lẹ chẹ?` -> speaker `[mother], ọ́bọ lẹ chẹ bie mẹ? (gold)`. "Both are slightly wrong"
  - ig_bank_gram_028 [annotator_6] (gold): model `[child] lẹ aj'ẹñwu? (v4.4)` -> speaker `[child] lẹ d'ọmọ aj'ẹñwu?`. "(gold; a5 [child] lẹ a jeñwuu; a7 [child] yẹ a chẹ ñwu?)"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Paired statement-and-question prompts ('Write: the soup is ready. Now ask your mother whether the soup is ready.') so the particle is the only difference; routed to all annotators. Single-annotator question prompts (gram_024, 033, 040) gave both_inadequate with no usable form.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Write 'The water is hot' in Igala, then turn it into the question 'Is the water hot?'
  - Your friend explained the road to the clinic. Ask her in one Igala sentence whether you have understood her correctly, then write her reply if you have.
  - Ask your father in one Igala sentence whether the yams are ready to be dug.
  - Write 'He has come' and 'Has he come?' in Igala.
  - Ask the children whether they have eaten, then write their 'yes' answer and their 'no' answer.

**Shapes that produced decisive, explained judgments**

- Short translation (6 to 12 English words) with a named person and a named place, one grammatical target per sentence: Single visible word for the target (ef / efu / efẹwọ; lo vs lo ti; chi vs chẹ), so judgments were decisive and edits were one segment with a stated reason. gram_002 4 decisive of 4, gram_001 3 of 4, gram_006 3 of 6 plus 5 edits. (ig_v42_gram_001, ig_v42_gram_002, ig_v42_gram_006)
- Spoken-line numeric prompts: an age, a headcount, a price, a date, or counting aloud, with the number stated in the English: Speakers know the exact form and write it: '70 in igala is ẹ́tẹẹ̀gwa', '85 is ọgbọmẹlẹ ñyọ mẹlú', '14th is ẹgwẹlẹ not ẹgwẹfa'. 2 of 3 decisive on gram_018, 020, 021, 023 and two edits each. (ig_v42_gram_018, ig_v42_gram_020, ig_v42_gram_021, ig_v42_gram_014)
- Question plus two answers (yes / no) or three-step paradigms (same verb, three tenses): Forces the speaker to show morphology side by side; produced multi-segment edits with glosses (gram_030: five segments by a5) and glossed paradigms from four annotators on gram_033. (ig_v42_gram_030, ig_bank_gram_033, ig_bank_gram_024)
- Commands with a countable object and a spatial target: Drew explanations naming the rule ('Under the tree in igala is ofe [tree] not t'ane [tree], 'Outside in igala is ugbọda not [house]) and exposed du/kó + tẹ/jọ agreement. (ig_v42_gram_035, ig_v42_gram_034)
- Reported speech with a matrix verb and a different embedded subject: Isolated kakini doubling and pronoun switching; the most-edited prompt in the bucket (5 edits, 6 judgments). (ig_v42_gram_006, ig_v42_gram_031)

**Shapes that produced ties and "both inadequate"**

- Open-ended 'say something about your area / tell a visitor where a town lies / describe the landscape' with no fixed content: Speakers answer with their own content (annotator_8 wrote 'Masaka deju [hand] mararaba Nyanya', outside Igala-land) and tie because there is nothing to compare; gram_044 3 ties of 3, gram_008 1 tie plus an unrelated gold, gram_017 'three things in one sentence' got 4 different towns. (ig_v42_gram_044, ig_v42_gram_008, ig_v42_gram_017)
- Single-judgment prompts with an idiomatic target (hope you pass, is the soup ready, go and buy pepper): One annotator, both_inadequate, explanation 'Both are inadequate' or 'Both are slightly wrong', no corrected form in the comparison; the gold alone carries the rule. (ig_v42_gram_040, ig_v42_gram_033, ig_v42_gram_024)
- Long multi-clause biography or duration sentences ('how long he taught', 'studied X and now works in Y'): Too many simultaneous targets (loanwords for school, 'now', clause linker, pronoun person), so judgments tie (gram_013 3 ties of 3) or the edit rewrites half the sentence with 'I wrote the correct sentence' and no per-word reason. (ig_v42_gram_013, ig_v42_gram_005, ig_v42_gram_011)
- Bare 'Translate X into Igala, keeping the [correlative / counterfactual / focus] structure': The structural label does not reach the speaker; gold on gram_034 (focus) ranges from a two-word cleft to a full clause, and gram_035 gold copies between annotators (a4, a5, a7 identical). Also 15 holdout bank prompts and 18 longform prompts never ran at all. (ig_bank_gram_034, ig_bank_gram_035, ig_bank_gram_038)
- Any prompt routed only to annotator_8 or annotator_7 without annotator_5 or annotator_3: annotator_8 tied 29 of 29 and writes 'In Igala the word for ... is' with a gold that often departs from the prompt; annotator_7 ties 19 of 42 and gives 3 explanations in 42 judgments (but 29 edits). Explanations come from a5 (17) and a3 (10). (ig_v42_gram_009, ig_v42_gram_016, ig_v42_gram_022)

**What the speakers' own writing shows**

- tone marks: Four of six gold writers (a4, a5, a6, a7) write with no accents; annotator_3 marks nearly every syllable in bank gold (Ògìjò à gwì àmì ìmọtọ) and annotator_8 marks irregularly (Ógwú nyọ́ kāà, with macrons). Dotted ẹ/ọ are treated as letters and corrected (Abobule -> Abobulẹ 'Wrong tone marks'). The tone_marks tag is used for spelling and word errors far more than for accents. Nobody corrected the tone on ki/kì/kí.
- contractions: annotator_6 contracts consistently with a straight apostrophe (k'[water], aj'ẹñwu, t'[money], am'onobulẹ, ch'ọmaye); annotator_3 writes expanded forms with tones (kì [mother] mì); a4, a5, a7 mix and place apostrophes inside words (Chẹ'ñwu, kọ'mi, a'loti, [food], jẹ'ñwu, ọdọ'mẹ). annotator_6 expands ef'ádṣa to efu [market]; annotator_3 keeps ef'[market]. Salem's expanded-form request is only annotator_3's practice.
- ki usage: ki is written unmarked by all but annotator_3 (who writes kì for REL and kí/kíní in kakini). It appears as relativizer ([food] ki [mother] mi hi), after chẹñwu in conditionals (chẹñwu k'[water] lọ, 7 of 7), in kakini/ka ki ni, after egba ('when': egba k'[water] chanẹ), and contracted with pronouns: ku = ki + u (ẹnwu ku ka 'what I said', a5, a7), ka = ki + a (Ẹnẹkẹlẹ ka lo t'[money], a6, a3), kẹ = ki + ẹ (a5). ku also precedes plural ma (Abobulẹ ku ma t'ẹja). Relative clauses are not closed with lẹ in 14 of 14 bank gold rows.
- dialect: Only the v42 gold carries a dialect: annotator_3 'other' on all 9 rows (forms: efewo idah i gwu i, akukọ, lotọdọda, ka lo tókó), annotator_8 'ankpa' once (kwì-[goat] lokoja mà, chẹ ká kákìnì) and general_idah otherwise (ìsakulu, ácìbìtì, Áko'na), a5 and a6 general_idah. annotator_7 edits onokẹrẹ to onokẹlẹ (r -> l, the Ogwugwu alternation reversed) but keeps Ónokẹ́rẹ on gram_025. annotator_6 doubles consonants ([father]). The models mix: the bare arm leans Yoruba (ṣ'uṣe, má, ra, nla, alaafia), the rag arm imports onokẹrẹ, onobirẹ, àwéhì.
- other: Yes-no questions close with ba/bá (a5, a7), a (a3), yí (a5) or nothing. 'and' between nouns is kpai; between clauses speakers juxtapose or use taki/kaki, annotator_8 uses kpai. Future/intention is na (a na lo, Mama na li, Na alọ), counterfactual is ma (a ma dẹdẹ). 3sg possessive ñwu (a3, a4, a5, a7), wñ (a6), un/wn (a8); dative ñwi before [mother] (4 annotators). 'now' = abajọyi sentence-final, or preverbal ya (a5, a6, a7). Equative tag i che / ma che agrees in number (a4, a5, a6, a7, a8). annotator_5 and annotator_7 both wrote 'ọdọ mẹta ki la le gudu (yi)' for 'three years ago'.

**Open questions (speakers disagree)**

- Seventy: annotator_5 ẹ̀tẹẹ̀gwa (blended) vs annotator_8 ọ́gbọmẹta nyọ mẹgwā (three twenties plus ten) on ig_v42_gram_018; one hundred: ọ́gwú-mẹ́lù (bare arm, kept by a5) vs ọgbọ mẹ́lu (rag, kept by a7) vs Ógwúmẹ́lū (a8) on gram_015; one thousand: annotator_5 akpulu (gram_019 gold) vs the v4.4 row's icham nyogwoko. Route the same numbers to a3, a4, a6 to break the ties.
- Preverbal negative ma in 'why are you not playing' (a5 'wẹ ma ch'íya', a7 'wẹ ma chiya', a8 'wà ma chìya', all with a final n) against the prompt's 'ONLY a clause-final nasal'. Is ma a negative, an irrealis, or both (the same ma gives 'would have' in gram_031)? Salem should rule.
- na as a future marker with non-first-person subjects (a4, a5 'a na lo'; a7 'Mama na li'; a4, a7 'a na mi') against row 1's 'na only in announce frames'. Pronoun or particle, and does it combine with á (a8 'Ná á lọ')?
- ku: the prompt says 'before plural ma'; gold shows ku = ki + u ('ẹnwu ku ka' a5, a7) and annotator_5 writes kú wẹ. Is the ku/ka/kẹ series simply ki + pronoun elision, and is kì/kí tone (Salem's REL vs COMP) recoverable at all from speakers who never mark it?
- lẹ closing a relative clause: required by the prompt, absent from all 14 bank gold rows on gram_022 and gram_030, but kept by a3 and a7 in 'ku ma t'ẹja ọmọ lẹ' on gram_041 (where lẹ may be 'the' on ọmọ). Needs a minimal pair.
- 'and' between clauses: annotator_5 tákí (gram_011 edit, 'Tákí - And'), annotator_6 taki (gram_020 gold), annotator_8 kpai (gram_011, gram_016 gold), a4/a7/a8 bare juxtaposition on gram_020. The prompt says kpai never joins clauses.
- Dative and 3sg possessive: four annotators write 'ñwi [mother] ñwu' (gram_018) while the prompt bans nwi and gives -wn; annotator_6 writes wñ, which the prompt says never to end a word with. Which is the Idah written norm?
- 'understand': annotator_5 nẹrejú ('To understand in igala means enẹrejú') vs annotator_7 keeping gbo on the same prompt (gram_028). 'wait': dẹdẹ/dede (a3, a4, a5, a7) vs dago (a6) vs v4.4's nẹdẹ. 'teacher': akukọ (a3) vs ẹnẹ ukọchẹ (a5) vs ak'onẹ ẹñwu i che (a6) vs iticha (a7). 'farmer': éluchẹ (a5) vs álucha (a8) vs akọko kept (a3).
- Prohibition without kì: annotator_3 'Ẹ lò tọmọ ń' against six speakers with ki/kì on gram_026; is the kì-less form a variety (a3 declares 'other') or an omission?
- 'all gone home': kpa (both arms, kept by a7 on comparison) removed by annotator_3 ('le t'[house]') and replaced by chakadu by annotator_7 in his edit; what carries 'all' in Idah speech?
- Expanded vs contracted writing (Salem) is only annotator_3's practice; annotator_6, the most consistent Idah writer, contracts everything. If expanded forms become the target, whose gold is the reference, and should the prompt's elision rule be reversed?
