- The confidence field is null on all 130 rows and the dialect field is null on 194 of 244 gold; neither the UI nor the bank captured what the write-up asks for (one declared variety per answer).

## Lexicon (85 judgments, 44 edits, 248 gold)

Data read: comparisons-lexicon_disambig.json: 85 rows, every one read. Winners 25 tie, 25 b, 22 a, 13 both_inadequate; rag-v3 took 31 decisive wins, bare 16. Only 26/85 carry an explanation (a5 9, a7 9, a8 4, a3 3, a6 1). annotator_8 tied all 16 of her judgments; annotator_5 picked a 14 times and b 5. Tags by arm: grammar bare 36 / rag 21, tone_marks bare 21 / rag 9, wrong_word bare 6 / rag 3, wrong_language bare 5 / rag 2, cultural 5/3, invented 2/1. edits-lexicon_disambig.json: 44 edits (a5 17, a7 17, a3 7, a6 3; 30 on rag-v3, 14 on bare), segment tags tone_marks 21, grammar 10, other 6, wrong_word 4, cultural 3, english_mixed 2, not_igala 1. gold-lexicon_disambig.json: 248 rows (a8 62, a5 49, a7 44, a3 35, a6 34, a4 24), 29 on v42 prompts. prompts-lexicon_disambig.json: 92 prompts. prompt-informativeness.json: 28 lex rows. prompt-v4-4.txt, grammar-rows-oct8.json (30 rows, incl. the Sep 2026 'words the community corrected' and 'open questions' rows), writeup-oct8.txt.

### lexicon_disambig 1. Borrowed academic and technical nouns: the two arms split (rag keeps the English letters 'biology', 'economics', 'password', 'operation', 'East'; bare respells Ẹkọnọmiks, sáyíkọ́lọ́jì, páswọ̀dù, opereshọn, Gírésì) and the speakers split three ways: respell with Igala letters, keep the English word, or replace with a native word or paraphrase. The model also wraps the loan in a relative clause (kì [name]-wn chi economics) that a speaker deleted.

- Rule a speaker would state: No settled rule yet. annotator_5 and annotator_7 state: a loan is written with Igala letters (s becomes ch, no English spelling), e.g. ẹkẹnọmiks, cháyíkọ́lọ́jì, paswọdú, ágriki. annotator_8 and the write-up say the opposite: a foreign word or name keeps its letters. annotator_6 and annotator_5 sometimes prefer a native word (igede for password, ukọlọ ọlá for operation, chegbulu Atẹ for university). Replacement types: ẹkẹnọmiks/ẹkọñọmiks (respelled borrowing), cháyíkọ́lọ́jì (respelled borrowing), paswọdú (respelled borrowing), igede (native, literally padlock), ágriki (respelled borrowing), chegbulu Atẹ (native paraphrase), yuñivasítí (respelled borrowing), ukọlọ ọlá (native paraphrase 'work on the body'), psychology/biology kept (bare loan).
- Count: 23 judgments, 10 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Partly. Rule 9 (names keep their letters, Igala has no s but a name is not an Igala word) and rule 4 (keep the everyday loanword, never coin an Igala-looking form) cover names and everyday loans, but nothing tells the model whether an academic or technical loan is respelled (s to ch, dotted vowels) or kept in English letters, and the write-up's phonology section says foreign words are not respelled, which two annotators contradict. The 'kì [name]-wn chi' circumlocution is not banned. Prompts must test whether rule 9 holds for names and collect a decision for common nouns.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_lex_004 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `I ch'ukọchẹ economics.` -> speaker `I ch'ukọchẹ ẹkẹnọmiks`. "Ecnomics is English not igala"
  - ig_v42_lex_005 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `sáyíkọ́lọ́jì` -> speaker `cháyíkọ́lọ́jì`. "They is no s in Igala Alphabet"
  - ig_v42_lex_008 [annotator_6] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Ẹ kì du password wẹ ñw'ẹnẹ duu ǹ.` -> speaker `Ẹ kì du igede wẹ ñw'ẹnẹ du-n`. "segment tagged english_mixed; annotator_5 on the same text wrote 'Paswọdú is igala meaning your password'"
  - ig_v42_lex_027 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Ónokẹ́rẹ òókáà kọ ukọchẹ kì [name]-wn chi economics efu university.` -> speaker `Ónokẹlẹ lẹ kọchẹ ẹkọñọmiks ukọchẹ efu yuñivasítí.`. "Wrong words, spelling and sentence (tags wrong_word, tone_marks, grammar)"
  - ig_v42_lex_001 [annotator_8] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Ẹnyọ // Gírésì (A) vs Ufedenyo // Grace (B)` -> speaker `ufẹdọ-ẹnyọ abekì eju (a8 gold); a5 tagged Ẹnyọ/Gírésì wrong_word and chose Grace`. "Output A is correct ,out B is mixed with English"
  - ig_v42_lex_003 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `[child] ọmaye mi á kọchẹ biology efu yunifasiti` -> speaker `[child] [child] wẹ á kọchẹ biology efu chegbulu Atẹ`. "Chegbulu atẹ - University"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Pair a single borrowed noun inside one spoken sentence with a required one-line English note on how the noun was written, then ask the speaker in the prompt to write the noun as THEY would spell it in a text message. The decisive judgments came when the loan was the only variable (lex_004, lex_027 shared 0.75 and 0.33 decisive); the respelling evidence came from the 'add an English line' shape (lex_005, lex_027). Avoid asking 'is this an Igala word or English', which produced meta-commentary judged both_inadequate.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Your sister has started studying nursing in Lokoja. Tell your father in one Igala sentence what she is studying, then add one English line saying how you wrote the word for the subject and why.
  - Write one Igala sentence for a Wikipedia article saying that a man teaches chemistry at a secondary school in Ankpa. Keep the subject in the sentence and spell it the way you would type it in an Igala text message.
  - A woman called Mercy runs the shop next door. In one Igala sentence say that Mercy sells kerosene. Then say in English whether you changed any letters of her name and why.
  - Tell your mother in Igala that your brother has a job as an engineer at the electricity company. Write the word for engineer the way you would actually write it to her on WhatsApp.
  - A university lecturer is named in an Igala article as teaching sociology. Write the sentence, then in English say whether you kept the English word, respelled it with Igala letters, or used a different Igala expression, and which of the three you think most Igala speakers would do.

### lexicon_disambig 2. Everyday loan objects and Hausa or Yoruba loans: the model's spelling or choice of the community loanword is wrong (bokiti for bokẹti/iboketi, Baturi for Batiri, adọkita for dọkita, asibiti where a speaker wants achíbítí, ọ́sẹ for aladi, allura for ogwu, eku for igede), and the arms disagree on whether the object is a loan at all (fọmu vs ate/ọ'tìtìmù for mattress, tọchi vs uganẹ for torch).

- Rule a speaker would state: The community has one fixed spelling per adopted loan and it must be copied: bokẹti or iboketi (bucket), Batiri (battery), dọkita with no initial a- (doctor), achibiti (hospital), aladi (week), igede (padlock), ogwu (medicine, also 'vaccine'), fónu/foonu (phone), jẹ́nì or jẹ́nẹ́rétọ̀ (generator). Yoruba ọsẹ and Hausa allura are rejected. Replacement types: bokẹti/iboketi (borrowing, corrected vowel), Batiri (borrowing), dọkita (borrowing minus prefix), achíbítí (borrowing, s to ch), aladi (community loan replacing Yoruba loan), ogwu (native replacing Hausa loan), igede (native), àgbà (native, one annotator), ate and ọ'tìtìmù (native, two annotators) vs fọmu (borrowing, two annotators), uganẹ (native) vs tọchi (borrowing).
- Count: 21 judgments, 16 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Partly. ọsẹ is in NEVER WRITE; the grammar row 'words the community corrected' gives aladi, igede, dọkita (no a-), ugbo, neke. Nothing on bucket, mattress, torch, battery, hospital spelling, vaccine, or the Hausa loan allura. Prompts should test the told items (week, padlock, doctor) and collect the untold ones.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_lex_014 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `fọmu, bokiti, tọchi kpai eku` -> speaker `fọmu, bokẹti, tọchi kpai àgbà`. "Bọkiti is wrong // Bọkẹti is right"
  - ig_v42_lex_014 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `fọmu, bokiti, tọchi kpai eku` -> speaker `ate, iboketi, uganẹ kpai ìgèdè`. "segments tagged english_mixed and not_igala; a8 gold on the same prompt: ọ'tìtìmù , Ibokiti, Tọ́chì, kpaí Ìgèdè"
  - ig_v42_lex_009 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `efu ọ́sẹ kí a wá yí, todù kí ma chẹ allura nwí amí [child]` -> speaker `efu aladi kí a wá yí, todù ku ma di ogwu nwí amí [child]`. "Aladi - week; Di ogwu - igala"
  - ig_v42_lex_006 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Baturi foonu mi kpa. Ubo k'u á chaj'u?` -> speaker `Batiri foonu mi lekwu. Ugbo unẹkẹ a chaj'u?`. "My phone battery died; where can I charge it?"
  - ig_v42_lex_012 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `tọd'uny'ogwu lẹ (hospital)` -> speaker `achibiti (a5 gold: na dọ'kọ achibiti)`. "Hospital in igala is achíbítí"
  - ig_v42_lex_010 [annotator_6] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `abo adọkita a ch'opereshọn nwu [money] wẹ` -> speaker `dọkita a ch'ukọlọ [money] wẹ`. "segment ch'opereshọn tagged cultural; a5 wrote dọkítọ aná chí ukọlọ ọlá [money] wẹ"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: A spoken sentence to a named family member that lists three or four concrete modern objects (a shopping or packing list, a market errand, a clinic visit). Lists produced the richest edits (lex_014: three annotators each corrected a different item) and decisive verdicts (0.75). Put at most one Yoruba or Hausa bait word in the English prompt (week, needle, injection) so the edit isolates the loan.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - You are sending your son to the market. In Igala, tell him to buy a torch battery, a bar of soap, a bucket and a padlock, the way you would really say it.
  - Tell your neighbour in Igala that the clinic nurse will come next week on Wednesday to give the baby an injection, in the words you would use to her.
  - Your daughter's phone screen is broken. In one Igala sentence tell your husband you will take it to the repairer at the motor park tomorrow.
  - Read out to your wife in Igala the four things the hospital asked you to bring for her stay: a mattress, a bedsheet, a bucket and a flask.
  - Tell your brother in Igala that the doctor at the general hospital said your father must come back in two weeks for an X-ray.

### lexicon_disambig 3. Non-Igala forms the speakers reject outright: letters č and ṣ (Ọ̀wọ̀čo, Éfodṣa, ádṣa, Ọlọ́dṣi, Idṣégwẹ), words no speaker recognises (onka, ọ̀wá, àwéhì, uloji, Ọ̀gẹ́bẹ̀, é-dohì), and Yoruba lexis from the bare arm (Áríwa, ókè, odò, ọ̀rọ̀, tàbí, Àmàà, alafia, nla, ọ̀kpa). rag-v3 produces the invented shapes, bare produces the Yoruba.

- Rule a speaker would state: If a word needs a letter outside the Igala alphabet, or is not one a speaker would say, it is not Igala: south is opata/apata, 'this one' is oka, 'the other one' is ẹkẹji, 'over there' is ọmọ yí or ọmọ, right hand is [hand] awohì, left hand [hand] awọtọ, town is [goat]/[goat], north is ojukpale or ukpale. Replacement types: Opata (native), oka/ẹkẹji (native), ọ̀mọ yí (native), awohì (native, vowel corrected), ojukpale (native), deletion of uloji and Ọ̀wọ̀čo (no replacement).
- Count: 21 judgments, 13 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Yes, explicitly. The letters rule bans č and ṣ; the 'Nobody's words' list names ádṣa, éfodṣa, onka, awehi, onobirẹ, onokẹrẽ; NEVER WRITE names tàbí, Àmàà, nla, alafia, ọkpa. So every new prompt here is a regression test: re-run the exact contexts (south, this one/that one, left/right hand, town south of Idah, rainy season) with changed surface details and check whether the banned form reappears or a new invented form takes its place.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_lex_016 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Ọ̀wọ̀čo, Opata.` -> speaker `Opata.`. "Ọ́wọ̀čo - wrong // Opata - right; edit rationale: Ọ̀wọ̀čo is not igala"
  - ig_v42_lex_017 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `onka de. onka lẹ de ọmọ.` -> speaker `oka de. ẹkẹji lẹ de ọmọ.`. "Onka sounds like Idoma"
  - ig_v42_lex_017 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Èlẹ́ dẹ́ ọ̀wá.` -> speaker `Èlẹ́ dẹ́ ọ̀mọ yí`. "Ọ́wa is not igala"
  - ig_v42_lex_018 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `ch'ọ́w'àwéhì` -> speaker `chọ'wọ awohí`. "[hand] Awehí is wrong // [hand] Awohí is right meaning right hand"
  - ig_v42_lex_026 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Éfodṣa lẹ d'ọ̀wọ̀čo Idah.` -> speaker `[goat] yí dẹ ... (both a5 and a7 chose the bare [goat] sentence)`. "tagged wrong_language by a7, grammar by a5; no explanation"
  - ig_v42_lex_015 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Áríwa chẹ ọ̀rọ̀ kù ma a lò. Ọ̀rọ̀ ọ̀mùnẹ̀ chẹ Òkè.` -> speaker `Ojukpale/Ukpale (a7 gold)`. "Both are not correct"
  - ig_v42_lex_021 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Apá [hand] uloji Steeti Kogi` -> speaker `Apá [hand] Steeti Kogi`. "Because oloji is not supose to be there (reason: No word)"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Short two-sentence deictic or directional tasks where exactly one slot is the risky word and the rest is trivially right, so a tie is unlikely and the edit lands on the slot. lex_016 and lex_018 (0.67 decisive) produced the clearest 'X wrong, Y right' explanations in the whole slice. Keep each prompt to one target word; the five-word lists of directions in older prompts produced four different gold systems.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - A child drops two coins. One rolls to your foot, the other under the bench. In Igala say 'this one is here' and 'the other one is under the bench', as two short sentences.
  - Your friend asks where the new borehole is. In one short Igala phrase, say it is on the south side of the village.
  - You are teaching a child to greet with the right hand. In Igala tell him: give the elder your right hand, not your left hand.
  - For an Igala Wikipedia article, write one sentence saying the town of Ayangba lies north of Anyigba.
  - Tell your sister in Igala that the rainy season has started early this year, in the sentence you would really say.

### lexicon_disambig 4. Cardinal directions and up/down the river: the bare arm uses anẹ (ground, west) for south and Yoruba Áríwa/ókè/odò; rag invents. The speakers themselves run two systems: opata = south, ojukpale/ukpale = north, apa/apo = east, ichi = west (a3, a5, a6, a7) versus ọpata atẹ = north, ọpata ọganẹ = south, [sun] [morning] = east, [sun] anẹ = west (a4, a8 Ankpa). atẹ (up) and ọganẹ (down) also carry upriver/downriver.

- Rule a speaker would state: Direction is a side ([hand] or 'apa') plus a landmark: east = where the sun rises ([sun] [morning]) or apa/apo, west = [sun] anẹ or ichi, south = opata or apata, north = ojukpale/ukpale; the Ankpa system builds north/south from atẹ (up) and ọganẹ (down). Upriver = lo ti atẹ aji, downriver = wa ọganẹ aji. Replacement types: all native; 'East' (English) replaced by apa baki [sun] [morning] (native paraphrase, a7) or Apo (native, a3 gold).
- Count: 12 judgments, 7 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: No. The prompt text has no direction words. Only the scholarship_note grammar row records 'south = opata (two annotators, one rejects); north unresolved'. Prompts must collect, per self-declared variety, the four words and the up/down river pair, and make the annotator say which system is theirs.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_lex_026 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `[goat] yí dẹ [hand] anẹ Idah.` -> speaker `[goat] lẹ dẹ [hand] apata Idah.`. "segment anẹ -> apata, reason: South; yí -> lẹ tagged wrong_word"
  - ig_v42_lex_026 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `[hand] anẹ Idah` -> speaker `[hand] Awoto baki ojukpale baki ukpale Idah`. "I wrote the correct word (tags grammar, cultural); note ojukpale/ukpale is the word a7 gives for NORTH in lex_015 gold"
  - ig_v42_lex_021 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `[hand] East Kogi State` -> speaker `[hand] apa baki [sun] [morning] Kogi State`. "I corrected the spelling and added the correct word for east; a3 gold: Apo kogi state"
  - ig_v42_lex_020 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `I á lo [head] aji kpai [money], i á wa ùbì.` -> speaker `I á loti ulẹ [head] aji kpai [money], i á ñwọ dabí wa ọganẹ aji`. "Ọganẹ aji is igala; a8 gold: lọ tì atẹ ajì dà kubì wa ogane ajì"
  - ig_bank_lex_028 [annotator_8] (gold): model `(gold only)` -> speaker `East [sun]-[morning] west [sun]-anẹ North ọpàta-átẹ South ọpàta-ọganẹ (dialect ankpa); a4: North Ọpata Atẹ / Ojukpale, South Ọpata Ọganẹ; a3: East Apo, West Ichi, South Opata, North Oj'ukpale`. "four annotators, two systems"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: One direction per prompt, embedded in a sentence with a real Kogi landmark (Idah, Ankpa, the Niger, the Benue confluence), plus a final clause asking the speaker to name their town. Never ask for all four at once (ig_bank_lex_028 gave five incompatible gold lists with no explanation).
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Your cousin asks where Ankpa is from Idah. In one short Igala sentence tell him Ankpa is to the north-east, and at the end write the name of the town whose Igala you speak.
  - A fisherman says he will paddle up the Niger towards Lokoja and return the next day. In Igala, say those two things the way a river person would.
  - For an Igala Wikipedia article, write one sentence saying that Ibaji lies to the south of Idah where the Niger and Benue have already met.
  - A child asks which way the sun goes down. Point and say it in one Igala sentence, then say whether that same word is what you use for 'west' when reading a map.
  - Your neighbour's son left for the north to find work in Kano. In one short Igala phrase say which way he went, and give every word for that direction your own town uses.

### lexicon_disambig 5. Verb sense near-misses in everyday speech: kpa (kill) for a dead battery where speakers say lekwu/le kwu or ta'kpa; chẹ allura (do a needle) for giving a vaccine where speakers say di ogwu; a lá ọla for an operation where speakers say li efu ọla or kpa wu; tẹ/tá for switching on a generator where speakers want tiá, fú, ti or tájo; t'òde for 'outside' where speakers write t'òda, t'[name] or ọdoda; kí before ma where speakers want ku.

- Rule a speaker would state: Choose the verb for the thing, not for the English word: a battery or phone 'dies' (lekwu) or 'finishes' (ta'kpa), it is not 'killed' (kpa); medicine is 'given' with di/du ogwu; a generator is 'lit' (tájo, ti); 'go outside' is lo t'ọda. Replacement types: all native verbs; chẹ allura (Hausa loan + light verb) replaced by di ogwu (native).
- Count: 18 judgments, 15 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: No for the verbs themselves (die/finish, switch on, give medicine, go outside are not in the prompt or the 30 rows). The di vs du question is only recorded as open. The kí/ku split before plural ma IS told ('Relativizer kì (singular), ku before plural ma/me') and a5 and a3 still corrected kí to ku on bare output, so that part is a test of whether telling worked.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_lex_006 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Baturi foonu mi kpa.` -> speaker `Baturi foonu mi ta'kpa.`. "Ta'kpa means finish in Igala not kpa, Kpa means kill"
  - ig_v42_lex_006 [annotator_3] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `fónù mi kú, bátìrì ñwu mẹ. Égó ù nẹ kù n cháàjì ñwu?` -> speaker `fónù mi le kwu, bátìrì ñwu dọ gẹ n ugbo ù nẹ ke cháàjì ñwu?`. "Wrong tone marks and spelling (all segments tagged tone_marks though the change is lexical)"
  - ig_v42_lex_009 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `todù kí ma chẹ allura nwí amí [child]` -> speaker `todù ku ma di ogwu nwí amí [child]`. "Di ogwu ñwi ami ọmọtọ - igala, Meaning to give drug to children; Ku - igala"
  - ig_v42_lex_013 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Lo t'òde kì tá jẹ́nì.` -> speaker `Lo t'òda kẹ fú jẹní lẹ tájo`. "Lo'tọda not lo'tode, Meaning go outside; Tájo - igala"
  - ig_v42_lex_013 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Abíne, ló t'óde k'ẹ tẹ jẹ́nì.` -> speaker `Abíne, ló t'[name] k'ẹ tiá jẹ́nì.`. "the spelling (tẹ -> tiá); a3 on the bare text: 'Lo kẹ nya ti jẹ́nì', rationale 'Wrong word'"
  - ig_v42_lex_010 [annotator_3] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Ma a lá ọla nwu tódu ki ma du [goat] lẹ gwá, ki ọla nwu ki dabi gba.` -> speaker `Ma li efu ọla nwu tódu ku ma di'wọ lẹ, kwi ọla nwu todu ki dabi gbiti`. "segment a lá -> li efu tagged wrong_word; a6 on the same text: Ma kpa wu na todu ku ma du ẹñwu lẹ kwo, k'ọla ñwu ki mẹ"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Household imperative or report where one English verb has a tempting literal Igala verb with a different meaning (kill/die, light/switch on, do/give). Two sentences max, spoken to a named relative. These produced edits with verb-level rationales (lex_006, lex_009, lex_013 all had 3 or more edits).
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Your torch has gone dead. In Igala tell your sister the torch is finished and ask her to put in new batteries.
  - Tell your younger brother in Igala to go outside and switch off the generator before he sleeps.
  - The nurse gave your baby medicine at the clinic. Tell your mother in Igala what the nurse did, in one sentence.
  - Your phone fell in water and will not come on. Tell your friend in Igala what happened and ask whether the repairer can bring it back to life.
  - Tell your husband in Igala that the doctors will open your father's stomach tomorrow to remove the thing that is paining him.

### lexicon_disambig 6. Kin and address terms: the model picks or invents the wrong vocative and the wrong possessive chain. Bare wrote Ìnya mì for aunt (tagged invented by two annotators), rag wrote Abíne for younger brother (one annotator rejects for Agíní, one keeps), rag wrote [child] ọmaye mi for 'your grandchild' where the speaker wants [child] [child] wẹ, bare wrote gbà [child]-[child] wẹ̀ (word order), rag wrote Íiye for mother where [mother] suffices.

- Rule a speaker would state: Address a relative with the plain kin word plus possessive: [mother] (mother), Yaya mi or Anti (aunt), Abíne mi or Agíní or ọmaye mi (younger brother, variety-dependent), [money] mi (husband), ọ́yà (wife); grandchild to the grandmother is [child] [child] wẹ. Replacement types: Yaya mi (community loan from Hausa yaya, a5), Anti (English loan, a3), Agíní (native, a5), [child] [child] wẹ (native).
- Count: 11 judgments, 9 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Partly. The vocatives row says address is the plain kin word plus honorific plural; onobulẹ/onokẹlẹ are given. Aunt, grandchild chain and younger brother are not told; 'younger brother: abíne kept by one annotator, agíní by another' sits in the open-questions row only.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_lex_008 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Ìnya mì, má dù páswọ̀dù wẹ̀ ñwù ẹ́nẹ k'ẹ́nẹ tótó n.` -> speaker `Yaya mi Ẹ kì du paswọdú wẹ (a5 edit); Anti ẹ ki d'igede kuma dubi nwu ẹnẹdu'n (a3 gold)`. "tags invented, grammar (a7); invented (a6); a5 reason: Yaya - Aunty"
  - ig_v42_lex_011 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Abíne mi, eyi chi ejodudu wẹ.` -> speaker `Agíní`. "Abíne -wrong // Agíní - right (a7 kept Abíne and wrote 'Abíne mi ọnẹkẹlẹ')"
  - ig_v42_lex_003 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Íiye, [child] ọmaye mi á kọchẹ biology` -> speaker `Íiye, [child] [child] wẹ á kọchẹ biology`. "[child] [child] wẹ - your grand child"
  - ig_v42_lex_003 [annotator_3] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `[mother], mà gbà [child]-[child] wẹ̀ tí [house]-ùkọ̀chẹ ẹ́kpa.` -> speaker `[mother], mà mi [child] wẹ̀ gba tí [house]-ùkọ̀chẹ ẹ́kpa.`. "The words were misplaced"
  - ig_bank_lex_014 [annotator_8] (gold): model `(gold only)` -> speaker `achogba ẹnekẹlẹ (older) / okẹkẹlẹ (younger); a3: Ọmaye ọnẹkẹlẹ kekele / lile; a6, a7: Ọmaye ẹnẹkẹlẹ imọtọ / ogijo; a5: achọgba mi ọnẹkẹlẹ / okekele mi ọnẹkẹlẹ`. "three systems for older/younger brother across six annotators"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: One spoken sentence to a specified relative where the relationship itself is the content (telling grandmother about her grandson, telling aunt about her nephew). Ask the speaker to begin the sentence with the word they call that relative. Decisive share was 0.75 on lex_003 and lex_008; edits carried the kin forms.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Tell your mother's younger sister in Igala that her son passed his exam, starting with the word you call her.
  - Your father's older brother is visiting. Tell your children in Igala who is coming, using the word you use for him.
  - Tell your grandmother in Igala that her granddaughter (your daughter) will visit her on Saturday.
  - Your wife's mother has arrived. In one Igala sentence tell your wife, starting with the word you call her mother.
  - Tell your younger brother in Igala to greet your older sister's husband properly when he arrives, using the kin words you really use for both.

### lexicon_disambig 7. Announcement frame: both arms open with u kà / u á kà ('I say / I will say') and three annotators independently replace it with the na tẹnẹ ku kà frame, then ka ki ni or kakini before the reported content.

- Rule a speaker would state: 'I want to tell you (that)' is na tẹnẹ ku kà ñwẹ (ka ki ni / kakini ...), never u kà ñwẹ or u á kà. Replacement type: native frame.
- Count: 13 judgments, 7 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Yes. Grammar row 'the speaker is na in these frames' (community_verified) and the 'polite volitive na tẹnẹ' row both give it. New prompts here test whether telling worked: any announcement to a listener should now open with na tẹnẹ.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_lex_009 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `[mother], u kà nw'ẹ, abọ asibiti a w'[goat]` -> speaker `[mother] [child], na tẹnẹ ku kà nw'ẹ, ka ki ni abọ asibiti a w'[goat]`. "Mother i want to tell you that Health workers are coming"
  - ig_v42_lex_009 [annotator_6] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `[mother], u kà nw'ẹ,` -> speaker `[mother], na tene ku ka ñwẹ kakiki,`. "It makes sense but need a little corrections to be well constructed"
  - ig_v42_lex_010 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `ọ́yà, u á kà ukọla òókáà ñwẹ.` -> speaker `ọ́yà, na tene ku kọla káá ñwẹ.`. "Ú á ká ukọla óókaa - wrong // Na tene kọlá káá - right"
  - ig_v42_lex_012 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Àmì Ígáláà, u kà k'ọla èjì.` -> speaker `Àmo mi Ígáláà, Mẹ na te [money] tane keke ọchu du [house] Ogwu.`. "I wrote the sentence well; a5 gold: Abo mi igala na tene ka'wu mẹ kakini"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Deliver news to a named listener (mother, wife, the village on radio), one or two sentences, where the content is simple so that the opener is the only contested part. lex_009 and lex_010 (0.6 decisive, 5 judgments each) produced the frame three times over.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Tell your mother in Igala that your sister has given birth to a boy this morning, as you would really say it to her.
  - On community radio, tell listeners in two Igala sentences that the market will move to the new site next month.
  - Tell your wife in Igala that your employer has transferred you to Lokoja and you will leave next week.
  - Announce in Igala to the people in the church hall that the meeting is postponed to Sunday evening.

### lexicon_disambig 8. Polysemy and calendar prompts where the model fabricates or collapses senses: 'time' (rag: Ẹgwa for occurrences, speaker: Íko for right moment and Ẹ ronu mẹta or Ùdẹ ẹgba nù mẹ́ta for three times), year/season (rag: Ọlọ́dṣi, Idṣégwẹ; speaker: Ọlọji, Afu ọwẹ), next/last year (Ọdọ kī yá/ojonwawn vs Ọdọ ki ya wa i; Ọdọ kī lē vs Ọdọ ẹchi), Sunday ([god] Sọnde/Sándè/abudo vs [god] aladi), one thousand naira (Náírà ọ̀kpa mẹ́lú, tọsin onka vs Nara íchámû nyú ọ̀gwáọ́kọ́). All five calendar prompts were judged both_inadequate by annotator_7 alone with no edit, so only her gold carries the forms.

- Rule a speaker would state: Sunday is [god] aladi (the week word); last year is ọdọ ẹchi; next year is ọdọ ki ya wa i; rainy season is ọlọji, dry season afu ọwẹ; a count of times uses ẹgba or ùdẹ with the numeral; 1000 is icham(u) nyu ọgwọkọ. Replacement types: all native except aladi (community loan) and Nara (borrowing).
- Count: 7 judgments, 1 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Partly. 1000 is in the vigesimal row (icham nyogwoko). Year, season, Sunday and next/last year are named as a vocabulary hole in the open-questions row only; nothing in the prompt text. ọkpa is banned. Prompts must collect these forms from more than one annotator.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_lex_025 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Ẹgwa - count of occurrences (ẹgwa ẹ́ta)` -> speaker `Íko - right moment to do something; Ẹ ronu mẹta - count of occurrences`. "Ẹgba - stretch of time; a8 gold: Ùdẹ ẹgba nù mẹ́ta"
  - ig_v42_lex_024 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `[god] Sọnde (ọ̀là kw'efu ichi English) // [god] abudo (ọ̀là Ígáláà)` -> speaker `Servici chi [god] aladi (a7 gold)`. "Both are in adequate"
  - ig_v42_lex_023 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Ọlọ́dṣi - rainy season // Idṣégwẹ - dry season` -> speaker `Ọlọji Raining Season // Afu ọwẹ: dry season/Harmatan (a7 gold)`. "Both are inadequate (tag invented on rag)"
  - ig_v42_lex_022 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Ọdọ kī yá. // Ọdọ kī lē.` -> speaker `Next year: Ọdọ ki ya wa i // Last year: Ọdọ ẹchi`. "Both are wrong"
  - ig_v42_lex_028 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Náírà ọ̀kpa mẹ́lú ... Tọ́sọ̀n ọ̀kà` -> speaker `Nara íchámû nyú ọ̀gwáọ́kọ́`. "Both are inaccurate"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Replace the bare 'give the word for X' with a dated spoken sentence that forces the word (a wedding next Sunday, a harvest last year), one target per prompt. The bare word-list shape got one judgment each, all both_inadequate, zero edits.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Tell your friend in Igala that your brother's wedding is next Sunday at the Catholic church, in the sentence you would really say.
  - Tell your neighbour in Igala that the rains came late last year but this year they came in April.
  - Say in Igala that you have called your son three times today and he has not picked up.
  - A trader asks two thousand five hundred naira for the yams. In Igala, offer her one thousand five hundred, saying the amounts the way market people say them.
  - Tell your mother in Igala that the harmattan will start next month and the children need sweaters.

### lexicon_disambig 9. Tone-mark tagging is noisy and out of step with speaker practice. The bare arm marks every syllable and draws 21 tone_marks tags; rag marks little and draws 9. But speaker gold almost never carries accents (52/248 rows, 47 of them from annotator_8), and many 'tone_marks' edit segments are in fact word replacements (eku to àgbà, t'òde kì tá to kẹ nya ti).

- Rule a speaker would state: Speakers do not require tone marks; they require the right word and dotted vowel. A tone_marks tag from annotator_3 or annotator_5 usually means 'wrong word or wrong spelling', not 'wrong tone'. Only annotator_8 writes tone consistently and her marks could serve as the reference when the write-up's 'always mark tone' rule is applied.
- Count: 22 judgments, 10 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Yes: 'no tone marks unless the question asks for them'. The write-up asks the opposite. Prompts should split the two: some ask explicitly for tone marks (then only annotator_8-style gold is comparable), most do not.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_lex_013 [annotator_3] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `t'òde kì tá` -> speaker `kẹ nya ti`. "segment tagged tone_marks, rationale: Wrong word"
  - ig_v42_lex_014 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `eku.` -> speaker `àgbà`. "segment tagged tone_marks, reason: àgbà - igala"
  - ig_v42_lex_005 [annotator_3] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Ónobirẹ·` -> speaker `Ónobulẹ·`. "Wrong spelling (segment tagged tone_marks)"
  - ig_v42_lex_008 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `duu ǹ.` -> speaker `duu ñ.`. "i just changed the tone mark (the change is grave-n to velar nasal ñ, i.e. the negative particle, not a tone)"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Two variants of the same sentence prompt, one ending 'write it with tone marks' and one without, assigned to different annotators, so the tag can be separated from lexical correction. Include a word pair that differs only by tone or by ẹ/ọ ([money] money vs [money] husband vs [money] canoe) so a true tone judgment is forced.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Write in Igala, with tone marks, the sentence 'my husband gave me money for the canoe', so that a reader can tell the three [money] words apart.
  - Tell your child in Igala that the moon is full tonight and the salt is finished, with every vowel written as you would in a careful text (dotted vowels where needed).
  - Write the Igala for 'he carried the child' and 'he killed the goat' with tone marks, and say in English which syllables change tone.

**Shapes that produced decisive, explained judgments**

- Spoken sentence to a named relative that names three or four concrete modern objects (packing list, market errand, clinic visit).: Decisive share 0.75 on ig_v42_lex_014 and 0.5 to 0.6 on lex_006, _009, _010; every item drew a separate correction with a reason naming the word (bokẹti, ìgèdè, aladi, ogwu, Batiri). The list isolates each loan. (ig_v42_lex_014, ig_v42_lex_009, ig_v42_lex_006, ig_v42_lex_010)
- Two-slot deictic or body-side contrast where one slot is the risky word (this one / the other one; left hand / right hand; the south).: Produced the only 'X wrong // Y right' explanations in the slice (a5 on lex_016 and lex_018) and edits that replaced exactly the invented form (onka to oka/ẹkẹji, àwéhì to awohì, Ọ̀wọ̀čo deleted). (ig_v42_lex_016, ig_v42_lex_018, ig_v42_lex_017)
- One Igala sentence containing a borrowed academic noun plus a required English line on how the noun was written.: The English line made the model commit (transliterate vs keep) and made the speaker commit too: 'They is no s in Igala Alphabet', 'Ecnomics is English not igala', and annotator_8's gold keeping 'psychology'. Decisive share 0.75 on lex_003 and lex_004. (ig_v42_lex_005, ig_v42_lex_027, ig_v42_lex_004, ig_v42_lex_003)
- Deliver simple news to a listener (mother, wife, radio audience).: Content is easy so the opener and the loan nouns are the only contested parts; three annotators independently supplied the na tẹnẹ frame on lex_009/lex_010. (ig_v42_lex_009, ig_v42_lex_010, ig_v42_lex_012)

**Shapes that produced ties and "both inadequate"**

- Bare 'give the Igala word/expression for X' on calendar or abstract items (north, next year, season, Sunday, 1000 naira), one judgment each.: All five were judged both_inadequate by annotator_7 alone with 'Both are wrong/inadequate' and no edit; decisive share 0; the only usable output is her gold. The shape also lets the bare arm write Yoruba (Áríwa) and rag invent (Ọ̀gẹ́bẹ̀, Ọlọ́dṣi). (ig_v42_lex_015, ig_v42_lex_022, ig_v42_lex_023, ig_v42_lex_024, ig_v42_lex_028)
- Explain a modern concept in one or two sentences (internet, health insurance).: Both_inadequate from a6 and a7 ('Both outputs is not making sense in Igala'); the edits rewrite the whole text so no single rule can be read off; gold answers are long paraphrases that differ wholesale (a7 vs a6 on lex_007). (ig_v42_lex_007, ig_v42_lex_012)
- Meta-question appended to the task ('say whether these are the same words you would use on a map', 'say whether the name is an Igala word or taken from English').: The model answers the meta-question in broken Igala (Ẹn, ma chi ọ̀là lẹ; ọ̀là Ígáláà ń) and that part draws the grammar tag, hiding whether the lexical part was right. lex_019 and lex_024 both_inadequate or split. (ig_v42_lex_019, ig_v42_lex_024, ig_v42_lex_018)
- Ask for all four cardinal directions, or all yam kinds, or three Idoma-confused words in one prompt (ig_bank_lex_028, _031, _033).: Gold lists are long and mutually incompatible (two direction systems, four thank-you words) with no explanation, so disagreement cannot be resolved; a5 and a7 even list Anya as both the Igala and the Idoma word. (ig_bank_lex_028, ig_bank_lex_031, ig_bank_lex_033, ig_bank_lex_018)
- Any judgment by annotator_8.: 16 of 16 verdicts are ties with no tags; her explanations instead paste a full gold sentence ('In Igala the word for ... is ...'). Her comparisons teach nothing, her gold is the richest tone-marked data in the slice. Route her to gold-only tasks or ask for a forced choice. (ig_v42_lex_014, ig_v42_lex_018, ig_v42_lex_020)

**What the speakers' own writing shows**

- tone marks: Rare. 52/248 gold rows carry an acute or grave, 47 of them from annotator_8; annotator_3 and annotator_4 never mark tone, annotator_5/6/7 did so in 1 or 2 rows. In edits speakers keep the model's accents where present but never add any, and strip them when deleting words. The write-up's 'tone marks always' is a linguist's norm, not the annotators' practice. Dotted vowels are the real marker: 175/248 rows use ẹ or ọ, though annotator_3 often omits them (Ogijo, Ononojo, Omu, Ochu where a4/a7 write Ọgijọ, Ọnọnọjọ, Ọmu, Ọchu).
- contractions: Mixed and the write-up's 'expanded forms' rule is not followed. Gold contracts freely (I ku'kọla ka, E'la, me'ju, d'igede, ch'ugbo, k'ọla, am'[friend], w'ẹ). In edits a5, a7 and a6 removed 17 apostrophes net (k'u á to unẹkẹ a, ñw'ẹnẹ to wẹ ẹnẹ, nw'ẹ to ñwẹ, tọd'uny'ogwu rewritten) while a3 added them (t'ugbó, dọ'tákàdá, di'wọ). The negative nasal is written ñ, n, or -n (du-n, ẹnẹdu'n).
- ki usage: Speakers write an unmarked ki as relativizer and as complementizer alike ([child] ki ọnẹ bi, ẹnẹ ki che le'gwu, Ọdọ ki ya wa i; kakini/kakiki or ka ki ni for 'said that'), ku or kuma before plural ma ([child] ku ma bi, ku ma du ogwu; a5 twice corrected the model's kí to ku: 'Ku - igala'), and kẹ as the verb or clause linker ('kẹ tu gbo', 'kẹ dọwọ wẹ', 'kẹ fì jẹ́nẹ́rétọ̀'). Only annotator_8 marks the pair kì/kí (kì [child] wa kì anyị kpaí kì ya gba nẹ as REL; kí kọ́, aladí kí dé ì). The model's over-use of a complementizer frame shows in rag's 'kì [name]-wn chi economics' (deleted by a5) and bare's kí before plural ma.
- dialect: 176/248 gold rows have no dialect; general_idah 46 (a5, a7, a6, and a8 on v42 prompts), ankpa 21 (a8 on ig_bank prompts only), other 3 (a3), ogugu 2 (a5 on lex_031 only, elsewhere general_idah). Self-declaration is inconsistent within annotators. Dialect-tied forms: a8/a4 ọpata atẹ (north) and ọpata ọganẹ (south), [sun] [morning]/[sun] anẹ vs a3/a5/a6/a7 opata (south), ojukpale/ukpale (north), apa or apo (east), ichi (west); a8 bare verbs hì (cook), là/tà (buy/sell), Dù mẹ́ (lend) vs a3/a4/a5 E'yi/Ẹhi, E'la/Ẹla, E mẹ. The model's onobirẹ/onokẹrẹ (r for l) is rejected by Idah and Ankpa speakers, matching the write-up's note that r-forms belong to Ogwugwu only.
- other: Loanwords are accepted as 'true Igala' by the speakers themselves: all gold for ig_lex_001 'vehicle' is Imọtọ/I'moto (a3, a4, a5, a6), with a5 also offering the native 'Abẹrẹ mẹlẹ'. Speakers write the English word unchanged when the prompt did (a7 kept biology, a8 kept psychology), yet respell when they think of it as Igala (ẹkẹnọmiks, paswọdú, ágriki, achibiti, Batiri, bokẹti, jẹ́nẹ́rétọ̀). The announcement frame na tẹnẹ ku kà, the greeting Nago, and the vocatives [money] mi, ọ́yà, [mother], Yaya mi, Anti recur across annotators.

**Open questions (speakers disagree)**

- Thank you: Nago (annotator_5, annotator_6 gold on ig_bank_lex_018) vs Anya (annotator_3 gold) vs Agba or Awa (annotator_4, annotator_7, annotator_8). annotator_3 and annotator_7 then list Anya as an IDOMA word on ig_bank_lex_031 ('Anya, it's an Idomo word that means weldone', a7), contradicting a3's own gold; the v4.4 grammar row says 'Plain thanks is anya'. Needs a forced-choice prompt per variety.
- South and north: opata = south for a3, a5, a6, a7 (a5 edit 'anẹ -> apata, reason South'); a4 and a8 (ankpa) use ọpata atẹ = north and ọpata ọganẹ = south. On ig_v42_lex_026 a7 rendered 'south of Idah' as [hand] Awoto baki ojukpale baki ukpale', using the word she gives for NORTH on lex_015. Either a slip or 'ojukpale' means something else in Idah usage.
- East: Apo (a3 gold lex_028 and lex_021 'Apo kogi state', a6) vs Apa (a5, a7) vs [sun] [morning] (a4, a6, a7, a8). Likely one word with a vowel difference plus a descriptive phrase; unverified.
- Loan spelling policy: respell with Igala letters (a5: ẹkẹnọmiks, paswọdú, ágriki, yuñivasítí; a7: cháyíkọ́lọ́jì) vs keep English letters (a8 gold psychology, éconómìkì; a7 kept biology; rag arm) vs native replacement (a6 igede for password, a5 chegbulu Atẹ for university, a5 ukọlọ ọlá for operation). The write-up says foreign words keep s; a7 says Igala has no s. For the name Grace, a8 preferred Gírésì and called 'Grace' English-mixed while a5 tagged Gírésì wrong_word.
- Padlock: igede (a7, a8, grammar row) vs àgbà (a5 edit on lex_014); igede is also what a6 and a3 use for 'password'.
- Younger brother: Abíne (rag; kept by a7 as 'Abíne mi ọnẹkẹlẹ') vs Agíní (a5: 'Abíne -wrong // Agíní - right') vs ọmaye (a8 gold lex_013). Older/younger brother across ig_bank_lex_014: imọtọ/ogijo (a6, a7), kekele/lile (a3), achogba/okẹkẹlẹ (a5, a8).
- Aunt as address: Yaya mi (a5) vs Anti (a3 gold) ; Ìnya mì (bare arm) was tagged invented by a6 and a7.
- Hand vs arm (ig_bank_lex_013): a4, a5 say one word [hand]; a6 [child]-[hand] for arm; a8 akẹkẹnẹ [hand] for arm while a7 glosses akekene [hand] as wrist.
- Borrow vs lend (ig_bank_lex_030): a5 and a7 say one word ẹmẹ; a3 E bi / E mẹ; a8 Emẹ / Dù mẹ́; a6 Emẹ kw'ugbo onẹ / Emẹ ñw'onẹ.
- Story vs news (ig_bank_lex_026): ọhiala covers both (a4, a7) vs Efoji/Inabali (a3), Ita/Inabali (a6), Alọ/Inabali (a5), Ohíala/Ukomu-ola (a8).
- Daytime (ig_bank_lex_015): Iyaja (a3) vs [god] ñwa (a4, a5, a7) vs Ọrọka (a6); a8 glosses [god] káà as day and [god] ñwa as 24 hours, the reverse of a4/a5/a7.
- Cook and boil (ig_bank_lex_022): E'yi/ẹyi (a5, a6, a7) vs Ẹhi (a4) vs hì (a8 ankpa); boil: ẹna (a4, a5, a7) vs Du kpuna (a6) vs Erọ (a3) vs hì [water] (a8).
- Vaccine: a5 edits to 'di ogwu' (give medicine) on both arms; a7 and a6 keep 'du ogwu'; di vs du is already an open question in the grammar rows and recurs here (a3 'di'wọ').
- 'Outside': t'òda (a5), t'[name] (a7), ọdoda (a8) against the model's t'òde; three spellings from three annotators for the same word.

## Orthography (50 judgments, 25 edits, 190 gold)

Data read: Read in full with the Read tool: review-split/comparisons-orthography.json (50 rows), edits-orthography.json (25 rows), gold-orthography.json (190 rows, two pages), prompts-orthography.json (52 rows), prompt-v4-4.txt, writeup-oct8.txt; grammar-rows-oct8.json (30 rows, the orthography, tone, numbers, Ejeba sounds and corrected-words rows read in full); the 14 orthography rows of prompt-informativeness.json. Counts were produced with a python tally over the same JSON (winners per arm, tags per arm, tone accents / dotted vowels / apostrophes / ñ / dialect per annotator in gold). Note: the comparisons file holds only the v4.2 prompts; the 6 comparisons per ig_bank_orth prompt and the 20 on ig_orth_001 listed in prompts-orthography.json are between older arms and were not in my slice, so the ig_bank_orth evidence below is gold only.

### orthography 1. Meta sentence instead of the text itself, built from two words nobody uses. The retrieval arm wraps every name in a sentence about the article: 'kwotejugede efu utokown Ígáláà' (appears in its answers to orth_001, 004, 005, 007, 008) and closes with a dangling 'ǹ'. Speakers delete the whole wrapper and keep only the name. This is the single biggest reason the retrieval arm loses in this bucket: of its 14 wins, none came on a prompt where it used this wrapper against a bare answer that gave the name plainly.

- Rule a speaker would state: When asked how a name should appear, write the name (or the sentence that carries it), never a sentence that says the name is written in the article. utokown and kwotejugede are not Igala words.
- Count: 22 judgments, 6 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Yes, twice: rule 7 (write the text itself, never a sentence about the article) and the NEVER WRITE list names utokown and kwotejugede explicitly. The next bank must test whether telling worked: the rag component is the source of these words, so v4.4 with retrieval is the arm at risk.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_orth_001 [annotator_4] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `[name] [goat] lẹ kwotejugede Lagos efu utokown Ígáláà. U é-kà ki u dabi ẹñwu du ǹ.` -> speaker `Lagos`. "edit rationale: We just write it as Lagos"
  - ig_v42_orth_005 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `[name] ìchèkpúlù kì kwotejugede efu utokown lẹ chi Green Valley International School.` -> speaker `[name] ìchèkpúlù lẹ chi Green Valley International School. U á kà [name] lẹ alu enefu.`. "edit segment ' kì kwotejugede efu utokown ' deleted, tagged grammar"
  - ig_v42_orth_008 [annotator_6] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Ma kwotejugede efu utokown Ígáláà. U é-kà kì u kọ ma [hand] ọmune kwí English ǹ.` -> speaker `Ma kọ efu ọtakada Wikipedia [road] ojoji-n todu ki ch'[name] efewo i che.`. "kwotejugede replaced by kọ, tagged grammar; rationale: It's not well constructed"
  - ig_v42_orth_004 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Zainab, Victor kpai Quadri kwotejugede efu utokown Ígáláà tọdu abọ ẹ́ta.` -> speaker `Zainab, Victor, kpaí Quadri. (the bare answer, chosen)`. "rag answer tagged wrong_word; bare answer won 3 of 4 judgments"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Ask for a sentence that carries the name and one real fact (born in, opened in, attended), so that an answer which only talks about the article is visibly off-task, and ask for the name alone in a second variant. Decisive on orth_003 (5 of 6) and orth_010 (3 of 4), which used exactly this shape; near-useless on orth_001 and orth_002 (1 of 4 and 2 of 5 decisive) which asked 'say whether you changed anything', an invitation to meta-talk from both arms.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - An Igala Wikipedia article says that the singer Teni was born in Lagos in 1992. Write that one sentence in Igala, spelling her name and the city as they should appear.
  - Write the first sentence of an Igala Wikipedia article about Dangote Cement: say what the company makes and where its head office is.
  - A notice in Idah says a new branch of First Bank opens on Monday. Write the notice headline in Igala, no more than eight words.
  - Write the caption for a photo in an Igala article: 'The [father] of Igala at the Ocho festival, Idah, 2019.'
  - Write one Igala sentence saying that Kogi State University is in Anyigba.

### orthography 2. Names respelled with Igala letters or marks: ñ inserted before k in Ankpa, the final h of Idah dropped, tone marks put on Idah and Osun. Both arms do it (rag 'Ida kpai Añkpa', bare 'Ìdá, Añkpa', bare 'Ọsun ... lẹ́tà ejodudu ñw chí Ọ'). Four of five annotators on orth_002 restore Idah and plain n; the tone_marks tag is used by speakers for these spelling corrections, not for tone.

- Rule a speaker would state: A place or person name keeps the letters it is written with in Nigeria: Idah with h, Ankpa with n, Osun with o; no tone marks and no dotted vowels on a name.
- Count: 10 judgments, 5 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Yes, rule 9 (a name keeps the exact letters the question gives it, never respell one). Test whether telling worked, and widen it: the prompt says letters but not accents or ñ, and the model's error here was adding marks, not changing letters.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_orth_002 [annotator_6] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Ida kpai Añkpa` -> speaker `Idah kpai Ankpa`. "explanation: Output A is almost correct. It should be written as 'Idah kpai Ankpa' not Ida kpai Añkpa; edit segments tagged tone_marks with reason 'Spelling is wrong'"
  - ig_v42_orth_002 [annotator_3] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Añkpa` -> speaker `Ankpa`. "Just a normal n without tone marks"
  - ig_v42_orth_002 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Ìdá, Añkpa` -> speaker `Ìdáh, Añkpa`. "I just spelt it correctly (keeps the tone marks and the ñ, adds the h)"
  - ig_v42_orth_008 [annotator_4] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Bayelsa kpaí Ọsun` -> speaker `Bayelsa Kpai Osun`. "We write it as Bayelsa and Osun we dont change the name name of a state when writing them on Igala Wikipedia"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Give names whose spelling tempts an Igala respelling (nk, s, h, final consonant, English vowels) inside a short factual sentence, and ask separately for one word the speaker WOULD respell (an old loan like school) so the boundary between name and loanword is drawn by the speaker.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Write one Igala sentence saying that Dekina, Ankpa and Idah are three towns in Kogi State.
  - Write one Igala sentence saying that a woman named Blessing Onuh moved from Lokoja to Abuja.
  - Write one Igala sentence saying that the river Niger meets the river Benue at Lokoja.
  - Write an Igala sentence saying the [father] of Igala lives in Idah. Spell the title and the town as you would in a newspaper.
  - Write in Igala: 'Musa went to school in Ankpa.' Spell 'school' the way Igala speakers write the word, and leave Ankpa as it is.

### orthography 3. The English language is named with an invented respelling or left as the English word. The bare arm produced four different forms in four answers (Ínglíìshì, Enyigilishi, Enẹfí, Ẹ̀ngẹ́lísì); the rag arm wrote 'English'. Four annotators corrected to enẹfu / ẹnẹfu / enefu and three of them used the frame 'alu ẹnẹfu' (the English way of writing) against 'alu Igala'.

- Rule a speaker would state: The English language is enẹfu (also spelled ẹnẹfu, enefu). 'In the English way' is alu enẹfu, 'in the Igala way' alu Igala. Never respell the word English.
- Count: 14 judgments, 6 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Not in the served prompt text; it is in grammar row 003f68fc (enẹfu, alu enẹfu). So v4.4 is told through the GRAMMAR block. Test whether that row is retrieved and obeyed.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_orth_007 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `íchí Enẹfí káká` -> speaker `ẹnẹfu`. "explanation: English in igala is ẹnẹfu (both_inadequate)"
  - ig_v42_orth_005 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `U á kà [name] lẹ kpai English.` -> speaker `U á kà [name] lẹ kpai Enẹfu.`. "edit reason: Enẹfu - English"
  - ig_v42_orth_001 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `àlù kù ma kọ́ ọ́ efu Ínglíìshì n̄` -> speaker `Lagos alu enẹfu a dabi kọ`. "I made the corrections so i can use the appriopriate words"
  - ig_v42_orth_005 [annotator_6] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `efu Enyigilishi, à tẹ ru Igala ñ` -> speaker `[name] [house]-ukọchẹ lẹ ki dẹ alu ẹnẹfu, a kọ alu Igala-n.`. "segment tagged invented"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Prompts where naming a language is unavoidable (a bilingual sign, a subject at school, a choice between languages) so the word for English, Igala, Hausa or Yoruba has to be written and the speaker's spelling of each is captured.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Write one Igala sentence saying that the sign at the clinic is written in English and in Igala.
  - A pupil's report card lists three subjects: English, Mathematics and Igala. Write the three subject names as they would appear on an Igala report card.
  - Write in Igala: 'My grandmother speaks Igala and Hausa but does not read English.'
  - Write one Igala sentence saying the radio programme is in Igala on Monday and in English on Tuesday.

### orthography 4. Tone marking at odds with the speakers in both directions. Where the prompt did not ask for tone, the bare arm marks nearly every syllable ([name] amí stétì Naíjíríyà méjì yí tẹ́fú ùkọ Ígálá') and speakers strip the marks in edits. Where the prompt asked for full tone marks (orth_011, 012), the rag arm gave none and still won 4 of 6 judgments because its words were nearer right; one annotator then added marks, another corrected the words and left them unmarked. In 190 gold answers only 85 carry any accent, and the two marked answers for 'morning' disagree with each other.

- Rule a speaker would state: Speakers' working rule: dotted vowels always, tone marks only when disambiguating or when asked, and when asked, mark the whole word. The linguists' rule (always mark) is not the speakers' practice; the v4.4 prompt's rule (never unless asked) matches the speakers but not the linguists.
- Count: 18 judgments, 9 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Partly, and in the opposite direction to the write-up: REGISTER says 'no tone marks unless the question asks for them'; ORTHOGRAPHY says 'mark tone as the dictionary and examples do'. The model is not told how to mark when asked (acute high, grave low, mid unmarked) nor that a name takes none. The prompt needs to settle the tone policy, and the bank must collect tone evidence from speakers in a form they will actually fill in.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_orth_008 [annotator_6] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `méèjì` -> speaker `meji`. "segment tagged tone_marks; rationale: It's not well constructed"
  - ig_v42_orth_007 [annotator_6] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `[name] takada lẹ chí` -> speaker `[name] ọtakada lẹ chi`. "chí -> chi tagged tone_marks"
  - ig_v42_orth_011 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Ogwu / Oguegwa / Ogwu mẹji` -> speaker `Ógwú / Ógwú-ẹ́gwā / Ọ̀gbọ̀méji`. "I made spelling corrections (adds tone to all three, macron for mid)"
  - ig_v42_orth_011 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Oguegwa / Ogwu mẹji` -> speaker `Ogwuẹgwa / Ọgbọ mẹji`. "I corrected the spelling (no tone marks added although the prompt asked for full tone marks)"
  - ig_orth_001 [annotator_3] (gold): model `n/a` -> speaker `[morning] (a3) vs [morning] (a6) vs [morning] (a3, a4, a5, a7, 11 answers)`. "15 gold answers for 'morning': 13 unmarked, the 2 marked ones carry opposite tones"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Minimal pairs and 'which syllable is high' questions are the only shapes where speakers marked tone deliberately and glossed it (ig_bank_orth_028/029 gold: [money] aeroplane vs [money] money; Òchù moon vs Óchú witch; Iló bell vs Ilò bitter leaf; [hand] hand vs [hand] broom; kọ́ build vs kọ̀ refuse; áwò comb vs áwó star). Bare 'write X with full tone marks' yields unmarked gold (13 of 15 on 'morning'). So: ask for a pair plus a gloss, or for a sentence where the unmarked spelling is ambiguous, and ask the speaker to say in English which word is which.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - The Igala word spelled [money] can mean farm and [money] can mean money. Write two short Igala sentences, one with each word, and mark the tone on both words so a reader cannot confuse them.
  - Write the Igala words for head and thief, which differ only in tone, each in a three-word sentence, with the tone marked on the word.
  - Write an Igala sentence meaning 'He refused to build the house', marking tone on the two verbs so that refuse and build are not confused.
  - Write the Igala word for moon and the word for witch, mark the tone on each, and say in English which syllable is high and which is low.
  - Write the Igala word for bell and the word for bitter leaf, with tone marks, and say which of the two you would still understand if the marks were missing.

### orthography 5. Numbers above twenty: the bare arm invents forms (ọgbọ́mẹ̀tā for 30, ogwéjì for 40, Ọgbọ́mẹ́gwá for 200); the rag arm fuses or mis-multiplies (Oguegwa for 30, Ogwu mẹji for 40, Ọgbọ ko for 200). Speakers agree on the arithmetic (30 = twenty + ten, 40 = ọgbọ meji, 200 = ọgwọkọ) but spell the words three ways (ogwu ẹgwa, Ogwuẹgwa, Ógwú-ẹ́gwā) and split on 100 (ógwúmẹlú vs ọgbọ mẹlu) and on 50 (ooje vs ọgbọ meji nyọ mẹgwa).

- Rule a speaker would state: 30 is ogwu ẹgwa (two words), 40 ọgbọ meji, 50 ooje, 100 ogwu mẹlu, 200 ọgwọkọ; the additive linker is ñyọ, never kpai; never fuse twenty and ten into one word.
- Count: 6 judgments, 4 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Yes, in grammar row f93cdd3b (every one of these forms, including 'never oguegwa' and 'ñyọ never kpai'). Test whether telling worked; the 100 and 50 splits are open and need more speakers.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_orth_011 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Oguegwa` -> speaker `Ogwu ẹgwa`. "Ogwu ẹgwa not oguegwa"
  - ig_v42_orth_012 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Ọgbọ mẹ́lu (one hundred) / Ọgbọ ko (two hundred)` -> speaker `Ógwúmẹ́lu / Ọ́gwọ́kọ́`. "Hundred in igala is Ógwúmẹlú; edit tags tone_marks on Ooje -> Óóje and wrong_word on Ọgbọ ko"
  - ig_v42_orth_012 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `50: Ọgbọ́mèjì ñyọ ẹ́gwá / 100: Ọgbọ́mẹ́lù / 200: Ọgbọ́mẹ́gwá` -> speaker `Ooje / Ọgbọ mẹ́lu / Ọ̀gwọ́kọ́`. "bare answer tagged cultural; a7 kept Ọgbọ mẹ́lu for 100 where a5 and a8 write ógwumẹlù"
  - ig_v42_orth_012 [annotator_8] (gold): model `n/a` -> speaker `Ọ́gbọmẹjì nyọ mẹgwa (50), ógwumẹlù (100), ọ́gwọkọ́ (200)`. "gold gloss: Fifty(50) Ọ́gbọmẹjì nyọ mẹgwa"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Numbers in a real frame (a price, an age, a year of birth, a phone number, a crowd size) written in words, so that spacing, the ñyọ linker and the vigesimal base are all exercised in one answer, with the digits given so the speaker's words can be checked against them.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - A yam seller in Idah market says a tuber costs 250 naira. Write what she says in Igala with the number in words.
  - Write in Igala: 'My grandfather is 73 years old and my grandmother is 68.' Write both numbers in words.
  - Write in Igala, numbers in words: 'The school has 120 pupils and 7 teachers.'
  - Read this phone number aloud in Igala and write what you say: 0803 455 2190.
  - Write in Igala: 'He was born in 1950 and died in 2021.' Keep the years as digits and write the rest in Igala.

### orthography 6. Translating or adapting a proper name into Igala. The rag arm turned Grace into Ufedenyo and Green Spring into Emimi Ójómi, and offered 'Eñwu é-wánẹ̀' for Things Fall Apart; the bare arm offered 'Amẹñwu kpaná'. Speakers kept Grace, Egbuson and Green Spring Montessori untouched (3 of 4 chose the bare arm; the one gold sentence keeps all three names). On the novel the speakers split: two say leave the title in English, two give an Igala rendering.

- Rule a speaker would state: A person's name and a school's name are never translated. A book or film title is left in its original language in an article; an Igala rendering, if given at all, goes in brackets after it.
- Count: 8 judgments, 4 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Yes for persons, places, schools (rule 9). Not for titles of works: the prompt says nothing about book, film or song titles, and the speakers themselves split (a4 wrote the title in Igala: 'Eñwu é-wánẹ̀ ki kpatuka'; a5 and a7 keep English). Collect evidence.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_orth_010 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Ómo nobír'òókáà k'[name]-wn ch'Ufedenyo Egbuson lẹ d'ìchèkpúlù k'[name]-wn ch'Emimi Ójómi Montessori lẹ.` -> speaker `[child]-obùle kí [name] ñwu chí Grace Egbuson lotí [house]-ùkọ́chẹ kù ma dọ́ kí Green Spring Montessori.`. "rag answer tagged wrong_language, wrong_word, tone_marks, grammar"
  - ig_v42_orth_010 [annotator_8] (gold): model `n/a` -> speaker `[child] ìgbẹ́le kì [name]-n chì Grace Egbuson ā nyì chẹ́gbulu ku ma dóọ̀ kì Green spring Montessori`. "gold gloss: a lady girl call Grace Egbuson attended a school call Green Spring Montessori"
  - ig_v42_orth_007 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Things Fall Apart (Eñwu é-wánẹ̀) kwotejugede efu utokown lẹ.` -> speaker `Things Fall Apart (Ame eñwu ki wánẹ̀)`. "Deleted what was not appriopraite (keeps the English title first, Igala in brackets)"
  - ig_v42_orth_007 [annotator_5] (gold): model `n/a` -> speaker `Ẹ'tọnọ alu ẹnẹfu`. "gold gloss: You will leave it in English; the same annotator's second gold 'Ẹ todu ọtakida lẹ nọ abẹlẹ' = you leave the name of the book like that"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Sentences carrying a person's name whose English meaning tempts translation (Grace, Blessing, Victory, Sunday) and a title of a work with a translatable meaning, in a context (article, poster, school notice) that fixes the register.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Write one Igala sentence saying that Blessing Sunday and Victory Ocheni passed their exams.
  - An Igala film poster announces a Nollywood film called 'The Wedding Party'. Write the poster's first line in Igala, and show how the film's title appears.
  - Write one Igala sentence saying that the hymn 'Amazing Grace' was sung at the funeral.
  - Write one Igala sentence saying the pupils read the book 'Purple Hibiscus' in class this term.

### orthography 7. Institution class word order: bare arm 'Banki Access' vs rag arm 'Access Bank'. Four annotators chose Banki Access (a3: 'Nothing is wrong', a4, a5, a7) and tagged Access Bank not_igala; one annotator (a6) chose Access Bank ('Output B is correct') and tagged Banki Access not_igala; a8 tied. The gold confirms the majority ('Banki Access', 'Ma fi banki Access bi efewo Ankpa') but a8's gold respells the whole name ('Amì ácẹssì bàkì').

- Rule a speaker would state: The class word leads as an Igala or loan word and the proper part follows unchanged: banki Access, chekpulu Green Valley. One annotator disputes this for a bank; the rule holds for school by 4 of 4 golds.
- Count: 12 judgments, 3 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Yes, rule 9 names 'Banki Access' as the pattern. Test whether telling generalises beyond bank (school, church, hospital, company, market, radio) and collect more judgments on the a6 dispute.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_orth_003 [annotator_3] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Banki Access` -> speaker `Ma fi banki Access bi efewo Ankpa (gold)`. "Nothing is wrong; Access Bank tagged not_igala"
  - ig_v42_orth_003 [annotator_6] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Access Bank` -> speaker `Access Bank`. "Output B is correct; Banki Access tagged not_igala"
  - ig_v42_orth_005 [annotator_4] (gold): model `n/a (bare: [house]-ukọchẹ Green Valley International School; rag: ìchèkpúlù)` -> speaker `Chegbulu Green Valley International (a4), Chegbulu green valley international (a5), Chekpulu green valley International (a3)`. "a4 edit rationale: I just went striaght to the point how its been called"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: The most decisive shape in the bucket (orth_003: 5 of 6 decisive). Repeat it with other institution types and a short notice frame so the class word and the name both have to be written.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - A new branch of Zenith Bank has opened in Idah. Write the one-line Igala notice announcing it.
  - Write the Igala name of the hospital called Grimard Catholic Hospital as it would appear on a signboard in Anyigba.
  - Write one Igala sentence saying that St Peter's Church in Ankpa is holding a harvest on Sunday.
  - Write the name of the company MTN Nigeria as it should appear in an Igala radio advert, in one sentence.
  - Write one Igala sentence saying that the market called Ega market in Idah is closed tomorrow.

### orthography 8. s and ṣ in Igala words, and the school loanword. The bare arm wrote the Yoruba 'ṣe ùṣe' for 'worked' and was corrected to 'chi ukọlọ' (ch for the s-sound, Igala verb). For 'school', speakers write the old loan with ch (Chekpulu a3, Chegbulu a4 a5, chẹ́gbulu a8, Ichegbulu a4 edit) while the rag arm wrote ìchèkpúlù and a8 once wrote 'ísakúlu' with s. Speakers disagree on kp vs gb inside the loan.

- Rule a speaker would state: Igala words have no s: where an English loan has become an Igala word (school) it is written with ch; a name keeps its s (Lagos, Osun, Bayelsa, Access). Open: chekpulu or chegbulu.
- Count: 9 judgments, 3 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Yes: the ORTHOGRAPHY letters list excludes s and ṣ for Igala words and NEVER WRITE bans ṣe/se; rule 9 keeps s in names. Not told: that an old loan like school is an Igala word (ch) while a brand or place is not. Collect the loan vs name boundary from speakers.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_orth_009 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `takí i wá ṣe ùṣe efu [goat] Bayelsa` -> speaker `takí i chi ukọlọ efu [goat] Bayelsa`. "I correted the spelling and word (segment tagged tone_marks)"
  - ig_v42_orth_005 [annotator_4] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `[house]-ukọchẹ Green Valley International School` -> speaker `Ichegbulu Green Valley International baki [house] Ukoche Green Valley International`. "edit: both forms offered, school loan written with gb"
  - ig_v42_orth_005 [annotator_8] (gold): model `n/a` -> speaker `Úkọchẹ ísakúlu ẹ́domẹgwẹ́ji kì ko Green Valley International School lẹ́`. "the one speaker form with s in a common noun"
  - ig_v42_orth_009 [annotator_8] (gold): model `n/a` -> speaker `efu [goat] lágóshì ubìla ì yà chukọlọ efu [goat] báyésha`. "a8 respells Lagos and Bayelsa with sh and tone, against a4, a6 and the write-up"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Sentences that contain one everyday loanword with s in English (school, bus, bicycle, soap, sugar, radio) and one proper name with s (Lagos, Osun, Musa), so the speaker writes both and the boundary shows in a single answer.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Write in Igala: 'Musa took the bus to school in Lagos.' Spell 'bus' and 'school' the way Igala speakers write them.
  - Write in Igala: 'She bought soap and sugar at Osun market.'
  - Write in Igala: 'The pastor from Bayelsa preached at the church in Idah.'
  - Write in Igala: 'My sister works for Access Bank in Lokoja.'

### orthography 9. The final nasal (negation, and the possessive 'its name') is spelled four ways across the two arms: rag writes a free-standing 'ǹ' at sentence end, bare writes 'n̄' and 'ñ', rag writes the possessive as [name]-wn'. Speakers write the negative as a hyphenated -n (a6: 'alu Igala-n', 'rida-n', 'ojoji-n') or as ñ, and the possessive as [name]-n' (a8) or [name] ñwu' (a5 edit), and nasal vowels with a tilde (a3 Ãnyà, a5 Õchẹ̀), with ñ (a6 Ẹñwu, Añọ) or with -wn (a8 Ẹwn, Efuwn, chẹwn).

- Rule a speaker would state: Not settled by the speakers. The model should never write a detached 'ǹ' or 'n̄' as a word; the negation attaches to the last word (-n or ñ). Nasal vowels: three speaker conventions coexist (tilde, ñ, wn).
- Count: 5 judgments, 3 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Partly: 'negation: ONLY a clause-final nasal, written ñ' and 'never end a word in -wñ'. The model still emits ǹ and n̄; the speakers do not write ñ for the negative either. Collect which spelling each speaker uses and whether the nasal attaches to the word.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_orth_006 [annotator_6] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `NYSC a kọ ọ. U n̄ kọ ẹkpa [name] ñwu n̄, todu amonẹ chakadu mọ ọ nyan̄ NYSC.` -> speaker `NYSC a kọ. U kpilọ [name] ñwu n̄, todu amonẹ chakadu mọ ru NYSC.`. "segments 'n̄ kọ ẹkpa' -> 'kpilọ' tagged grammar and 'ọ nyan̄' -> 'ru' tagged grammar, english_mixed"
  - ig_v42_orth_005 [annotator_6] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `à tẹ ru Igala ñ` -> speaker `a kọ alu Igala-n`. "negation written as hyphenated -n by the speaker"
  - ig_v42_orth_010 [annotator_8] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `k'[name]-wn ch'Ufedenyo` -> speaker `kì [name]-n chì Grace Egbuson (gold)`. "possessive 'its name' written [name]-n by a8, [name] ñwu by a5's edit of the bare arm"
  - ig_bank_orth_031 [annotator_8] (gold): model `n/a` -> speaker `Ẹwn, Efuwn and chẹwn (a8) vs Ẹñwu, Añọ, Ulañwu (a6) vs Ãnyà, Ẽbì (a3)`. "three nasal-vowel conventions on one prompt"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Short negative sentences paired with their positive, and 'its name is' sentences, so the nasal spelling is forced and comparable across annotators.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Write in Igala: 'He did not go to Idah. He went to Ankpa.'
  - Write in Igala: 'The town's name is Ajaka, not Ejule.'
  - Write in Igala: 'I do not know the name of the river.'
  - Write two Igala words that end in a nasal sound, each in a short sentence, and spell the ending the way you normally write it.

### orthography 10. Contracted vs expanded forms. The rag arm's orth_010 answer was contracted at every joint (nobír'òókáà k'[name]-wn ch'Ufedenyo ... d'ìchèkpúlù) and lost 3 of 4 with a grammar tag each time; the bare arm's unelided [house]-ùkọ́chẹ kù ma dọ́ kí' was kept. But the speakers themselves contract in their own gold: ẹ'jẹñwu, wọla'ule, Ẹ'gwugwa, A'jọfẹ, A'ñyini (a4, a7), W'ọlalẹ (a8), ch'[name] (a6 edit), while a3 and a5 write the same words spaced (Eì jẹñwu, E gwù gwà, Wọ lálẹ).

- Rule a speaker would state: Speakers tolerate one elision at a frozen joint (w'ọla, ẹ'jẹñwu) and reject a sentence where every word boundary is elided; the write-up asks for expanded forms throughout. The v4.4 prompt currently teaches the apostrophe as the default.
- Count: 4 judgments, 2 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Told the opposite of the write-up: 'Elision: vowel meets vowel across a word break -> drop the FIRST vowel, apostrophe at the joint'. The prompt also bans hyphenated prefixes (é-) which the rag arm still used ('é-wánẹ̀', 'é-kà'). Needs a decision between Salem and the speakers; the bank should collect both forms from the same speaker.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_orth_010 [annotator_3] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Ómo nobír'òókáà k'[name]-wn ch'Ufedenyo Egbuson lẹ d'ìchèkpúlù k'[name]-wn` -> speaker `[child]-obùle kí ọ́dọ́ ñwu chí Grace Egbuson lọ [house]-ùkọ́chẹ kù ma dọ́ kí (bare answer, chosen)`. "rag answer tagged grammar; a7 edit of the bare answer expands obùle to onobùle: 'Spelling correction'"
  - ig_bank_orth_016 [annotator_4] (gold): model `n/a` -> speaker `ẹ'jẹñwu (a4) vs Eì jẹñwu (a3) vs Ejẹnwù (a5) vs A jẹñwu (a7)`. "four spellings of 'to eat' across four annotators"
  - ig_bank_orth_017 [annotator_8] (gold): model `n/a` -> speaker `W'ọlalẹ (a8), Wọla'ulẹ (a7), wọla'ule (a4) vs Wọ lálẹ (a3), Ọlalẹ (a5, a6)`. "the same greeting elided at two different joints or not at all"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Ask for the same short sentence twice: as the speaker would write it in a text message, and as they would write it in a school textbook. The pair shows which joints they contract and which they consider full forms.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Write 'Welcome, come and eat' in Igala twice: once as you would type it to a friend, once as it would be printed in a primer.
  - Write in Igala, in full words with no apostrophes: 'The child's name is Ojochide.' Then write it again the way you would say it quickly.
  - Write in Igala: 'Good morning, how is the house?' first in full forms and then in the shortened everyday form.
  - Write the Igala for 'to greet' and 'to eat' as two separate words each (particle plus verb), then as one joined word if you ever write them joined.

**Shapes that produced decisive, explained judgments**

- Institution or person name inside a one-line notice or one-sentence biography with a real fact (opened in, born in, attended).: Decisive and explained: orth_003 5 of 6 decisive, orth_010 3 of 4, orth_009 2 of 3. Speakers wrote full gold sentences and word-level edits (lọ -> lotí, ṣe ùṣe -> chi ukọlọ, obùle -> onobùle). (ig_v42_orth_003, ig_v42_orth_010, ig_v42_orth_009)
- Minimal pair with an English gloss for each member.: The only shape where speakers mark tone deliberately and explain it ([money] aeroplane vs [money] money; Òchù moon vs Óchú witch; Iló bell vs Ilò bitter leaf; kọ́ build vs kọ̀ refuse). a6 even wrote 'First o high tone, Second o low tone'. Eight gold answers each on orth_028 and orth_029. (ig_bank_orth_028, ig_bank_orth_029)
- Numbers in words with the digits given.: Speakers correct both the arithmetic and the spelling and say why ('Ogwu ẹgwa not oguegwa', 'Hundred in igala is Ógwúmẹlú'); 2 of 3 decisive on both prompts despite only 3 judgments each. (ig_v42_orth_011, ig_v42_orth_012)
- A prompt that names a dialect-sensitive word (thank you) and lets the speaker list variants.: a7 and a5 volunteered dialect labels unprompted ('Agba Idah, Awa Ankpa, Añya Ogugu'), the only self-declared dialect data in the bucket besides the dialect field. (ig_bank_orth_033)

**Shapes that produced ties and "both inadequate"**

- 'Write the name as it should appear, and say whether you changed anything from the English spelling.': Both arms output the same unchanged name plus meta-talk, so speakers tie or state policy without an Igala form: orth_001 1 of 4 decisive (2 ties, 1 both_inadequate), orth_002 2 of 5 decisive. The explanation 'In Wikipedia we dont change the name of a place' is useful once, not twelve times. (ig_v42_orth_001, ig_v42_orth_002, ig_v42_orth_008)
- 'Write the Igala word for X with correct spelling and tone marks' (single word, no context).: Speakers give the bare word and ignore the tone request: 13 of 15 gold for 'morning' are unmarked, and the marked ones contradict each other; no sentence, no rule, no judgment to compare arms on. (ig_orth_001, ig_bank_orth_018, ig_bank_orth_020, ig_bank_orth_023)
- 'Write the single Igala word for hello.': Three gold answers say there is no such word (a4, a7 twice); the rest give unrelated hails (Agba oo, aidẹ, àgbá). The prompt presupposes a word that does not exist. (ig_bank_orth_039)
- Acronym prompt (NYSC) asking whether to expand it.: Nobody expands it; the one explained judgment is a gold sentence ([child] mi fi NYSC che kpa mẹ'), the other two are untagged wins. 1 both_inadequate of 3 with nothing learned about spelling. (ig_v42_orth_006)

**What the speakers' own writing shows**

- tone marks: 85 of 190 gold answers carry any tone accent: a3 20/32, a5 20/33, a8 23/40, but a4 8/30, a6 6/28, a7 8/26. Dotted vowels are near universal (ẹ, ọ in 117 of 190). Where tone is marked speakers disagree (morning: [morning] a3 vs [morning] a6; farm: [money] a3 vs [money] a5 vs [money] a4 a7 a8; elder: Ògìjò a3, Ógìjò a5, Ógijò a6, Ọgijọ a4 a7, Ogijo a8). Three annotators mark mid tone with a macron (a7 Ógwú-ẹ́gwā, ákpálā; a4 ábúkōdo; a5 [father]), against the 'mid unmarked' convention. In edits speakers strip model tones (méèjì -> meji, chí -> chi, ọ́dọ́ -> [name]) and use the tone_marks tag for spelling fixes (Idah, Ankpa, Óóje).
- contractions: 14 of 190 gold use an apostrophe (a4 5, a7 4, a8 4, a5 1, a3 0, a6 0): ẹ'jẹñwu, wọla'ule, Ẹ'gwugwa, A'jọfẹ, A'ñyini, W'ọlalẹ, Ẹ'tọnọ, ch'[name]. a3 and a5 write the same items spaced (Eì jẹñwu, E gwù gwà, Wọ lálẹ). Speakers reject the rag arm's wall-to-wall elision (orth_010) but accept one elision at a frozen joint. Hyphenated compounds appear in gold and edits ([child]-onobùle, [house]-ukọchẹ, Igala-n, [name]-n).
- ki usage: No speaker in this slice writes kí (high, COMP). The relativizer appears as unmarked ki (a4 'Eñwu é-wánẹ̀ ki kpatuka'; a7 'Ame eñwu ki wánẹ̀'; a6 'todu ki ch'[name] efewo i che', 'ku ma dọ ọtakada lẹ dẹ') or as low kì by a8 ('kì [name]-n chì Grace Egbuson ... ku ma dóọ̀ kì Green spring'). ku before plural ma appears in a6 and a8, matching the v4.4 rule. The rag arm's 'kì kwotejugede ... lẹ' relative was deleted, not re-marked.
- dialect: 165 of 190 gold leave the dialect field empty; 19 say general_idah (a4, a5, a6, a8), 6 say other (all a3). a3 shows Ogwugwu/Ogugu features: 'Eri' for song where a5 a6 a8 write Eli/Elì (the r/l alternation Salem describes), 'Ányà' for thank you which a7 labels Ogugu dialect; a3 also writes Chekpulu (kp) where a4 a5 a8 write Chegbulu (gb). a7 lists thank-you by variety: Agba (Idah), Awa (Ankpa), Añya (Ogugu).
- other: Names: Idah, [father], Onuh keep a final h in 4 annotators' writing (a3, a4, a6, a7) while the linguist-style [father]/[father] (a3, a5) drops it; the market day is written Afor/Áfọr/Àfọr with a final r by a3 a5 a7 a8 and afọ by a6. 'and' is kpai for a3 a5 a6 and a7, manyi once for a4 ('Zainab, Victor manyi Quadri'). English the language: enẹfu (a7, a5), ẹnẹfu (a5, a6, a4), enefu (a7). 'In the X way' = alu X (alu ẹnẹfu, alu Igala; a4, a6, a7). Nasal digraphs vary within the same word: Ejẹnwù (a5) vs Ejẹñwu (a6), E ñyòkò (a3) vs E nyòkò (a5), ẹñyọ (a5) vs Ẹnyọ (a3, a6, a4). Only a8 respells foreign names (lágóshì, báyésha, Víkítò, Kúádírì, Lagọs, ácẹssì); a4 and a6 state the opposite rule in words.

**Open questions (speakers disagree)**

- Tone policy: the write-up says always mark; the served v4.4 prompt says never unless asked; speakers mark 45 percent of the time and contradict each other on the marks ([morning] a3 vs [morning] a6 for morning; [money] a3 vs [money] a5 for farm). Decide whose rule the arm follows before scoring tone_marks, and note that speakers use that tag for spelling.
- Contraction policy: the write-up says expanded forms; v4.4 teaches apostrophe elision; a4, a7, a8 contract (ẹ'jẹñwu, W'ọlalẹ, A'jọfẹ), a3 and a5 do not (Eì jẹñwu, Wọ lálẹ, E gwù gwà).
- Name respelling: a8 alone respells (lágóshì, báyésha, Víkítò, Kúádírì, Lagọs, ácẹssì, Ída/Ánkpà) against a4 ('we dont change the name of a place'), a6 ('todu le ya rida-n'), a3, a5, a7. Is a8 reporting a spoken-form convention the others reject, or an error?
- Idah and Ankpa: Idah kpai Ankpa (a3, a4, a6) vs Ìdáh, Añkpa (a7, keeps ñ and tone) vs Ída, Ánkpà (a8). Does the h count as part of the name for every speaker?
- Banki Access (a3, a4, a5, a7) vs Access Bank (a6, explicit 'Output B is correct', tagged Banki Access not_igala). Does the class-word-first rule hold for a6 with other institutions?
- The school loan: Chekpulu (a3, rag ìchèkpúlù) vs Chegbulu (a4, a5, a8) vs Ichegbulu (a4 edit) vs ísakúlu with s (a8 gold). kp or gb, and is an initial i- part of the word?
- 100 = ógwúmẹlú (a5, a8) or ọgbọ mẹlu (a7 left it); 50 = ooje (a5, a7) or ọgbọ meji nyọ mẹgwa (a8); 30 spelled ogwu ẹgwa (a5 explanation), Ogwuẹgwa (a5 edit) or Ógwú-ẹ́gwā (a7).
- Final nasal spelling: model writes ǹ, n̄, ñ, -wn; speakers write -n (a6: Igala-n, rida-n, ojoji-n), [name]-n (a8), ñ. Nasal vowels: tilde (a3 Ãnyà, a5 Õchẹ̀), ñ (a6 Ẹñwu, Añọ), wn (a8 Ẹwn, Efuwn, chẹwn). Which is the written standard the arm should produce?
- Titles of works: a4 renders Things Fall Apart in Igala ('Eñwu é-wánẹ̀ ki kpatuka'), a7 keeps English with Igala in brackets, a5 says leave it in English ('Ẹ'tọnọ alu ẹnẹfu'); a6 sentence keeps the English title. The v4.4 prompt is silent on titles.
- 'and' between names: kpai (a3, a5, a6, both arms) vs manyi (a4 gold orth_004); a5 judged orth_008 both_inadequate with 'And in Igala is kpai' although both answers used kpai/kpaí, so the tone mark on kpaí may be what was rejected.
- Word-final consonants (Afor, Idah, [father], Onuh) are written by most speakers although the write-up calls VC endings ungrammatical outside Ibaji; is this names-and-loans only, and does the market day take final r or not (a6 afọ)?
- Dotted vowel on common words differs by speaker: [morning] (a3, a4, a5, a7) vs [morning] (a6, a8); Ẹli (a4, a7) vs Eli/Elì (a5, a6, a8); ẹkpọ (a4, a7) vs ekpo (a6, a8) vs Èkpò (a3) vs Egbo (a5). Which spellings are errors and which are variety?

## Dialectal fidelity (64 judgments, 3 edits, 62 gold)

Data read: Read in full with the Read/Bash tools: review-split/comparisons-dialectal_fidelity.json (64 rows, every row read, flattened to scratchpad/dial-comparisons-flat.txt), review-split/edits-dialectal_fidelity.json (3 edits: dial_010 annotator_7, dial_013 annotator_7, dial_002 annotator_3; edits-diffs.json confirms these are the only dial edits), review-split/gold-dialectal_fidelity.json (62 gold rows: annotator_8 22, annotator_6 19, annotator_7 16, annotator_3 3, annotator_5 2; dialect field 59 general_idah, 3 other), review-split/prompts-dialectal_fidelity.json (23 prompts), review-split/prompt-informativeness.json (18 dial prompts with judgments), prompt-v4-4.txt (served v4.4 system prompt), grammar-rows-oct8.json (30 rows), writeup-oct8.txt (Salem/Lydia), and the 22 dial rows of review-split/v44-vs-gold.json (v4.4's own answer to each dial prompt next to the gold).
