  - ig_v42_reg_017 [annotator_7] (v4.4): model `Agba, na tẹnẹ ku tẹn'ẹ ọla. Ọla abakwan'[house] wẹ k'ẹ kà lẹ, ẹ gba ku wa du t'efu Wikipedia Ígáláà?` -> speaker `Abọgìjo àgbá ì chẹ nì ọ́hila kẹ ká nwu ofìgbalì wa, kì che récọdu ē chẹ jẹ ká dù tefu afù ka amonẹ kì lì kpaì ku ma gbọò?`. "four apostrophes in v4.4 (tẹn'ẹ, abakwan'[house], k'ẹ, t'efu) against none in the speaker's 29 words"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Sentences that force 'in' or 'for' before a vowel-initial noun (efu [market], efu ọdọ, efu [house], ñwu ẹnẹ, ñwu amọma) and that contain ki followed by a (we). Ask for the sentence as typed; optionally ask for a second, fully expanded line. Speakers converge on these spellings so every v4.4 ef'/ñw'/k'a is a countable miss.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - In one Igala sentence, tell a friend that your mother is in the market and will come home in the evening.
  - Write in Igala: 'We did not know that the meeting was in the school.' Keep 'in the school' in the sentence.
  - In one Igala sentence, say that the elders have kept the money for the children of the compound.
  - Write in Igala, as you would type it: 'If we go to the farm in the morning, we will finish before the rain.'
  - A teacher wants a line written in full, with no apostrophes, so pupils can read every word. Write 'The yams are in the barn for the festival' in Igala that way, then as you would normally write it.

### v44-vs-gold 5. ki, kì and kí: one all-purpose grave kì, no complementiser, extra relative closures. v4.4 writes kì 75 times (as relativiser, prohibitive, optative and 'that' alike) and kí twice; speakers write kí 21 times (18 by annotator_8), ki 275, kì 83; for 'said that' speakers use kakini / ka ki ni (34) and v4.4 does too (30) but also uses kì for 'what X is'. v4.4 closes relative clauses with lẹ 28 times (0.52 per 100 words) against 15 in gold (0.17), and on the two relative-clause translations three of four speakers write no closure. Salem's write-up: COMP kí and REL kì are distinct words with a meaning difference (assertion vs fact) and Igala keeps an overt C position.

- Rule a speaker would state: After verbs of saying, hearing, knowing and in 'own up that', the clause opens with kakini or kí (complementiser); kì only introduces relative clauses and the prohibitive or optative; ku before plural ma. Do not add lẹ after every relative clause. Mark the ki tone only when the whole answer is marked.
- Count: 24 judgments, 23 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Partly: 'Relativizer kì (singular), ku before plural ma/me', 'lẹ also closes relative clauses (head + kì ... lẹ)', and 'kì/ki starts a new clause'. No complementiser rule; 'said that = kakini' is in grammar row 30. The lẹ-closure rule is told and over-applied against three of four speakers.
- Evidence:
  - ig_bank_gram_022 [annotator_6] (v4.4): model `Ujẹu ki [mother] mi hi lẹ ch'urinyọ.` -> speaker `[food] k'[mother] mi yi arinyọ (annotator_6), [food] ki [mother] mi hi ariyọ (annotator_5), [food] kì [mother] mì hyì à rìnyọ (annotator_3), [food] kì [mother] mì hì árìyo (annotator_8)`. "nine gold answers from six annotators; none closes the relative with lẹ and none writes ch'urinyọ"
  - ig_bank_gram_030 [annotator_6] (v4.4): model `Onokẹlẹ kì a lo t'[money] ñwu lẹ leku.` -> speaker `Ẹnẹkẹlẹ ka lo t'[money] ñwu lekwu (annotator_6), [father] kà lò tókó ñwù lèkwù (annotator_3), ẹnẹkẹlẹ kì l'uché ká nyì ugbo-wn lé,lẹkwu (annotator_8)`. "only annotator_8 closes with lé; annotators 6 and 3 write ka (ki + a) and no closure; v4.4 also misspells lekwu as leku"
  - ig_v42_auth_006 [annotator_6] (v4.4): model `Na tẹnẹ ku kà ñwu mẹ ẹñwu kì kọmputa chẹ.` -> speaker `Kọmputa chi ẹñwu ki adu kọ ẹñwu tanẹ ki amọnẹ ki gba.`. "annotator_6's edit of the v3 answer drops the announcement frame and the 'what X is' clause; v4.4 marks kì (relative) on a complement"
  - ig_bank_gram_032 [annotator_6] (v4.4): model `Ma ka ñwu ami imọtọ kakini ma chẹ du ọdun lẹ t'[god] ọmune.` -> speaker `Ma ka ñwu abimọtọ lẹ kakini ma m'[god] icholo lẹ du tọgba (annotator_6), Mà ká ñwù ámì ímọtọ ká kíní má mì ìchòlò dù nyọgbà (annotator_3), Ma chẹ dù tì abìmọto lẹ ẹtì kì Ichọlọ-lẹ mu chì-tóò (annotator_8)`. "kakini is the speakers' complementiser for reported speech; v4.4 gets it here but writes ọdun (Yoruba 'festival') for icholo"
  - ig_v42_gram_036 [annotator_8] (v4.4): model `Idah chi [goat] ka ki dẹ efu Kogi State.` -> speaker `Idah chì [goat] kí dì [head]-ane státì kogì`. "annotator_8 writes kí on what is syntactically a relative, so the kí/kì split Salem describes is not what this speaker's spelling encodes; this needs a minimal pair, not a translation"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Minimal pairs in one prompt: 'I heard that the man came' versus 'the man who came'; 'own up that you are wrong' as a demand versus as a known fact; a relative clause whose head is plural (ku ma). Ask the speaker for both sentences plus a one-line gloss of the difference. Also a judged pair where the only difference is the closing lẹ.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Write two Igala sentences: 'I heard that Paul has died' and 'the Paul who has died was my teacher'. Then say in one line whether the small word after 'heard' and the small word after 'Paul' are the same word.
  - Your brother denies breaking the pot, but you saw him. Write in Igala: 'Own up that you broke the pot.' Then write how you would say it if you only suspected him.
  - Translate into Igala: 'The women who sell fish at the river have gone home.' Write it once, then say whether anything must come at the end of 'who sell fish at the river'.
  - Write in Igala: 'The child is happy that his father came' and 'The child who came is happy.'
  - In one Igala sentence, report what the chief said: that the market will not hold on Friday.

### v44-vs-gold 6. The formal announcement frame 'na tẹnẹ ku kà ñw'ẹ ka ki ni ...' applied to intimate and casual speech. v4.4 opens 12 answers with na tẹnẹ (telling one's mother the school subjects, telling a grandmother about biology, telling a husband a packing list, a friend about market day, a street greeting 'My guy', one's own grandmother's age); speakers use it in 2 of 636 gold answers, both on-air announcements (ig_v42_auth_003 annotator_7, ig_v42_lex_012 annotator_5). Grammar rows 1 and 19 teach the frame as 'the backbone of formal speech' and the model treats it as the default opener.

- Rule a speaker would state: na tẹnẹ ... ka ki ni opens public announcements and formal requests to elders or a gathering. To a parent, spouse, sibling, friend or child the answer starts with the content (a vocative at most). The frame marks register, so its presence in a casual line is a register error, not politeness.
- Count: 0 judgments, 11 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: The frame is served in grammar rows 1 and 19 ('This frame is the backbone of formal speech') with no restriction on addressee; the overuse is the rows' side effect. The REGISTER line asks for first and second person community speech, which the frame contradicts in intimate scenes.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_lex_002 [annotator_8] (v4.4): model `[mother], na tẹnẹ ku kà ñw'ẹ ka ki ni ma kọ [child] mi mathematics, enẹfu, social studies, kpai agricultural science efu ìchèkpúlù eñíni.` -> speaker `Ukocha Imatimatiki, ukocha ìgìlìshì,Ùkọ̀chẹ́ amẹ̀nwú kpaí ọ̀da [house] kpaí ányí,Ùkọ̀chẹ́ [money] kpaí amẹla`. "speaking to one's own mother, annotator_8 lists the subjects with no frame"
  - ig_v42_gram_015 [annotator_8] (v4.4): model `Na tẹnẹ ku kà ñwu mẹ ka ki ni ọdọ onobulẹ ogijo ef'inyi wa chi ọgbọ mẹ́lu.` -> speaker `[mother] kì chì ogìjo alu olókó ì chì Ógwúmẹ́lū`. "family gathering: annotator_8 names the woman and her age in nine words"
  - ig_bank_dial_010 [annotator_6] (v4.4): model `My guy, na tẹnẹ ku kà ukọla ñwẹ today.` -> speaker `Uche like afẹ wẹ (annotator_6), Guy [water] alọ tì school ugbata (annotator_8), Lia gwanẹ [head] chair (annotator_5)`. "annotator_7 judging the v3 pair: 'Guy i am going to school now, but I don't have transport; we both have gist later'; the urban mix never carries the formal frame"
  - ig_bank_auth_031 [annotator_6] (v4.4): model `Ónuku mi, na tẹnẹ ku kà ñwẹ ka ki ni amọnẹ wewe ef'[market] eñini, [child] kpa mi.` -> speaker `[market] wa chẹ kọ tabale ñini-i, amonẹ ch'ọbata lile ku ma neke dufu kwpefu [market].`. "annotator_6's colloquial recount starts with the market, not with an announcement"
  - ig_v42_auth_003 [annotator_7] (v4.4): model `Amonẹ wa, ẹ gbañọ. [house] ukọchẹ Kogi State Polytechnic a dabi chi aladi ki ya wa.` -> speaker `Mẹ gba chakadu na tẹnẹ ku ka ñwu mẹ ka ki ni [house] ukochẹ Kogi Polytechnic a nwa bi Aladi ki ya wa`. "the one place the frame belongs (radio) is the one place v4.4 left it out"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: One message, five addressees: the same fact told on the radio, to the council of elders, to your mother, to a friend of your age, to your small child. Each addressee is a separate prompt so the speaker writes five lines; the frame should appear in at most the first two. This also feeds grammar row 28 (vocative plus mẹ for elders).
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - The borehole in your village will be repaired on Thursday. Say it in Igala as the announcer on Radio Kogi would read it.
  - The borehole will be repaired on Thursday. Say it in Igala to the council of elders at their meeting, as you would stand and say it.
  - The borehole will be repaired on Thursday. Say it in Igala to your mother as you come in from the road.
  - The borehole will be repaired on Thursday. Say it in Igala to a friend of your own age you meet at the stream.
  - The borehole will be repaired on Thursday. Tell your six-year-old in Igala, so she stops asking.

### v44-vs-gold 7. Expository template prose: 'X chi ẹñwu ọgbọgaga ọjanẹ Ígáláà' opener (ọgbọgaga 27 times in v4.4, 4 in gold), tọdu five times the speakers' rate (58 vs 20), five or six short lines, English glosses in brackets where not asked, and a closing Àmí (4 vs 0). v4.4 is more than twice as long as any gold in 20 rows and writes 2.37 sentences per answer against the speakers' 1.58; speakers write one comma-chained sentence (median 7 words, mean 10.6) linked by ki, ku ma, kẹ, manyi. The METHOD line 'one thought per sentence, short clauses' and the REGISTER '~7-word sentences' produce lists where the community writes a single run-on.

- Rule a speaker would state: An expository answer is one or two sentences in the speaker's own voice; no 'is an important thing in Igala land' opener; no Amen outside a prayer; no bracketed English unless the prompt asks. Chain clauses with ki, ku ma, kẹ and manyi as speakers do rather than cutting every clause into a new sentence.
- Count: 19 judgments, 0 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: The length and sentence-cutting are prompted: METHOD 2 'one thought per sentence, short clauses' and REGISTER '~7-word sentences'. The 'important thing' opener and Àmí are not told anywhere; 'No preamble, no meta-commentary, no translation unless asked' is told and the bracketed glosses break it.
- Evidence:
  - ig_bank_cult_002 [annotator_7] (v4.4): model `Ọ́kà okilì chi ùjẹu ọgbọgaga ọjanẹ Ígáláà. Abọ Ígáláà a du ọ́kà okilì ch'ọjẹ ñwi ọnọnọjọ kpai ẹgba ichọlọ. Ọdọ du, ma a chi ichọlọ iyalọ ta ku ma chanẹ jẹ ọ́kà okilì ẹtitọ.` -> speaker `[yam] anẹ Igala chi ẹñwu ku ma jẹ i che ọfọfo`. "annotator_7 and annotator_8 gloss: 'Yam doesn't hold cultural importance to the Igala culture as it holds to the Igbo culture'; v4.4 asserts the opposite in 43 words and calls yam ọ́kà okilì instead of [yam]"
  - ig_bank_cult_025 [annotator_7] (v4.4): model `Úkọ́lọ́ achọchọ chi úkọ́lọ́ ọgbọgaga ọjanẹ Ígáláà. Ami ọnọbulẹ a chọ ukpe ku ma dọ Achi. ... Úkọ́lọ́ lẹ a jẹ ñwu ma nẹ [money] ku ma du deju ti akwọra [house] ma.` -> speaker `Abọ Igala a di amakpa chi amọ, ukpa ucha, manyi ugba`. "47 words against 11; the speaker names the craft (pottery) in one sentence"
  - ig_bank_auth_033 [annotator_7] (v4.4): model `[god] ki d'ẹnyọ ñwu [house] etito yi. / [god] ki du urẹdọ kpai ẹgbiti ñwu mẹ efu [house] yi. / Mẹ a bi [child] wẹwẹ efu [house] yi kpai uyo. / [god] ki ch'[hand] mẹ, i la k'[child] mẹ. / Àmí.` -> speaker `Wọla ọjilẹ [house] ẹtito (annotator_7), [god] ki du ẹnyọ ñwu [house] titọ ki dẹ-i (annotator_6)`. "an elder's house blessing is one line for two speakers; v4.4 adds four lines and Amen"
  - ig_v42_lex_025 [annotator_8] (v4.4): model `Ẹgba chi ọla ñwu stretch of time. / Ẹgba chi ọla ñwu right moment. / Ẹgwa chi ọla ñwu count of occurrences (alu ki chi ẹgwa mẹta).` -> speaker `Ugbó ẹgba kibaa / Àsìkò ì jẹ. / Ùdẹ ẹgba nù mẹ́ta`. "annotator_8 gives three Igala phrases; v4.4 writes the English senses into the Igala lines and uses ẹgwa (ten) for 'times'"
  - ig_bank_cult_024 [annotator_7] (v4.4): model `Ọjanẹ Ígáláà, ẹchẹdọ ọnọnọjọ chi ẹñwu ki ñyọ ñyọ'ñyọ i chẹ. Abọ Ígáláà a di ọjima ñwi ilowa. Ichẹñwu ki ilowa wa ti [house], ma a di [food] kpai amí ñwu.` -> speaker `Chẹñwu ki ọnọnọjọ lọ tu [house] ẹnẹ Igala, ma gbi ọnọnọjọ lẹ kpai [hand] mẹji. Ẹjọdudu ma di [water] kpai ọbi ñwi ọnọnọjọ kpai ẹdẹbọ manyi urẹdọ.`. "annotator_7 opens with the scene (when a stranger comes), v4.4 with 'hospitality is a very good thing in Igala land'; note ilowa (Yoruba-shaped 'stranger') against the speaker's ọnọnọjọ"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Describe-to-a-person prompts with a hard cap: 'in one or two sentences, in the words you would say to a visitor', optionally 'begin with what happens, not with how important it is'. Pair each with the speaker's gloss so the one-sentence gold becomes a register exemplar. Avoid open essays (see uninformative shapes).
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - A visitor asks what the Ocho festival is for. Answer in two Igala sentences at most, as you would say it to her at the gate.
  - In one Igala sentence, tell a child why people pour drink on the ground before an elder drinks.
  - A friend from Lagos asks what pounded yam means at an Igala wedding. Answer in Igala in one or two sentences, the way you would answer across the table.
  - In two Igala sentences, say what your family does when a neighbour's elder dies: what you bring and what you say.
  - In one Igala sentence, tell a stranger which day your town's market holds and why people from other towns come.

### v44-vs-gold 8. Speech-act formulas still wrong where speakers converge: good night, welcome, condolence, farewell, arriving at a house of mourning, replying to an elder's thanks. v4.4 'Wọla [name] for good night against six speakers' Ch'[morning] (gloss 'till morning'); 'Nnọ, [road] lẹ' for a returning traveller against four speakers' 'Wọla ulẹ'; Nnọ (Igbo) three times despite grammar row 6 and annotator_5's judgment 'Nnọ is igbo not igala'. These prompts had 5 to 8 golds each and the highest convergence in the slice, so they are the sharpest separators the arena has.

- Rule a speaker would state: Good night = Ch'[morning] (till morning), never wọla + night. Welcome and welcome back = Wọla ulẹ. The wọla frame also carries ukọlọ (work), [money] (from the farm), ọjilẹ (congratulation), akwuli (condolence). Consolation = [god] ki di añyi ẹdọ wẹ or Ẹ ki kp'ẹdọ bi'ẹn. Farewell = Ch'[god] omunẹ / chẹgbatugba. Never Nnọ; sorry = Nago.
- Count: 35 judgments, 6 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Partly: grammar row 10 gives the wọla frame and its licit slot nouns (morning, afternoon, evening, night, homecoming, work) and this is where 'Wọla [name] came from (night is listed as a slot noun, which the speakers contradict for good night); row 6 says do not import Nnọ; row 8 gives Ch'ugba t'ugba. The specific formulas Ch'[morning], Wọla ulẹ for a returning traveller, akwuli, ékìkwúra-n are not served.
- Evidence:
  - ig_bank_auth_004 [annotator_5] (v4.4): model `Wọla [name]` -> speaker `Chọ'[morning] (annotator_7), Ch'[morning] ke-oo (annotator_6), Ch’[morning] (annotator_3), [father] cho dudu (annotator_5), Ch'[morning] (annotator_4), Àgbá ì chodudu [god] kì k'uwa kwane kpọ ọla fiya (annotator_8)`. "annotator_5 on the bare and v3 pair: 'Good night in Igala is Cho dudu'; six annotators converge, v4.4 built good night from the greeting frame"
  - ig_bank_auth_002 [annotator_7] (v4.4): model `Nnọ, [road] lẹ.` -> speaker `Wọla ulẹ (annotator_7, annotator_4), Wọla ulẹ ooo, ulẹ a’ke (annotator_5), W'ọla ulẹ, abẹle kpai eju [road] ulẹ (annotator_6), Ẹ wọlulẹ (annotator_3)`. "annotator_7 on the earlier pair: 'the correct translation is Wọlẹ ule'; annotator_5: 'Welcome in igala is wọla ulẹ'"
  - ig_bank_auth_018 [annotator_5] (v4.4): model `Nnọ, [god] kì chẹ ñwẹ.` -> speaker `[god] ki di añyi ẹdọ wẹ (annotator_7), W'ọla akwuli, [god] ki du añy'ẹdọ ñwu wẹ kpai akwọra [house] wẹ (annotator_6), Ẹ ki kp’ẹdọ bi’ẹn (annotator_3), [god] kì danyedo-we (annotator_8)`. "annotator_5 on the v3 pair: 'Nago not Nno'; the comfort verb is di añyi ẹdọ, not chẹ"
  - ig_v42_reg_015 [annotator_7] (v4.4): model `Awa, ab'[house].` -> speaker `Ma wola ì yahì ukwu ì, [god] kì fu ukwu chì ìmì-nwu`. "annotator_7 gloss: 'Sorry for the death may rest in peace'; v4.4 (a repaired answer) gives a generic 'thank you, people of the house'"
  - ig_v42_reg_013 [annotator_7] (v4.4): model `Awa, [mother].` -> speaker `Àgbá [mother] ékìkwúra-n`. "annotator_7 gloss: 'Thank you ma, don't boarder'; the reply to thanks has a formula v4.4 does not know"
  - ig_v42_reg_010 [annotator_8] (v4.4): model `[mother] mi, u wa kw'[money]. / Awa, [child] mi.` -> speaker `Àbò mẹ wola òrọka / [mother] [child] mì àgbá è wolá [money]`. "annotator_8: the mother answers with wola [money] (well done from the farm), the wọla frame v4.4 never uses for returns from work"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Situated one-liners with a fixed speaker, addressee and moment (parting at night, arriving at a wake, replying to an elder's thanks, greeting someone back from the farm, from a journey, from the stream). These produced the most convergent gold in the slice (auth_002, auth_004, auth_007, gram_026) and decisive judgments (auth_001, 005, 007, 009, 010, 015, 023 at 0.75 or more).
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Your grandmother is going to bed and you are staying up. Give in Igala exactly what you say to her as she goes.
  - Your aunt comes back from the stream with water on her head. Give in Igala what you say to her as she sets it down.
  - Your neighbour arrives home after two weeks in hospital. Give in Igala the first thing you say when you see him at his door.
  - You hand an old man his walking stick and he thanks you warmly. Give in Igala what you say back.
  - A classmate's father has died and you meet her on the road the next morning. Give in Igala the words you say, before anything else.
  - Your friend sneezes loudly at the table. Give in Igala what people say to him, if anything is said.

### v44-vs-gold 9. Dialect attribution the prompt forbids, with the Idah/Ankpa facts reversed, and a copied template. In 5 of 22 dialect rows v4.4 asserts which town says what: 'Abọ Idah a ka kini Wa mọ, muda abọ Ankpa a ka kini Lia mọ' is the reverse of what two speakers report (Idah lia/lẹya, Ankpa and Ogugu wa, Ibaji lẹwa); 'abọ Ibaji a kà ki ni mi a lọ ti [money] and 'Ibaji aka ka kini ókà' are invented; 'Ka bu ẹle' is lifted from annotator_6's gold. The meta sentence 'Uchẹ ki bọ aka kini X abẹki bọ aka kini Y' is annotator_6's own gold template reproduced in six dialect answers. v4.4 itself uses wa for 'come' in six answers and lia once, while the Idah speakers (annotators 6, 7, 8) write lia 21 times. Salem's point that one answer must stay in one variety cannot be judged until the model names its variety.

- Rule a speaker would state: Name the variety the answer is written in when the prompt asks about dialect, and keep its markers consistent (Idah: lia/liya, ñwu; Ankpa and Ogugu: wa, often nwu; Ibaji: lẹwa). Never state what another town says unless a reference says so; 'I write Idah Igala and do not know the Ankpa form' is a correct answer.
- Count: 64 judgments, 0 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Yes: METHOD 8 'Never assert which town or area uses a form unless your reference material says so - saying you do not know is correct.' The model violates it in a quarter of dialect rows, and the served DICTIONARY apparently gives wa = come without a variety label. Salem's one-variety rule is not in the prompt.
- Evidence:
  - ig_bank_dial_015 [annotator_8] (v4.4): model `Abọ Idah a ka kini "Wa mọ", muda abọ Ankpa a ka kini "Lia mọ".` -> speaker `Idah kakì lẹya kẹ jen̄wù, / Ankpa kpaì ogugu kakì wá kẹ jen̄wù / Ibajì kakì lẹwa kẹ jamìn̄wu (annotator_8, ig_bank_dial_007); Abo ogugu a ka ki ni 'La ka jẹñwu' abo Idah a ka ki ni lia ka jẹñwu (annotator_7, ig_bank_dial_007)`. "two speakers independently put lia in Idah and wa in Ankpa/Ogugu; v4.4 says the opposite"
  - ig_bank_dial_007 [annotator_7] (v4.4): model `Uchẹ ka a kà ki ni "u a lọ ti [money]" ama abọ Ibaji a kà ki ni "mi a lọ ti [money]".` -> speaker `Abọ Igala okpakpa a ka ki ni 'liya ẹmi' abo ọgugu a ka ki ni 'wẹ mi' (annotator_7, ig_bank_dial_004)`. "no speaker anywhere reports an Ibaji 'mi' subject pronoun; three judges called the v3 and bare pair both_inadequate on this prompt"
  - ig_bank_dial_005 [annotator_6] (v4.4): model `Abawe, abawe de / Uchẹ ki bọ a ka ki ni "abawe" abẹki bọ a ka ki ni "abawe de"` -> speaker `Uchẹ ki bọ aka kini "wa" abeki bọ aka kini "lia" (annotator_6, ig_bank_dial_004); "Abu wele", or "Agbebo" (annotator_8); abu'wẹ gbọ manyi ayẹ gbọ (annotator_7)`. "v4.4 reuses annotator_6's sentence frame from another prompt and fills it with an unattested 'abawe'; all four judges marked both earlier arms inadequate"
  - ig_bank_auth_014 [annotator_7] (v4.4): model `Am'[child] mi, mẹ wa jẹñwu.` -> speaker `[child] mi lia kẹ jẹñwu (annotator_7), Amọma mi mẹ lia j'[food] mẹ (annotator_6), Mẹ lia jẹñwu (annotator_3), [child] mì, ẹgba [food] dẹ, ma lìa ā jẹnwu (annotator_8)`. "four speakers write lia for come; v4.4's wa is the Ankpa/Ogugu form in an otherwise Idah-shaped answer"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Replace 'note any variation' prompts (13% decisive) with variety-labelled production: 'answer in the Igala of your own town and name the town' or 'answer in Ankpa Igala'. Use the known markers (come, eat, for, and) as the probe word so a mixed answer is detectable. Collect the annotator's town once and let the dialect field do the rest.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Call two children in to eat. Write it in the Igala of your own town, and write the name of your town after it.
  - Say 'come here and see this' in Igala as it is said in Idah, then as it is said in Ankpa. If you only know one, write that one and say which.
  - Write 'he went to the farm with his brother' in Igala. Then say which town's Igala you wrote it in and name one word in it that another town would say differently, if you know one.
  - In the Igala of your own town, tell a friend you will come tomorrow. Name the town.
  - An Ankpa trader and an Idah trader greet each other at Anyigba market. Write the two greetings in Igala, one in each variety, and say which is which. If you cannot write one of them, say so.

### v44-vs-gold 10. Wrong or invented words that speakers already corrected on v3, still present in v4.4, several in repaired rows. ùjẹu for food (7 tokens, 5 rows; speaker edits replaced jẹu with jẹñwu twice), etegwa for seventy, oguegwa for thirty (repaired row; the served grammar itself bans it), ọwọcho for south (deleted by two annotators), ojonwawn for next week (replaced by aladi ki ya wa), bokiti (annotator_5: Bọkẹti), anẹ [tree] for 'under the tree' (annotator_5: ofe [tree]), iticha (akukọ / ẹnẹ ukọche), Ọgẹbẹ for north (Ojukpale/Ukpale), Oye for harmattan (Yoruba; speaker afu ọwẹ), abo otu for age grade (Igbo-shaped; speakers [head]-ẹgọ, Ofìgbẹlì, ami agbadufu), ọ́kà okilì for yam ([yam]), Onola/Onota as buyer/seller (ẹnẹ ki ala / ẹnẹ ki [father]), ẹgwa (ten) for 'times' (ùdẹ ẹgba), ọdun (Yoruba) for festival, ilowa (Yoruba-shaped) for stranger.

- Rule a speaker would state: Build the lexical blocklist from the edits (tokens removed in three or more edits and never inserted) and the allowlist from inserted tokens; serve both. Specific pairs: food [food]; seventy ẹtẹẹgwa; thirty ogwu ẹgwa (two words); south opata/apata; north ojukpale/ukpale; next week aladi ki ya wa; bucket bọkẹti; under the tree ofe [tree]; teacher akukọ or ẹnẹ ukọchẹ; harmattan afu ọwẹ; yam [yam]; stranger ọnọnọjọ; festival ichọlọ; times ùdẹ ẹgba.
- Count: 40 judgments, 31 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Partly: row 14 bans oguegwa and gives ẹtẹẹgwa; row 15 lists opata for south; row 30 says teacher iticha was edited to akukọ; row 7 gives aladi ki ya wa. None of ujẹu, ọwọcho, ojonwawn, Oye, otu, ọ́kà okilì, bokiti, anẹ [tree] is on the NEVER list, and the repair round let oguegwa and ọwọcho through.
- Evidence:
  - ig_bank_gram_018 [annotator_6] (v4.4): model `I du ùjẹu lẹ ñw'[mother] wn.` -> speaker `I d'[food] lẹ ñwu [mother] wñ (annotator_6), Ìmì [food] dù ñwì [mother] ñwù (annotator_3), i mu [food] lẹ duwi [mother] ñwu (annotator_5)`. "annotator_7's edit on v3 (ig_bank_auth_003): 'é-jẹu í gbede kẹ nẹ ukpahiu' corrected to 'Jẹñwu ñyo'ñyo ki ni ukpahiu'; annotator_5 (ig_bank_auth_014) corrected 'wa jẹu' to 'wa jẹñwu'; v4.4 still writes ùjẹu in auth_022, cult_002, cult_011, gram_022, gram_038"
  - ig_v42_gram_007 [annotator_8] (v4.4): model `Ọ̀là Wikipedia ka kà kakini [goat] ka dẹ dab kilomita oguegwa [hand] ọ̀wọ̀cho kpai ólòdùdu Idah.` -> speaker `Éfú [goat] ka dọ̀mọ̀ t'[hand] ónwú kpaí ọ̀lọ̀ Ídah, í újìjì-n chì ñwu ìmẹlì ọgwu egwa`. "repaired row; annotator_5 had corrected v3's 'Oguegwa' to 'Ogwuẹgwa' (ig_v42_orth_011) and judged 'Ogwu ẹgwa not oguegwa'; grammar row 14 says 'written as two words, never oguegwa'; 'dab' is a truncated dabi and ọ̀wọ̀cho was deleted by annotators 5 and 7 on ig_v42_lex_016"
  - ig_v42_gram_018 [annotator_5] (v4.4): model `Íyeyemi mi, ọdọ ñwu chì etegwa.` -> speaker `[mother]-ye mì chì ọdọ ọ́gbọmẹta nyọ mẹgwā (annotator_8); Íyeyemi chí ọdọ’mẹ ẹ̀tẹẹ̀gwa (annotator_5 edit)`. "annotator_5 judging the v3 pair: '70 in igala is ẹ́tẹẹ̀gwa'; v4.4 keeps v3's misspelling"
  - ig_v42_lex_009 [annotator_7] (v4.4): model `àmì dọkita á wa t'[goat] aladi ojonwawn ku ma du ogwu ñw'amọma lẹ.` -> speaker `Abo [house] ógwu á wa efu [goat] kẽkẽ,má wà ańubi aladí kí dé ì (annotator_8); annotator_7 edit: 'uka ojonwawn' replaced by 'Aladi ki ya wa i'`. "ojonwawn was removed in three edits and never inserted; v4.4 writes it in lex_009 and auth_008 while writing the correct 'aladi ki ya wa' in auth_003"
  - ig_v42_auth_012 [annotator_7] (v4.4): model `Oye chi afufu ofu ki ya wa efu ukpe ọdọ.` -> speaker `Ekọ afu ọwẽ chi ochu ẹkẽgwaka i cha chanẽ`. "annotator_7 gloss 'Harmattan season usually starts in the month of November'; Oye is the Yoruba word for harmattan and the judge tagged both earlier arms wrong_language and cultural"
  - ig_bank_cult_038 [annotator_6] (v4.4): model `Akwọra [house] kpai abo otu a ch'úkọ́lọ́ ọgbọgaga ọjanẹ Ígáláà.` -> speaker `Ojanẹ Igala ma du abo [head]-ẹgọ ch'ukọlọ efewo (annotator_6); Ofìgbẹlì chì abo kì chẹ ewewe (annotator_8); am’okorobia kpai ama gbadufu (annotator_3)`. "annotator_7's edit on v3's 'kpai ẹgbẹ ami agbadufu': '(kpai ẹgbẹ is not supposed to be there)'; v4.4 replaced the Yoruba ẹgbẹ with an Igbo-shaped otu"
  - ig_v42_gram_035 [annotator_5] (v4.4): model `Mẹ kó [yam] lẹ jọ anẹ [tree] lẹ.` -> speaker `Mẹ k’[yam] lẹ ji ofe [tree] (annotator_3); Mẹ kó [yam] lẹ jọ ofe [tree] (annotator_5 edit)`. "annotator_5: 'Under the tree in igala is ofe [tree] not t’ane [tree]; v4.4 learned kó ... jọ (concord) but not ofe"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Situated sentences that must contain the target word (seventy goats, thirty kilometres, going south, next week, a bucket, under the tree, the teacher, north on a map, harmattan, age grade, yam, three times). A one-sentence scene with one lexical target gives a decisive judgment and a one-word correction; these prompts scored 0.75 or more decisive on lex_003, lex_004, lex_008, lex_014.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Your uncle is seventy and your aunt is thirty. In one Igala sentence, tell a visitor both ages.
  - The yams are under the big tree behind the house. Tell your sister in one Igala sentence where to find them.
  - The vaccination team comes next week; the market is in three days. Say both to your neighbour in Igala, two short sentences.
  - Your daughter needs a bucket and a torch for school. Ask the shopkeeper for them in Igala as you would at the counter.
  - A child asks why the air is dusty and cold this morning. Answer in one Igala sentence, naming the season.
  - Your cousin is the new teacher at the primary school. In one Igala sentence, introduce her to an elder.
  - You cooked the soup three times and it still burned. Tell your friend in Igala, in one sentence.
  - Describe in one Igala sentence who the age-grade of young men in your town is and what they do at a funeral.

### v44-vs-gold 11. Loanword policy opposite to the speakers'. Following METHOD 9 and 10, v4.4 keeps school subjects and technical nouns in English letters (mathematics, social studies, agricultural science, biology, economics, psychology, password, camera, computer science, secondary, primary, Kogi State) and calques others ([house] ogwu for hospital, ẹnẹ [money] for farmer). Speakers do the reverse: they respell everyday loans into Igala letters (achibiti/ácìbìtì/asibiti hospital, chegbulu/chekpulu school, bọkẹti, tọchi, fónu, chaji, komputa, dọkita, iticha, servici, votíní, dẹraìva, státì/Steeti), paraphrase academic subjects (ukọchẹ + what it studies) or respell them (éconómìkì, ẹkẹnọmiks, Imatimatiki, ìgìlìshì), and replace camera with foto and password with igede (padlock). Names are the one place both sides agree to keep the letters, and that part is solved.

- Rule a speaker would state: Common loans take the community spelling (achibiti, chegbulu, dọkita, bọkẹti, tọchi, fónu, chaji, komputa, státì); school subjects are described (ukọchẹ ọ̀da ...) or respelled without s; person, place and institution names keep their letters. Do not calque hospital or farmer when the loan or the native word exists (achibiti; álucha, ẹluchẹ).
- Count: 32 judgments, 9 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: The opposite is told: METHOD 9 'a name is not an Igala word - never respell one' is right for names and wrongly extended by the model to common nouns and subjects; METHOD 10 'keep the English word in your sentence - an omission is worse than a borrowing' pushes English spelling over the community's respelled loans. Grammar row 30 gives dọkita and enẹfu, which the model does use.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_gram_023 [annotator_8] (v4.4): model `Ma kọ [house] ogwu lẹ efu ọdọ 1965.` -> speaker `Ma chẹ kọ [house] ácìbìtì lẹ efu ọdọ ẹgba nẹbi mafẹ m’ẹlu`. "annotator_8 writes the hospital loan; annotator_5 on ig_v42_lex_012: 'Hospital in igala is achíbítí'; annotators 6 and 7 wrote asibiti in edits"
  - ig_v42_lex_004 [annotator_8] (v4.4): model `Ónokẹlẹ lẹ kọchẹ economics ukọchẹ efu university.` -> speaker `ukọ̀chẹ́ ọ̀da [money] kpaí [money] (annotator_8); I ch'ukọchẹ ẹkẹnọmiks (annotator_5 edit); Onekele lẹ che ùkochà éconómìkì kwì [house] ukocha Unìvacìtì (annotator_8, ig_v42_lex_027)`. "repaired row; the speakers paraphrase (study of the law of wealth, farming and money) or respell with Igala letters; none keeps 'economics'"
  - ig_v42_lex_003 [annotator_8] (v4.4): model `Íyeyemi, na tẹnẹ ku kà ñw'ẹ ka ki ni [child] ọmaye mi ọnẹkẹlẹ á kọchẹ biology ef'university.` -> speaker `Ọmọ ányá rẹ́ d'ọ̀fẹ̀ yunifásítì kí kọ́ ọ̀dá àmẹ̀nwú k'ọ́lá dẹ`. "annotator_8 gloss 'Studies of living things'; grandmother gets a paraphrase, not 'biology'"
  - ig_v42_auth_007 [annotator_3] (v4.4): model `Mẹ dago chaka kẹ dẹju ti camera.` -> speaker `Mẹ wa da’go dama to du ku mẹ di foto (annotator_3); Akwọra [house] mẹ chakadu mẹ gwanẹ tọdu ku mẹ di fọtọ (annotator_7)`. "both speakers say 'take a photo' with the loan foto; two judges called both earlier arms inadequate with wrong_language"
  - ig_v42_lex_008 [annotator_3] (v4.4): model `[mother] mi, na kà ñwẹ, wẹ kì du password wẹ ñw'ọnẹ du ñ.` -> speaker `Anti ẹ ki d’igede kuma dubi nwu ẹnẹdu’n (annotator_3); Ẹ kì du igede wẹ ñw'ẹnẹ du-n (annotator_6 edit); Yaya mi Ẹ kì du paswọdú wẹ (annotator_5 edit)`. "two speakers use igede (padlock) for password, one respells paswọdú; none keeps the English letters"
  - ig_v42_gram_037 [annotator_8] (v4.4): model `[father] mi chi ẹnẹ [money] i che. I de ef'[money] abajọyí.` -> speaker `[father]-mì chì álucha. / Ájọ́yì ì dì [money] álucha (annotator_8); [father] mi chi éluchẹ i che yí (annotator_5 edit)`. "annotator_5: 'Farmer in igala is Éluchẹ not Akọkọ'; v4.4 calques 'person of the farm'"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Everyday loan nouns inside spoken sentences (hospital, bucket, charger, generator, phone credit, driver, bank, school subjects) with the instruction 'write the word the way you would text it to a friend'. Pair one prompt that needs a proper name (solved) with one that needs a common loan so the two policies can be told apart in judgment.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Your mother is at the hospital in Idah with your aunt. Tell your brother in one Igala sentence, as you would text it.
  - Your son wants to study law at university. Tell his grandfather in Igala what he will study, in the words you would use to him.
  - Ask a driver in Igala to stop at the bank and then at the secondary school, as you would say it in the car.
  - Tell your daughter in Igala to buy phone credit and a torch battery on her way home.
  - Your niece passed chemistry and failed mathematics. Tell her mother in Igala in one or two sentences, as you would at the door.
  - Write for an Igala Wikipedia article one sentence saying that the hospital in Anyigba was built by the state government in 1998.

### v44-vs-gold 12. Second-person wẹ used as subject. v4.4 puts wẹ (object or possessive 'you/your') before the verb in 13 rows ('ki wẹ kpẹ', 'Wẹ chẹ gbọ', 'wẹ kì du password wẹ', 'kì wẹ a kọ', 'Wẹ kì ch'abalẹ ge ñ'); speakers write ẹ as subject (ẹ as subject 6 in gold vs wẹ as subject 4, all annotator_7) and corrected v3's 'Wẹ kpa [hand] wẹ' to 'Ẹ gw'[hand] wẹ'. v4.4 also answers a one-phrase question with a full pronoun ([water] chi ene ekegweji') where the speaker gives the bare phrase.

- Rule a speaker would state: You as subject is ẹ before the verb; wẹ follows a verb or noun (object or possessor). The full pronoun only for emphasis, pairing or a one-word answer, as grammar row 20 says.
- Count: 6 judgments, 10 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Yes: the pronoun table gives you = ẹ (subject), ẹ (object), wẹ (possessive), and row 20 says short clitics by default. The model disobeys in second-person questions and prohibitions.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_gram_028 [annotator_5] (v4.4): model `Wẹ chẹ gbọ ọla ku ka lẹ?` -> speaker `Ẹ wú kú ka yí che nẹrejú?`. "annotator_5: 'To understand in igala means enẹrejú'; subject ẹ, and v4.4's gbọ means hear"
  - ig_v42_gram_030 [annotator_5] (v4.4): model `Ẹ gwẹ̀ [hand] wẹ k'ẹ kì jẹñwu? / Ẹñ, u gwẹ̀ ma. / Ẹñ-ẹñ, u gwẹ̀ ñ.` -> speaker `[mother]: Ẹ gw’[hand] wẹ taki ẹ jẹñwu yí? / [child]: Eeh, u gw’[hand] mi. / [child]: Ẹn̄-ẹn̄, u gw’[hand] mi n̄.`. "annotator_5's edit of bare keeps ẹ as subject and 'taki' for before; v4.4 gets ẹ here but writes k'ẹ kì (prohibitive shape) for 'before you'"
  - ig_bank_auth_027 [annotator_7] (v4.4): model `[child] mi, ubo wẹ lo ki wẹ kpẹ t'ẹgba yi? Wẹ kì ch'abalẹ ge ñ.` -> speaker `[child] mi tọdu ẹñwu kẹ ma wa gwẹ ñyi ni'ñ (annotator_7); Ẹñwu chi kẹ kpọpẹ ta? (annotator_6); [child] yẹnẹ wá eñwu dé tubì, e má kì iko alo nee? (annotator_8)`. "three speakers use kẹ (ki + ẹ) or e for 'you' as subject; also ubo for where was corrected to ugbo in grammar row 30 and v4.4 still writes ubo"
  - ig_v42_gram_022 [annotator_8] (v4.4): model `[water] chi ene ekegweji.` -> speaker `Ẹnẹ ẹkegwèjì`. "annotator_8 answers the nurse with the bare ordinal phrase; v4.4 fronts the full pronoun [water]"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Second-person scenes: a question to one person, the same as a command and as a prohibition, in one prompt (three lines). Speakers converge on the ẹ/kẹ subject; wẹ before a verb becomes a countable error.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Your friend has a cup in her hand. Write three Igala lines: ask her whether she has drunk the water, tell her to drink it, and tell her not to drink it.
  - Ask your brother in Igala whether he locked the door, then tell him to go and lock it.
  - In one Igala line, ask a child whether she understood what you said, using the word for understand rather than hear.
  - Tell a visitor in Igala: 'You are welcome; you will sleep in this room; you should not go out at night.'

### v44-vs-gold 13. Money and large numbers as spoken. Speakers count money in named units (akpulu = 200 naira, pam'ẹlu = 5, pam'ogwu = 40, kọbọ, jẹkà, dẹlì) and compose large sums from them; v4.4 produces the abstract vigesimal string or claims people use English. Spoken years are open: v4.4 refuses to spell 1965 (digits) following grammar row 7, while annotator_8 composes a year-name. Seventy and thirty misspelled (see wrong words).

- Rule a speaker would state: In a market scene, prices are given in akpulu and pam' units (akpulu mẹbie ny'oje for 1,500), not in the counting-system string; counting objects uses the vigesimal series (ogwu ñyọ ka ...). Spoken year-names are an open question to elicit, not to ban.
- Count: 8 judgments, 4 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Grammar rows 14 and 27 give the vigesimal series and 'money in the market is counted in this system'; the named money units (akpulu, pam', jẹkà, dẹlì) are absent, and row 7 bans composing a year-name, which one speaker does.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_gram_019 [annotator_5] (v4.4): model `Ì chì Naira icham nyogwoko nyoke ọgbọ mẹ́lu ugbo mẹ́lu` -> speaker `[market] ñwu chí naira Akpulu mébie ny’oje`. "annotator_5 gloss 'The price is one thousand five hundred naira'; annotator_7's money list on ig_bank_dial_011: Pam'ẹlu = 5, Pam'ogwu = ₦40, Akpulu ká / Ọgwọ́kọ́ = ₦200, Akpulu mélu = ₦1,000"
  - ig_v42_lex_028 [annotator_7] (v4.4): model `Ma á lo nọmba Ẹnẹfu, ma á kà 'One thousand naira'.` -> speaker `Nara íchámû nyú ọ̀gwáọ́kọ́`. "the prompt allowed 'say plainly if people use the English number'; the speaker gives the Igala amount and the judge marked both earlier arms inadequate"
  - ig_v42_gram_023 [annotator_8] (v4.4): model `Ma kọ [house] ogwu lẹ efu ọdọ 1965.` -> speaker `Ma chẹ kọ [house] ácìbìtì lẹ efu ọdọ ẹgba nẹbi mafẹ m’ẹlu`. "prompt said 'saying the year out loud'; v4.4 wrote digits, annotator_8 spelled the year; on ig_v42_gram_009 both wrote 'Ọdọ 1999'"
  - ig_bank_dial_011 [annotator_8] (v4.4): model `Ogbo meji (forty), oje (fifty) kpai ogumelu (one hundred). U ma uchẹ ki ya kọ [money] [hand] omunẹ ñ.` -> speaker `1:[money] [king] dé / 2:gbalukà [money] leẹ́ / 3: kọbọ káà / 4: kọbọ megwa / 5: jeká kà / 6: jẹkà me'lu / 6: dẹlì káà (annotator_8); Ojanẹ igala, [road] ka ku ma kaluka [money] efu a ja che "ijẹka" ma du gbaluka (annotator_6)`. "two speakers describe market counting in ijẹka/jẹkà and dẹlì units; v4.4 recites the number series"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Market scenes with concrete prices (₦50, ₦200, ₦500, ₦1,500, ₦4,000) 'said aloud to the buyer', plus a counting scene (goats, yams) to keep the two systems apart; and a spoken-year prompt per annotator to settle whether year-names exist.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - A buyer asks the price of your basin of garri: it is two hundred naira. Say the price aloud in Igala as you would at the stall.
  - The goat costs four thousand naira and the hen five hundred. Say both prices to the buyer in Igala.
  - Count aloud in Igala the eleven yams you are putting in the sack, one after another.
  - Your grandfather says he was born in 1948. Write in Igala how he says the year aloud, or write it in digits if that is what people do, and say which you did.
  - A child asks how much a bottle of groundnut oil is: fifty naira. Answer in Igala as you would to the child.

### v44-vs-gold 14. The served NEVER list bans forms the community writes, and the model then avoids or mangles them. '-wñ' endings banned, yet the 3sg possessive is written wñ/wn by speakers ([mother] wñ, ọlawn, ugbo-wn) and the served pronoun table itself lists poss = -wn; v4.4 produced 'chẹwñ' and copied 'b'ẹwñ'. 'abẹki' is on the nobody's-words list, but abeki/abekì is the community's word for 'or' in nine gold answers. 'ati' is banned as Yoruba 'and' but speakers use it 14 times meaning 'until/up to' (ọchu ẹjọdudu ati ọchu ẹkẹta). 'Agó o' is banned yet annotator_8 answers a roadside greeting with 'Awa, ágóò'. 'iyawo' is banned and annotator_8 writes it three times.

- Rule a speaker would state: Move -wn/-wñ (3sg possessive), abeki 'or', ati 'until', iyawo and agóò from NEVER to 'verify with speakers'; keep the ban on -wñ only for the invented forms (chẹwñ). Elicit each in a sentence so the ban can be tested rather than assumed.
- Count: 0 judgments, 0 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Yes, these are prompt-level instructions (REGISTER 'never end a word in -wñ'; NEVER WRITE lists ati, Agó o, iyawo, abẹki) that the speakers' own writing contradicts. The pronoun table and the REGISTER line contradict each other on -wn.
- Evidence:
  - ig_bank_gram_018 [annotator_6] (v4.4): model `I du ùjẹu lẹ ñw'[mother] wn.` -> speaker `I d'[food] lẹ ñwu [mother] wñ`. "annotator_6 writes the possessive wñ that REGISTER forbids ('never end a word in -wñ'); annotator_8 writes ọlawn (ig_bank_gram_029) and ugbo-wn (ig_bank_gram_030)"
  - ig_bank_auth_023 [annotator_7] (v4.4): model `Ugwa [child] etito. [god] kì chẹwñ [child] lẹ.` -> speaker `Wọla ọjilẹ [child] ẹtitọ kẹ bi (annotator_7), W'ọla ojile [child] titọ (annotator_6), Wọla [child] etọ (annotator_3)`. "chẹwñ is nobody's word; the congratulation formula is wọla ọjilẹ, which three speakers give"
  - ig_v42_lex_001 [annotator_8] (v4.4): model `ufedenyo / Grace` -> speaker `ufẹdọ-ẹnyọ abekì eju`. "annotator_8 gives two words joined by abekì 'or'; the same word appears in ig_bank_dial_002 [water] abekì [water] oho', ig_v42_lex_018 'abekì [hand] awọhì' and annotator_6's ig_bank_cult_037; v4.4 uses it only inside the copied dialect template"
  - ig_bank_cult_016 [annotator_7] (v4.4): model `Abọ Ígáláà a lè bọ ẹkọ uchẹ [money] kw'ẹgba ẹñwu ẹgbe t'ẹgba uchẹ ẹja.` -> speaker `Ọchu ẹjọdudu ati ọchu ẹkẹta chi ẹkọ ku ma ñwa'nẹ [money] chẹ. Ọchu ẹkẹlẹ ati ọchu ẹkẹfa chi ekọ kọ'mi achanẹ lọ`. "annotator_7 (and annotator_8 who filed the same text) use ati for 'from month one to month three'; the NEVER list calls ati Yoruba"
  - ig_v42_reg_011 [annotator_8] (v4.4): model `Agba, [road] ki ñyọ.` -> speaker `Awa,ágóò`. "annotator_8 gloss 'Thank you'; the NEVER list bans 'Agó o' as Yoruba; annotator_5 on ig_v42_reg_015 said 'Agba not Agoo', so speakers disagree"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Sentences that require 'or', 'until', 'his/her' possessive after a noun, and a reply to a roadside greeting, each from several annotators, so the forms are attested by count rather than banned by inference.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Ask a trader in Igala whether the fish is fresh or smoked.
  - In one Igala sentence, tell a friend that the rains last from April until September.
  - Say in Igala: 'His mother gave her son the money' (two different people).
  - A man weeding his farm calls out a greeting to you on the path. Give in Igala what you call back without stopping.
  - Tell the children in Igala to wait until their father comes before they eat.

**Shapes that produced decisive, explained judgments**

- Situated one-line speech act: fixed speaker, fixed addressee, fixed moment (good night to a grandmother, arriving at a wake, replying to an elder's thanks, greeting someone back from the farm or stream). One line expected.: These produced the most convergent gold in the slice (auth_002: four annotators 'Wọla ulẹ'; auth_004: six annotators Ch'[morning]; gram_026: three 'Ẹ ki ...') and the most decisive judgments (auth_001, 005, 007, 009, 010, 015, 023 at 0.75 to 1.0 decisive), and v4.4 still misses several (auth_002, auth_004, auth_018, reg_013, reg_015). Each miss is a formula a speaker can write in two words. (ig_bank_auth_004, ig_bank_auth_002, ig_bank_auth_007, ig_v42_reg_013, ig_v42_reg_015, ig_bank_auth_001)
- Translate one short English sentence with a single grammatical target (negation, relative clause, yes/no question, two objects, plural concord), optionally written twice (as typed, then expanded or fully marked).: The translate shape had 0.61 decisive share, 7 to 9 golds per prompt, and speakers converge on the words while differing only on the orthographic variable under study (nasal spelling on gram_019, a-fusion on gram_017 and gram_023, lẹ closure on gram_022 and gram_030). v4.4's divergences here are exactly the orthographic rules Salem named (expanded forms, tone, a-fusion), so every judgment teaches a spelling rule. (ig_bank_gram_019, ig_bank_gram_022, ig_bank_gram_026, ig_bank_gram_028, ig_bank_gram_030, ig_bank_gram_017)
- Minimal pair in one prompt: two sentences differing in one feature (COMP kí vs REL kì; 'own up that' as demand vs fact; du vs kó; tẹ vs jọ; ẹ vs wẹ), with a request for a one-line gloss of the difference.: Not yet in the bank (bank-index has one 'minimal pair' prompt, orth_029). Salem's complementiser claim cannot be tested by translation alone because annotator_8 writes kí on relatives (gram_036); only a speaker-produced pair plus gloss separates the two. Also the only shape that makes the kì/kí tone meaningful for annotators who otherwise do not mark. (ig_v42_gram_036, ig_bank_gram_022, ig_v42_auth_006, ig_bank_gram_032)
- Register ladder: the same fact told to five addressees (radio, council of elders, mother, age-mate, small child), each as its own prompt.: Separates the over-applied 'na tẹnẹ ... ka ki ni' frame (12 v4.4 uses vs 2 gold, both on-air) and feeds grammar row 28 (vocative plus honorific mẹ). Speakers answered the two radio prompts with the frame and every intimate prompt without it, so the pattern is learnable and judgeable. (ig_v42_auth_003, ig_v42_lex_002, ig_v42_gram_015, ig_bank_dial_010, ig_v42_lex_012)
- Everyday loan noun inside a spoken sentence (hospital, bucket, torch, phone, charger, generator, driver, bank, school subject), with 'write the word the way you would text it'.: lex_003, lex_004, lex_008, lex_014 were 0.75 decisive; speakers converge on respelled loans (achibiti, bọkẽti, tọchi, fónu, chaji, komputa) or paraphrases, v4.4 keeps English letters or calques ([house] ogwu). The gap is policy-level (METHOD 9 and 10) and one prompt per noun measures it cleanly. (ig_v42_lex_014, ig_v42_lex_004, ig_v42_lex_008, ig_v42_gram_023, ig_v42_auth_007)
- Variety-labelled production: 'in the Igala of your own town, and name the town' or 'in Ankpa Igala', using a known marker word (come, eat, for, and) in the sentence.: Replaces the 'note any variation' shape (0.13 decisive). The dialect field and two speakers' reports give Idah lia vs Ankpa/Ogugu wa vs Ibaji lẹwa as checkable markers; a labelled answer lets Salem's one-variety rule be judged and lets v4.4's reversed attributions (dial_015) be counted as errors. (ig_bank_dial_007, ig_bank_dial_004, ig_bank_auth_014, ig_bank_dial_015)
- Market price said aloud to a buyer (₦50 to ₦4,000) and object counting in the same bank.: Speakers converge on named money units (akpulu, pam', jẹkà) and on the counting series; v4.4 knows only the series. gram_019 and lex_028 were judged both_inadequate for earlier arms and v4.4 still gets them wrong, so judgments will be decisive. (ig_v42_gram_019, ig_v42_lex_028, ig_bank_dial_011, ig_v42_gram_021)

**Shapes that produced ties and "both inadequate"**

- 'Give the Igala word for X and note any difference between areas' (dialectal_fidelity meta prompts).: 0.13 decisive share (4 of 30 judgments decisive, 20 both_inadequate); speakers do not know other towns' usage, invent or copy, and v4.4 reproduces annotator_6's template sentence with the facts reversed. 14 of these rows also have no gold at all (dial_003, dial_015, dial_018, dial_019). (ig_bank_dial_001, ig_bank_dial_004, ig_bank_dial_005, ig_bank_dial_006, ig_bank_dial_008, ig_bank_dial_014)
- 'Write how an Igala speaker would naturally ...' with no scene (no named addressee, moment or place).: 0.34 decisive share, 53 of 88 judgments both_inadequate; gold diverges wildly (auth_016 teasing: five unrelated answers) because the speakers imagine different scenes. The same speech acts become convergent once the scene is fixed (auth_004 vs auth_016). (ig_bank_auth_016, ig_bank_auth_020, ig_bank_auth_024, ig_bank_auth_026, ig_bank_auth_029, ig_bank_auth_034)
- Open cultural essay ('Describe/Explain the role of X in Igala culture').: Gold runs 11 to 198 words and speakers disagree on content (cult_002 yam: 'no special importance'); v4.4 answers with the same template every time, so a judgment costs minutes and teaches one register fact already known. Only 19 judgments were made on this bucket. Keep one or two capped at two sentences (see informative shapes) and drop the rest. (ig_bank_cult_013, ig_bank_cult_014, ig_bank_cult_032, ig_bank_cult_030, ig_bank_cult_036)
- Proper-name orthography (towns, states, people, institutions, acronyms, book titles).: Solved for v4.4: 'Idah kpai Ankpa', 'Bayelsa kpai Osun', 'Banki Access', 'Zainab, Victor kpai Quadri', 'NYSC' are identical to gold from annotators 3, 4, 5, 6; only annotator_8 respells (Záínab, Víkítò, Kúádírì, Ída, Ánkpà, lágóshì). Judgments will be ties, and on bare vs v3 these prompts already scored tie or 'nothing is wrong'. (ig_v42_orth_002, ig_v42_orth_003, ig_v42_orth_004, ig_v42_orth_006, ig_v42_orth_008, ig_v42_gram_003)
- Instruction-constrained Wikipedia sentences ('one sentence stating three things', 'third person with no I', 'no praise words', 'section headings only').: v4.4 follows the constraint better than the speakers (gram_016: annotator_7 wrote first person 'Mà gbẹ̀ [yam]; gram_017: annotator_8 wrote three lines; auth_011 headings match), so the comparison measures instruction-following, not Igala. Also solved for v4.4: dates (gram_014 matches the row-7 formula), 'lo ti' destinations, 'efẹwọ' before towns (gram_001 'Musa dodo efẹwọ Idah' equals annotator_6), counting 18 to 23 (gram_021 identical to gold minus tone), wh-questions in place. (ig_v42_gram_016, ig_v42_gram_017, ig_v42_auth_011, ig_v42_reg_004, ig_v42_gram_014, ig_v42_gram_001)
- Prompts that ask for an Igala line plus an English explanation of its image, or that permit 'say so plainly if you do not know'.: v4.4 complies with the English part and the speakers ignore it, so the pair is judged on tone marks (idiom_001, idiom_002 judged tie or tone_marks); hedge-permitting prompts (v42_dial_002, v42_lex_015, v42_lex_028) get 'U m'ọla yí ñ' or a digit from the model while speakers answer the question, and the judgment becomes both_inadequate with no rule learned. (ig_v42_idiom_001, ig_v42_idiom_002, ig_v42_dial_002, ig_v42_lex_028, ig_v42_lex_005)

**What the speakers' own writing shows**

- contractions: Apostrophes per word range from 0.04 (annotator_8) to 0.135 (annotator_6); v4.4 sits at 0.074. Speakers contract d' (45), k' (41), ch' (28), [child] (18), t' (13), am' (11) before vowel-initial nouns; they never write ef' or ñw' (0 each in 636 answers; efu 34 and ñwu 107 in full) and never k'a (ki + a is fused to ka, 94 standalone ka). 'lo ti', 'loti' and 'lo t'' all occur (7, 8, 8). The negative nasal is written six ways (attached to the verb 31, standalone ñ 26, hyphen -n 24, apostrophe 'ñ 20, nọ/no 20, standalone n 15), by annotator: 6 and 8 hyphenate, 5 attaches, 7 and 4 use apostrophe or standalone, 4/7/8 also write nọ.
- dialect: Dialect field on the 636 gold answers: general_idah 375, unset 157, other 58, ankpa 45 (all annotator_8), ibaji 1 (annotator_4). Markers reported by speakers themselves: Idah lia/liya/lẹya for 'come' (annotators 6, 7, 8: 21 uses), Ankpa and Ogugu wa, Ibaji lẹwa (annotator_8, ig_bank_dial_007; annotator_7, ig_bank_dial_004 and dial_007). annotator_8's ankpa-tagged answers lean to ñwi, nwu alongside ñwu, kpaì, ẹñ-nì, chì, ati, manyi; idah-tagged to kẹ, liya, nyọ, ukọlọ. Speakers keep one variety within an answer; annotator_8 switches tag between answers. Ogwugwu r/l: speakers write arinyọ/ariyọ, lekwu; no gold shows the r variety.
- ki usage: ki is unmarked for annotators 3, 4, 5, 6, 7 (annotator_7: ki 169, kì 4, kí 3); annotator_8 marks: kì 78, kí 18, ki 70, and uses kí on relatives too (gram_036 [goat] kí dì'). ku before plural ma is universal (190 in gold). kẹ chains verbs (40). 'said that' is kakini / ka ki ni (34); 'because' tọdu (20); 'if' chẹñwu/ichẹñwu (annotators 6, 7). Relative clauses are closed with lẹ/lé only sometimes (15 closures; annotator_7 9, annotator_8 3 as lé/lè); on gram_022 and gram_030 three of four speakers write no closure. Optative and prohibitive 'subject + ki + verb (+ nasal)' is written ki by everyone except annotator_8.
- tone marks: 194 of 501 gold answers in the file carry any mark and 90 carry marks on half or more of the words. Bimodal by annotator: annotator_8 marks 89% of answers (mean share 0.39), annotator_3 32% (0.25), annotator_5 16%, annotator_7 12%, annotator_6 1%, annotator_4 0%. When annotators 3 and 8 mark, they mark most or all words (shares 0.6 to 1.0, e.g. 'Mà ká ñwù ámì ímọtọ ká kíní má mì ìchòlò dù nyọgbà'); no speaker marks only particles. 'Igala' is never written with marks except annotator_8's lower-case ìgáláà inside a fully marked sentence.
- other: Sentence shape: 1.58 sentences per answer (v4.4 2.37), comma-chained clauses joined by ki, ku ma, kẹ, manyi, kpai; median 7 words per sentence but mean 10.6. The incompletive a is written fused to the verb (achi, adi, awa, agwa, aluchẹ: 135 fused vs 30 separate). Thanks: Agba, Awa, Anya, 'A chu ugbẹdẹ [god]; sorry: Nago; yes: Iii/Ẹẹ/Ììí; no: ññ, Idabẹlẹ-n, Oda'a. Amen is never written. English appears only when the prompt asks or as a bracketed gloss by annotator_7. Loans are respelled (achibiti, chegbulu, bọkẹti, tọchi, fónu, chaji, komputa, dọkita, státì) and school subjects paraphrased; names keep their letters for annotators 3 to 7, annotator_8 respells them. Possessive 3sg is written wñ/wn/-wn after a noun ([mother] wñ, ọlawn, ugbo-wn). abeki/abekì = 'or' (9 answers); ati = 'until'. Money is counted in akpulu (200), pam' and jẹkà units.

**Open questions (speakers disagree)**

- Complementiser kí versus relativiser kì: Salem's split predicts kí after 'heard', 'know', 'own up', but annotator_8 (the only consistent tone marker) writes kí on relatives ([goat] kí dì [head]-ane státì kogì', 'yunifásítì kí kọ́'). Do speakers hear two words, and does the assertion-vs-fact difference in (10a)/(10b) of the write-up survive a minimal-pair elicitation with glosses?
- Whose tone norm does the arena reward? Four of six active annotators write no marks; annotator_8 marks nearly everything; Salem asks for marks always. A v4.4 answer with marks on every word will beat one with none in annotator_8's judgments and lose in annotator_6's. Decide before building the bank whether 'full marks' prompts are scored by annotator_8 and annotator_3 only.
- Standard spelling of the negative nasal: hyphen (-n, annotators 6 and 8), apostrophe ('ñ, annotators 4, 5, 7), standalone ñ/n, attached Vñ, or the syllable nọ/no (annotators 4, 7, 8 write 'Ma jẹnwu nọ', 'Mà jẹnwu nóò'). Is nọ a different negator or a spelling?
- The 3sg possessive -wn/-wñ ([mother] wñ, ọlawn, ugbo-wn): the pronoun table lists it, REGISTER bans any word ending in -wñ, speakers write it. Which is right, and is 'chẹwñ' (auth_023) a garbled chẹ ñwu?
- NEVER-list words the community uses: abeki/abekì 'or' (9 gold answers), ati 'until' (14), iyawo (annotator_8, 3), ágóò as a roadside reply (annotator_8) against annotator_5's 'Agba not Agoo'. Were these banned on one annotator's judgment?
- Year-names: grammar row 7 says never compose one; annotator_8 spells 1965 as 'ẹgba nẹbi mafẹ m’ẹlu' when asked to say it aloud, while both sides write 'Ọdọ 1999' for a classroom. Is there a spoken year-name system or is this one speaker's improvisation?
- Relative-clause closure lẹ/lé: served as a rule, used by annotator_7 sometimes and annotator_8 as lé/lè, absent for annotators 3, 5, 6 on the same sentences. Variety marker, emphasis, or free?
- Younger brother: grammar row 15 keeps abíne; annotator_5 insists 'Abíne -wrong Agíní - right' (ig_v42_lex_011). Who is right, or are both varieties?
- Lexical conflicts among speakers that the model cannot resolve: play (annotator_5 íya, annotator_8 chìya, v4.4 j'ẹfẹ); understand (annotator_5 nẹrejú vs the model's gbọ); north (Ojukpale/Ukpale vs v4.4 Ọgẹbẹ; row 15 says unresolved); east/west (annotator_5 Apa/Ichi vs annotator_7 [sun] [morning]/[sun] anẹ); teacher (akukọ, ẹnẹ ukọchẹ, iticha accepted in judgment).
- Dialect of wa versus lia for 'come': the served dictionary gives wa without a variety label; speakers place lia in Idah and wa in Ankpa/Ogugu. Does v4.4's consistent wa make every imperative Ankpa-flavoured in an otherwise Idah answer, and does that count as mixing under Salem's rule?
- Retrieval versus generalisation: 25 of 249 v4.4 answers are 60%+ copies of a speaker text on the same training prompt, and six dialect answers reuse annotator_6's template sentence. The next bank must be held out from retrieval, and the 43 existing holdout prompts are too few to carry 100 new judgments.
- 14 prompts in the slice have no gold at all (auth_021, v42_cult_001, dial_003, dial_019, orth_030, lex_038, lex_009, lex_016, gram_038, cult_039, dial_015, dial_018, idiom_009, idiom_004): either queue them for gold or drop them from scoring, since v4.4's answers there (e.g. orth_030 numbers six to ten with tones, gram_038 passive 'Abobule lẹ hi ùjẹu lẹ') cannot be judged against speakers.

## The rules Salem and Lydia asked for, and how a prompt elicits each

Data read: Files: scratchpad/writeup-oct8.txt (the write-up, 1,322 words), scratchpad/prompt-v4-4.txt (41 lines), scratchpad/grammar-rows-oct8.json (30 rows), tasks/igala-grammar-deduced.md (R1 to R10.6 with grades), tasks/grammar-evidence-scholarship.md (sections 0 to 1.6), tasks/grammar-evidence-community.md (full), tasks/jwal-ejeba-2023-rule-inventory.md (tally, ship list, contradictions, grade A/B rows not served), tasks/salem-lydia-writeup-2026-09-25-inventory.md (82 rules, 15 contradictions, 17 questions), tasks/prd-salem-writeup-ingest-2026-10-08.md, web/prisma/seed-prompt-bank-v42.ts lines 1-70, Context.md session states (Sep 23, Sep 28, Oct 8). Data: scratchpad/review-export (exported 2026-10-08T11:14Z, frozen gold excluded by query, annotators pseudonymised): 1,685 comparisons (637 between the two pooled Gemini arms; 356 distinct pooled outputs), 213 train edits, 1,446 train gold, 249 v4.4 train answers, 589 prompts (43 frozen); scratchpad/v44-gram.txt and v44-gold-dump.txt (v4.4 vs gold per prompt); scratchpad/dial-comparisons-flat.txt. Seven analysis scripts written to scratchpad/rules-analysis/a1_ki.py to a7_counts.py (standalone-ki counting, tone-mark tagging and tone-only edits, dialect field and area assertions, s-token edits, agreement verb search, apostrophe rates and expand/contract edits, final-nasal edits, register explanations, per-family and per-shape informativeness, per-annotator judging style). No frozen gold was read; all counts are on the train split.

### rules-to-elicit 1. Clause linker ki/kí/kì emitted with no job, or in the wrong form. v4.4 writes a standalone ki-form in 113 of 249 train answers (45%) and two or more in 55 (22%), 4.41 per 100 words against 3.50 in gold; the bare arm has one in 76 of 178 distinct pooled outputs, retrieval 51 of 178, gold 314 of 1,446 answers (22%). The tone split the write-up rests on is invisible on both sides: gold writes ki 461 times, kì 139, kí 22; v4.4 writes ki 166, kì 75, kí 2. Speakers' edits remove the model's ki 23 times and add one 18 times, so the fault is placement, not count: ki before a plain main clause, kí where ku or kẹ or ka belongs, and the relativizer missing where a noun needs one. The community relativizer inventory is wider than the served kì/ku: ka (annotators 3 and 6), ka ki (annotator 7), ku before 2sg wẹ (annotator 5, twice).

- Rule a speaker would state: kí (high) opens a complement clause after a verb of saying, knowing, hearing or wanting and after a noun like news; kì (low) opens a relative clause and closes with lẹ; a ki with no clause after it is an error; the factive reading (kì) and the belief reading (kí) differ in meaning (write-up examples 9a/9b, 10a/10b). Before a plural clitic the linker is ku; the community also uses ka and ka ki as relativizers (open).
- Count: 26 judgments, 41 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Partly. L20: 'Relativizer kì (singular), ku before plural ma/me' and lẹ closes relative clauses; L25: 'kẹ links verbs; kì/ki starts a new clause - never swap them' (which the model reads as a licence); L27 small-word gate names kì and ku. Nothing about kí, nothing about the COMP/REL meaning contrast, nothing about the relativizer being obligatory when a noun is modified, nothing about ka / ka ki. Grammar rows 003f68fc and 5deb2dc1 serve kakini / ka ki ni only when retrieved. Evidence classes: the overt-linker rule is B (scholarship + community for the quotative frame); the kí/kì tone-meaning contrast is C (scholarship only: gold almost never writes kí, the Bible never marks tone). Speakers' answers on the designed prompts add the community leg for the contrast (do they distinguish the factive and the belief reading at all, and with which form), fix the relativizer inventory (kì vs ka vs ka ki vs ku, and what conditions ku before wẹ), and give decisive judgments on 'ki with no job'.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_auth_010 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Ù mọ̀ ọdọ kí ma bi mi n̄. Ẹñwu kí ù mọ̀ káká chí kí ù kpe gba.` -> speaker `Ù mọ̀ ọdọ ku ma bi mi ñ.`. "edit: three kí reduced to one ku before the plural clitic ma; the second sentence deleted whole"
  - ig_v42_reg_014 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `[money] ukọchẹ [child] mi ki wẹ san. Ẹñwu ki wẹ chẹ yi ... [god] ki chẹwun nwẹ. [god] ki dabi fi kọ wẹ. [god] ki k'[hand] t'[child] wẹ mọ.` -> speaker `[money] ukọchẹ [child] mi kú wẹ rá Ẹñwu kú wẹ chẹ yi ... [god] ki chẹwun nwẹ. [god] ki du [money] wẹ dabí wẹ.`. "edit: five ki reduced to two; the relativizers before wẹ rewritten kú, the optative ki kept"
  - ig_v42_gram_027 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Amọ́ma kí wẹ nọ́ t'[house], aláfíya ma wà?` -> speaker `Amọ́ma kẹ tọnọ t'[house], chọ’lafía bá?`. "Amọma kẹ tọnọ not Amọma ki wẹ ma"
  - ig_v42_lex_013 [annotator_3] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Lo t'òde kì tá jẹ́nì.` -> speaker `Lo kẹ nya ti jẹ́nì.`. "edit: kì between two commands replaced by the serial linker kẹ (annotator_5 made the same replacement: Lo t'òda kẹ fú jẹní lẹ tájo)"
  - ig_v42_gram_029 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Éñwú chẹ kí wẹ a ch'ẹyẹ kpaí ma n?` -> speaker `Éñwú chí kú wẹ ma ch'íya kpa ma n?`. "edit: kí before wẹ rewritten kú"
  - ig_v42_gram_036 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Idah chí [goat] èfù Kogi State.` -> speaker `Idah chí [goat] ka ki de èfù Kogi State.`. "edit: the speaker ADDS a relativizer (ka ki) where the model had none: a town that is in Kogi"
  - ig_bank_gram_030 [annotator_6] (gold): model `Onokẹlẹ kì a lo t'[money] ñwu lẹ leku. (v4.4)` -> speaker `Ẹnẹkẹlẹ ka lo t'[money] ñwu lekwu`. "gold for 'The man whose farm we visited has died': annotator_6 ka, annotator_3 kà ([father] kà lò tókó ñwù lèkwù), annotator_8 kì ... lé (ẹnẹkẹlẹ kì l'uché ká nyì ugbo-wn lé), annotators 4, 5, 7 ki: four relativizer shapes on one sentence"
  - ig_bank_auth_003 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Jẹñwu nyọ́nyọ́ kí ọla wẹ nẹ ugbó.` -> speaker `Jẹñwu nyọ́nyọ́ kí ọla wẹ ki gbo`. "Nẹ gbo is wrong / Ki gbo is correct (the optative kì is wanted here, and the model's kí before ọla was kept)"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Every prompt forces BOTH a complement clause (after say / know / hear / want / be glad) AND a relative clause (whose, which, who) in one short task, so one answer shows both linkers; minimal pairs carry the write-up's belief/fact contrast in English only (translate twice: once as what the speaker thinks, once as established fact); several prompts ask for a two-clause sentence that has NO embedding (two events in sequence) to catch ki with no job; two prompts ask the speaker to say which form they write for 'that' after say and for 'who/which' before a noun and whether the two sound the same (tone question without the word tone). Score: decisive pairs plus edits on ki; gold with kí present is the community leg.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Translate into Igala, in one sentence: 'The woman who sells yam said that her son has come back from Lagos.' (one relative clause, one that-clause after say)
  - Translate 'The child is happy that her father came' twice: first as what the child believes (she thinks he came), then as a plain fact everyone knows. If the two Igala sentences differ by one word, say which word.
  - Translate 'Own up that you are wrong' (the speaker is accusing, it may not be true) and 'Own up that you were actually wrong' (it is settled). Write both.
  - Translate 'I heard the news that the market will not hold tomorrow' and 'the trader whom we paid has not come', each in one Igala sentence.
  - In two short Igala sentences, tell a neighbour that the rain started and then the children ran inside. Join the two events the way you would speak, not with a word for 'that' or 'which'.
  - What is the Igala word you put after 'say' or 'know' before the thing said or known? And the word you put after a noun before 'who did something'? Write each in a short sentence of your own and say whether they are pronounced the same.

### rules-to-elicit 2. No zero complementiser, and the community linker after a verb of saying is ka ki ni / kakini, not kí and not a bare clause. v4.4 and the pooled arms sometimes drop the linker after say or use Yoruba-shaped kà kíni; speakers put kakini after 'said', 'told' and even 'known' (gram_031 'Had we known the elder was coming' answered with kakini by annotator_6, ka ki ni by annotator_7).

- Rule a speaker would state: After a verb of saying, telling, knowing or hearing the clause is introduced by ka ki ni (also written kakini, ka kini); it is never dropped and never replaced by a bare kí.
- Count: 6 judgments, 5 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Not in a prompt line. Row 003f68fc ('said that = kakini') and row 5deb2dc1 (na tẹnẹ ku kà ... ka ki ni) are served only when the grammar block retrieves them; L24 says clauses are joined by a new sentence, which pulls the other way. Evidence: B (corpus kakini 7,356 always overt; community split ka ki ni 6 / kakini 4 in the Aug data, now 7 of 7 on gram_032). Missing: scholarship on ka ki ni specifically (the write-up treats kí as the complementiser and never mentions kakini). Speakers' answers add: whether kakini is also the linker after know / hear / be glad (the write-up's verbs) or only after say and tell, and whether a noun complement ('the news that') takes kakini or kí.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_gram_006 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Àmònẹ Ajaokuta ka kakini Ibrahim chẹ àwéhì tì Lokoja mẹ. (winner) vs bare: Am'Ajaokuta kà kíni Ibrahim lọ Lokoja kpa.` -> speaker `kakini`. "Say that in igala is kakini"
  - ig_v42_gram_006 [annotator_6] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Am'Ajaokuta kà kíni Ibrahim lọ Lokoja kpa.` -> speaker `Abo Ajaokuta kakini Ibrahim lo ti Lokoja.`. "edit: kà kíni rewritten kakini, the destination given ti"
  - ig_bank_gram_032 [annotator_7] (gold): model `Ma ka ñwu ami imọtọ kakini ma chẹ du ọdun lẹ t'[god] ọmune. (v4.4)` -> speaker `Ma chẹ ka ñwi ami imọtọ ka ki ni ma mi [god] ichọlọ du tọ’gba`. "seven gold answers on 'The children were told that the festival had been postponed': annotators 6 and 3 kakini / ká kíní, annotators 7, 4, 5 ka ki ni / kaki, annotator_8 kì; none drops the linker"
  - ig_bank_gram_031 [annotator_6] (gold): model `` -> speaker `I chẹñwu ka ma kakini abo ogijo awa, a ma dago`. "gold for 'Had we known the elder was coming': the linker after know is kakini (annotator_7: Chẹ ñwu ka ma ka ki ni ọgijọ a wa a ma dẹdẹ)"
  - ig_v42_lex_009 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `[mother], u kà nw'ẹ, abọ asibiti a w'[goat] ...` -> speaker `[mother] [child], na tẹnẹ ku kà nw'ẹ, ka ki ni abọ asibiti a w'[goat] wa Aladi ki ya wa ...`. "edit: the speaker inserts ka ki ni where the model ran 'I tell you' straight into the clause"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Reported-speech and reported-knowledge sentences with the matrix verb varied (said, told, knew, heard, is happy, the news that), each one sentence, and two prompts that ask the speaker to retell someone's words first as a quote and then as 'she said that ...' so the linker appears in a frame the speaker controls.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Translate 'My mother told me that the goats have been sold' into one Igala sentence.
  - Translate 'We knew that the road to Ankpa was closed' and 'We heard that the road to Ankpa was closed'. Is the word before the clause the same after know and after hear?
  - A friend said to you 'I am coming tomorrow'. Report it to your sister in Igala: first as her exact words, then as 'She said that she is coming tomorrow'.
  - Translate 'The news that the chief has died reached the village this morning' into Igala, in one sentence.
  - Translate 'The elder is glad that the children came home' into one Igala sentence.

### rules-to-elicit 3. Tone marks: the model tones when told not to and is tagged for wrong marks, speakers barely tone and remove the model's marks. v4.4 carries tone marks on 172 of 249 train answers (69%) despite REGISTER 'no tone marks unless the question asks'. In the pooled judgments the tone_marks tag was applied 102 times, and in 112 of the 119 tagged outputs the marks were PRESENT (bare 66, retrieval 46): the tag means wrong marks, not missing marks. Edits remove tone marks in 119 of 213 and add them in 23. Gold is toned in 404 of 1,446 (28%), driven by one writer: annotator_8 304/376, annotator_3 40/207, annotator_5 25/181, annotator_7 23/300, annotator_6 6/273, annotator_4 6/106. On the 70 prompts that ask for tone marks, 84 of 242 gold answers (35%) carry any. The write-up's tone-always rule therefore cannot be scored against gold; the measurable thing is tone COMPETENCE on minimal pairs under an explicit request, and tone on the meaning-bearing monosyllables (kí/kì, jọ/jọ̀, kọ́/kọ̀).

- Rule a speaker would state: Mark tone when the question asks, and always on the few small words where tone is the whole meaning (kí that / kì who, which, may; jọ sit.PL / jọ̀ rejoice; kọ́ build / kọ̀ refuse); otherwise write the community's dotted, untoned form. A tone mark that is wrong is worse than none.
- Count: 102 judgments, 142 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Yes, the opposite direction: REGISTER L30 'no tone marks unless the question asks for them'; L33 'Mark tone as the dictionary and examples do'. The model ignores L30 69% of the time. No line names the meaning-bearing monosyllables. Evidence: the system (three tones, downstep) is C scholarship (rows 3ce5d469, c928abd5 external_sourced); the meaning-bearing status is B (scholarship + the Aug corrections kọ́/kọ̀, [money]/[money] in deduced R8.2); the writing convention is A against the write-up (corpus 0 of 30,907 verses toned, community 28%). Missing: a community leg that is not one annotator, and any measure of whether the model's marks are RIGHT when asked. Speakers' answers add: per-annotator toned minimal pairs under explicit request (stratify by annotator), the community's own kí/kì marks, and decisive judgments on pairs where only the marks differ.
- Evidence:
  - ig_v42_auth_004 [annotator_6] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Sule Ejeh chi ọ́nẹ k'a kọ eli kù ma bí efu [goat] Ankpa. Abajọyí, i gwù efu [goat] Port Harcourt.` -> speaker `Sule Ejeh chi ẹne kia k'eli Igala ku ma bi efewo Ankpa. Abajọyi, i dodo efewo Port Harcourt.`. "Some tonal mark were incorrect (the untoned retrieval answer won; the speaker's edit strips every mark)"
  - ig_bank_auth_012 [annotator_6] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Àgbá [god]` -> speaker `Agba [god]`. "tone-only edit: all marks removed, the dot kept (annotator_3 on the same pair chose the toned Àgbá [god] over the undotted Agba [god] and wrote 'Correct': dots decide, tone does not)"
  - ig_v42_orth_002 [annotator_3] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Ida kpai Añkpa` -> speaker `Ida kpai Ankpa`. "Just a normal n without tone marks"
  - ig_v42_lex_008 [annotator_7] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Ẹ kì du password wẹ ñw'ẹnẹ duu ǹ.` -> speaker `Ẹ kì du password wẹ ñw'ẹnẹ duu ñ.`. "tone-only edit: the model's low-toned negator ǹ rewritten as the community's ñ"
  - ig_v42_lex_010 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `ọ́yà, u á kà ukọla òókáà ñwẹ.` -> speaker `Na tene kọlá káá`. "Ú á ká ukọla óókaa - wrong / Na tene kọlá káá - right (the speaker tones her own correction: tone appears when it carries a contrast)"
  - ig_bank_gram_022 [annotator_3] (gold): model `Ujẹu ki [mother] mi hi lẹ ch'urinyọ. (v4.4)` -> speaker `[food] kì [mother] mì hyì à rìnyọ`. "the only gold that tones the relativizer low (kì); annotators 5 and 6 write ki / k'[mother] untoned"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Two prompt types. (a) Explicit request, short target: 'write X and Y with every tone mark' on pairs the speakers themselves volunteered (build/refuse, farm/money/canoe family, month/moon) and on the write-up's kí/kì and jọ/jọ̀; the model's competence is measured because the request overrides the register line, and the speaker's gold is a toned minimal pair. (b) No request, natural sentence, where a tone-bearing small word decides the meaning (a relative clause vs a that-clause, sitting vs rejoicing), to see whether speakers tone it unprompted. Avoid 'with correct diacritics' on single common nouns (35 orthography prompts already did that, 35% compliance).
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Write the Igala for 'to build' and 'to refuse' with every tone mark on both, then put each in a three-word sentence.
  - Write 'the children are sitting here' and 'the children are rejoicing' in Igala with full tone marks on every word, so a learner can hear the difference.
  - Write two Igala sentences with full tone marks: one meaning 'the man who came yesterday is my uncle', one meaning 'I know that the man came yesterday'. Mark the tone on the linking word in each.
  - Give three pairs of Igala words that are spelled the same without tone marks but mean different things, and mark the tones on all six.
  - Write a two-line Igala exchange (a greeting and its reply) exactly as you would text it to a friend, then rewrite the same two lines with every tone mark for a learner's book.
  - Translate 'The pot is here' and 'The pots are here' into Igala with full tone marks, as a learner should see them.

### rules-to-elicit 4. Expanded vs contracted forms. v4.4 writes an apostrophe contraction in 145 of 249 answers (58%); gold in 510 of 1,446 (35%), by annotator: annotator_6 130/273, annotator_3 76/207, annotator_5 58/181, annotator_7 105/300, annotator_8 113/376, annotator_4 27/106. Edits run both ways: 30 reduce apostrophes, 29 add them, so the speakers do not treat contraction as an error; the write-up's learner form (ki ọla, not k'ọla) has never been asked for, and its ambiguity claim (k' could be ki or ka) has never been tested on real model contractions. Gold writes k'ọla 10 times and ki ọla 5.

- Rule a speaker would state: Elision at a vowel-vowel junction is licensed, not obligatory (soften L21 'drop the FIRST vowel' to 'may drop'); never add or strip a word-initial vowel; when a learner or a reference form is asked for, write every word in full and tone it.
- Count: 0 judgments, 59 corrections
- Already told to v4.4: Yes, as an obligation: L21 'vowel meets vowel across a word break -> drop the FIRST vowel, apostrophe at the joint (w'ọla, k'ọla, aj'ẹñwu). Never add or strip a word-initial vowel'; REGISTER 'apostrophized elision'. k'ọla is the prompt's model example and the write-up's example of what to avoid. Evidence: the process is A (deduced R6.1: corpus fusion near-exceptionless, community 29% to 35%, scholarship names elision); the prescription 'write expanded' is C (write-up only) and conflicts with the data. Missing: a community leg on the learner form (do speakers expand consistently, and do they expand k' to ki or to ka?). Speakers' answers add exactly that: the expansion of real contractions, which also tests the write-up's ambiguity claim.
- Evidence:
  - ig_bank_auth_008 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Gba ọla wẹ nyọnyọ.` -> speaker `Gbọ’la wẹ nyọnyọ.`. "edit CONTRACTS the model's expanded form (annotator_6 on the same prompt wrote D'eju tẹ ọla wẹ, annotator_5's gold De’ju tọla wẹ)"
  - ig_bank_auth_014 [annotator_4] (gemini-3-1-pro): model `Àmọ́ma mi, ẹ wá jẹ [king]!` -> speaker `Am'[child] mi, mẹ wa jẹwñu!`. "edit contracts the plural prefix, strips the tone, and fixes the plural command (mẹ)"
  - ig_v42_lex_006 [annotator_5] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `Ubo k'u á chaj'u?` -> speaker `Nago Ugbo u neke chaji ñwu?`. "edit EXPANDS two model contractions (k'u, chaj'u) into full words; annotator_7 also expanded: Ugbo unẹkẹ a chaj'u?"
  - ig_bank_auth_001 [annotator_6] (gemini-3-1-pro-rag-v3): model `[god] ki chẹnyọ ñwu wẹ` -> speaker `[god] ki d'ẹnyọ ñwu wẹ`. "edit: the right verb written contracted (d'ẹnyọ); annotator_3's explanation on the same prompt: [god] ki Dẹnyọ ñwu wẹ (fused, no apostrophe)"
  - ig_bank_gram_016 [annotator_6] (gold): model `Ichẹñwu [water] a lọ, a lo t'[money] ñ. (v4.4)` -> speaker `Chẹñwu k'[water] lọ, alo t'[money]-n`. "gold: three annotators write the conditional 'if it rains' three ways: k'[water] (6), kì [water] (3), Komi (5): the same junction contracted, expanded with tone, and fused"
- Prompt shape that elicits it: Dual-form prompts: ask for the sentence as spoken AND as a learner should be shown it (full words); expansion prompts that give the speaker a contracted phrase from the write-up (k'ọla) or a served-prompt form (w'ọla, aj'ẹñwu) and ask which full words it stands for; and plain sentences with a known junction (if it rains, come and eat, good morning) to measure the model's contraction rate against each speaker's. Score: the model's expanded form against the speaker's expanded form is the first clean test of whether the model knows the underlying words.
- Example prompts the analyst proposed:
  - Write 'If it rains, we will stay at home' in Igala twice: once the way you would text it to a friend, once with every word written out in full for a learner.
  - The written form k'ọla appears in Igala texts. Which two full words does the k' stand for here, and could it ever stand for a different word? Write the phrase in full.
  - Write the morning greeting to your mother in full words for a learner's first lesson, then write it the way people actually say it.
  - Translate 'Come and eat with us this evening' into Igala, then write the same sentence with no contractions at all.
